It went in two sittings, split by there being no Azure account: the offline half chose **ACR Basic** (on managed identity with `acrPull` rather than price — the measured finding being that at $5.00/month the registry costs *more* than the $4.21 replica it serves), did the cost arithmetic (**the idle rate is a vCPU discount only**; memory bills the same idle or active), named **the index digest as the provenance record**, and implemented the commit-SHA tag rule in `scripts/build-image.mjs`. The deploy half then measured six things rather than inheriting them: **ACR accepts an OCI image index**; **`HOST=0.0.0.0` is mandatory**, because the `127.0.0.1` default binds the container's own loopback and is unreachable by the ingress proxy while looking healthy from inside; the listening-line trap is confirmed in production and is sharper than recorded, since the replica logs **two** lines with loopback first even at `HOST=0.0.0.0`; **`pid` is 1**, so the exec-form `CMD` holds on the platform that actually sends `SIGTERM`; **no platform secret was needed** and the `secrets` array is empty, with the mechanism identified for Epic 2's Alpaca key; and a deliberate `PORT=0` revision crash-looped while `/health` returned **200 on all 19 polls**, with the failing revision holding `trafficWeight: 100` while `Activating` and the old revision at weight 0 still serving — so **traffic weight is not what serves**. The prediction that a configuration failure would be invisible at the log destination is **half wrong in the useful direction**: it writes a bare line rather than a structured record, but Container Apps collects stdout and stderr together, so it is visible. `/health` reports `version: "0.0.0"` deliberately — the tag and digest already answer "what is deployed", and writing a version into `package.json` at build time would dirty the tree the tag rule needs clean. **The account prerequisite that blocked it is recorded as `ACCOUNT-SETUP.md`**, a prerequisite row in Story 1.11's task table rather than a ninth task, because nothing between 1.11.1 and 1.11.3 had owned creating a subscription. **Task 1.11.4 deployed the frontend and closed the two Story 1.5 acceptance criteria that had been open since 2026-08-31** — live at `https://red-smoke-029583a0f.5.azurestaticapps.net`, four routes deep-loading cold, a made-up path rendering `NotFound` in a browser and `/assets/nope.js` a 404. The cache policy is `no-cache` on the document through `globalHeaders` and `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` on `/assets/*` through a route rule, and the ordering trap was confirmed in the direction that matters: a fallback-served path carries the `globalHeaders` value, which a route rule would never have reached. Every build in it is invoked **by name** — `pnpm build` and `pnpm image`, no `tsc`, no `vite build`, no `docker build`, no `--build-arg` — and the platform's generated workflow was declined outright because it builds the site on the deploy side. The step now also reads the **replica**, which carries `ready: false`, a rising `restartCount` and `CrashLoopBackOff` from ~75 s in, taking a failing deploy to **94 s** naming the cause (**36 s** for a container that will not start), with the deadline at 600 s as a backstop; both branches were executed before the text landed. Four failure classes happened against the live environment and **no request ever returned a non-200 HTTP status** through any of them, with `uptimeSeconds` never resetting — the failing revision holding `trafficWeight: 100` while the old one at weight 0 served, which is the **fourth** sighting of that. **A half-deployed merge was produced** — backend at the merge commit, frontend a commit behind, both answering, not broken _because `/health`'s contract did not change in that commit_ — and **re-running a failed deploy is safe but not a no-op**: the same commit rebuilt to a different digest, so a new revision rolled out and **the commit-SHA tag has now pointed at two different digests**. Four things its brief did not anticipate: **the backend's documented rollback does not exist on this app**, because `az containerapp ingress traffic set` belongs to _multiple_ revision mode and this app is deliberately _single_ — `az containerapp update --image <previous digest>` is what works, in **43 s**, against the frontend's **3 min 42 s** revert commit, and the fast half is **silently undone by the next merge**, watched happening; **the image is not bit-reproducible across runs** (60,247,138 B against the recorded 60,247,220 B); **`runningState` returned an empty string** on one poll of a healthy rollout; and **a check that runs from one machine over one link cannot tell its own network from the environment** — a 65-second "outage" was the laptop, proved by a three-host control and by the backend's own Log Analytics records showing 9 requests per 30 s through it against a probe-only idle baseline of 1–4. The frontend's upload is still not atomic and is **worse than recorded**: the ~2-second window holds **two** broken states, and the second — the incoming document served before the incoming asset exists — was not in Task 1.11.4's reading; it opens at the exact second the upload step reports success. Accepted deliberately. It found three recorded claims that had stopped being true. **The artefact paragraph in this file contradicted itself**: it said the JavaScript moved at Task 1.11.5 and then said the three original files were unchanged since Task 1.7.7 — two of the three had moved, and **`index.html`'s hash is the one nobody re-took**, because it changes at an identical 1,101 B when the hashed script filename it carries changes. Proved by rebuilding Task 1.11.4's commit, which reproduces `eab270a4…` exactly, so the figure was right when taken and wrong one task later. **The image-retention arithmetic was wrong by a factor of about 35**: manifests share layers, so 13 manifests occupy **76,112,906 B — 0.71% of the 10 GB included** rather than the 574.5 MiB their `imageSize` values sum to, two pipeline builds share 4 identical layer digests totalling 58,486,244 B, and each additional build costs **~1.75 MB rather than 60 MB** — roughly **6,000 deploy attempts** of headroom, not 170. **Retention is therefore deliberately deferred**, with registry usage passing 5 GB as the trigger; and of the 3 untagged manifests only **one** is a genuine orphan, the other two being the OCI image manifest and attestation beneath the local build's index, which are referenced rather than waste. **And the cost question could not be answered, which is itself the finding**: both billing APIs refuse this subscription with _"doesn't have valid WebDirect/AIRS offer type"_ — reproduced six times — the portal blade rendered nothing, and decisively **the whole environment is under six hours old** (first resource `2026-09-03T05:32:32Z`) against Azure cost data that lags 8–24 hours. So whether continuous probing breaks the Consumption plan's idle-billing condition is **still open, owned by Epic 2 and re-taken by Epic 3**. What did reproduce exactly is the estimate: re-read from the Retail Prices API, the replica is **$4.21** and ACR Basic **$5.00** for **$9.21/month** at the idle rate and **$19.04** at the active rate — and the $20 budget with alerts at 50/80/100% is in place and was re-read, sitting just **above** the active-rate total, so it would not fire on the change that matters most. Everything else held: the three probes, `minReplicas: 1`, an empty `secrets` array, `pid` 1, zero `OPTIONS` records, 16 log records a minute at idle, the cache policy at both ends, five pinned actions across eight uses, and the deployed bundle byte-identical to a local Mac build with the variable set. Story 1.11 is closed and Story 1.12 remains; nothing was added, deleted or re-ordered.** **Story 1.12 is complete (8 tasks). Task 1.12.1 promoted the health contract into `packages/shared` and named the states, and it is the first thing in this repository that makes `apps/backend`'s declared dependency on that package honest** — `HealthResponse` and `HealthStatus` moved out of `apps/backend/src/routes/health.ts` and are imported back, the schema and its `satisfies` guard stayed behind because `JsonSchemaProperty` is deliberately not shared, and the guard was **made to fire across the package boundary** (a temporary field on the interface is `TS1360` in the backend) rather than assumed to survive. `HealthStatus` is still the one-member `"ok"`, now behind a `HEALTH_STATUSES` const so the schema declares `enum: HEALTH_STATUSES` instead of a second literal — the shape `apiErrorSchema` already had with `API_ERROR_CODES`. **The healthy / degraded / unreachable vocabulary is a second type and not a widening of the first**: `BackendStatus`, because "unreachable" is the absence of a response, which no server can report about itself, and "degraded" is a judgement about an answer that did arrive; a test asserts the two unions share no member. **"Degraded" is defined structurally rather than by latency** — `not-ok-status` and `unreadable-body`, both producible — and latency was rejected with a stated reversal trigger, because it needs a second threshold below the request deadline with nothing keeping the two ordered and there is nothing to set it from while `/health` reads `process.uptime()` and returns; the consequence to know is that **a timeout is `unreachable`, not `degraded`**. `isHealthResponse()` ships beside the interface so the definition is executable, and it deliberately accepts an unrecognised `status` member and unknown extra fields, because a newer server is a version skew rather than a broken one. `/health` was verified **against a running server** and is unchanged field for field at 61 bytes, and the deployment risk this task made live was closed by running the image rather than reasoned about: the container answers 200 with the shared package resolving through the pnpm symlink to real files, `dist/index.js` now **2,121 B** against Story 1.11's recorded 1,257. **Task 1.12.2 turned that one bare `fetch` into the frontend's transport, and `apps/frontend/src/api-client.ts` is now the only file in the application that calls `fetch`** — verified by grep rather than asserted, one call site. It owns the base URL, the deadline, the abort signal, the `ApiError` parse and the correlation id, and there is still no React in it: no state, no effect, no component. `api-base-url.ts` stayed a separate file **deliberately**, because folding it in would put a module-load-time `import.meta.env` read inside the module every test of the client imports and would cost `resolveApiBaseUrl` the property it exists for — a pure function of one argument, testable without a build, the same shape as `loadConfig(env)`. `health-probe.ts` was **not** deleted (that is Task 1.12.3's, with the thing that replaces the call) but it no longer calls `fetch`: it is now a console line over `getHealth()`, and its seven-branch `switch` is what proves the client's outcomes are each worth telling apart. **`API_TIMEOUT_MS` is 5 s and it is load-bearing on Task 1.12.1's vocabulary rather than a tuning knob**: `degraded` is defined structurally, so a deadline that expires is `unreachable` because nothing arrived, and moving this number moves which state a slow backend reports as. Two constraints bound it — well above a real round trip to a `minReplicas: 1` container whose `/health` reads `process.uptime()` and returns, and **strictly below Task 1.12.3's poll interval**, or a hung request is still outstanding when the next poll starts. That ordering is a real obligation the compiler cannot hold and **nothing checks it**; 1.12.3 inherits it. `scripts/check-ready.mjs`'s 2 s is not the comparable figure — it dials a loopback pair, this crosses the public internet. **There are seven outcomes and none of them is a throw**: `ok`, `unreadable-body`, `api-error`, `http-error`, `timeout`, `unreachable`, `aborted`. `api-error` and `http-error` are separate because only one of them carries a `requestId` a user could quote; `aborted` is separate because a caller's teardown is not a fact about the backend at all and 1.12.3 must not render one as a state. **Which signal fired is read off the signals rather than off the rejection** — `AbortSignal.timeout()` and the caller's controller are composed with `AbortSignal.any()`, both verified present under jsdom 30, and the two `aborted` flags are facts this module owns where a `DOMException` name is a string comparison against a value from another realm. **There is deliberately no retry**: retry is a property of the poll, and a retry buried in the transport would make the deadline a lie by turning the caller's five seconds silently into fifteen. **`isApiError()` was added to `packages/shared` beside the shape it checks**, for the reason `isHealthResponse()` sits beside `HealthResponse` — Task 1.7.3 shipped the contract with no predicate because nothing read it, and this is the first reader. It differs from the health predicate in one way on purpose: it **does** check `code` against `API_ERROR_CODES`, because a discriminator a caller switches on is not the same kind of thing as a `status` the interface renders, where an unrecognised value is a version skew a client can still display. **The `requestId` decision is recorded beside the type that carries it**: the whole id, never a prefix — it is a UUID v4 with no internal structure, so the first eight characters are not a shorter version of it but a different thing that matches nothing in a log — and only ever as a labelled reference beside a failure the user is already being told about, never as the message. It is the one internal identifier that may go on screen at all, because it names the _request_ and nothing about our hosts, versions, code paths or data; `ErrorFallback` still keeps a boolean rather than the error, and if a reference line ever reaches it that is a **new named prop**, never a widening of `detail`. **The `window` error listener question was re-taken and the answer is still no**, recorded in `report-error.ts` as a condition rather than a story number: the trigger was never "Story 1.12", it was _an endpoint that accepts a client error report_, and this story builds a client that reads `/health` rather than anything that accepts one. One thing this task changed that looks like it should count and does not — every transport failure is now a _result_ rather than a thrown error, so the unhandled rejection a listener would most plausibly have caught here is structurally gone rather than merely unreported, which **weakens** the case. Epic 10's agent stream is the first thing that plausibly brings the endpoint. Task 1.11.5's 15 tests were **re-homed rather than deleted**: the base URL, the correlation id and the never-rejects property moved onto the client with the `fetch` call they were about, and `health-probe.test.ts` is now four tests about console lines. The frontend's suite is **74 across 11 files** and `packages/shared` is **37 across 4**, so `pnpm test` is **160** — ~~160~~ ~~170~~ ~~183~~ **189** since Task 1.12.3 took the frontend to 84 across 11, Task 1.12.4 to 97 across 12 and Task 1.12.5 to **103 across 12**. The artefact moved for the second time in two stories: **346,167 B of JavaScript** against Task 1.11.5's 344,537 on an unchanged 10,926 B stylesheet, still four files, and `index.html` is 1,101 B at a new hash (`575f3730…` on a default build) because it carries the hashed script filename — the trap this file already records, invisible to a size comparison. **Task 1.12.3 gave the application its first state, its first effect and its first network loop, and deleted the probe in the same change** — `apps/frontend/src/use-backend-health.ts` is one hook, `useBackendHealth()`, owning the state, the interval, the visibility rule and the teardown, and rendering nothing. **The interval is `HEALTH_POLL_INTERVAL_MS = 30_000`, a literal, and the reason is in the code**: it clears Task 1.12.2's 5 s floor six times over, and the three costs decided the rest — 4 rendered lines a minute in the shared dev terminal against 24 at 5 s, 2 requests a minute **per open tab** against the deployed backend's existing 16 idle log records a minute, and the Consumption plan's idle-rate condition of under 1,000 bytes per second, which platform probes do not count against and these polls do. **A `VITE_` variable was rejected**, so this story still has exactly one environment variable and the three-place declaration set is untouched; the deciding argument is that the number is **coupled** to `API_TIMEOUT_MS`, which is a literal, and making one of a coupled pair configurable is what lets an operator invert an ordering nothing checks from a place where the other half is not visible. **That ordering is checked now rather than only stated** — a test asserts `HEALTH_POLL_INTERVAL_MS > API_TIMEOUT_MS`, which is this repository's own rule that a test beats an eighth `verify` step when the thing being checked is reachable from code. **Scheduling is a chain of `setTimeout`s rather than `setInterval`**, so the next poll is scheduled when the previous one _settles_ and two requests can never be outstanding at once — the overlap the floor exists to prevent is structurally impossible as well as arithmetically avoided, and the floor is kept anyway so a future move back to an interval cannot silently reintroduce it. **A hidden tab does not poll and a returning one polls immediately**, which costs Tasks 1.12.6, 1.12.7 and Story 1.13 something real rather than the code: an **automated tab reports `hidden`**, so a browser-driven measurement of the poll sees the first request and then silence unless it makes the tab visible. A tab hidden mid-flight lets that request finish and writes its result, because cancelling a request the backend has already been asked to serve buys nothing. **The seven outcomes collapse onto the three states in four lines of judgement**: both `api-error` and `http-error` are `degraded` / `not-ok-status`, because the client tells them apart only by whether the body carried a quotable `requestId` and that is not a distinction `BackendStatus` has; both `timeout` and `unreachable` are `unreachable`, because nothing arrived; and **`aborted` maps to no state at all**, returning the previous state untouched, which is the "resolved after unmount" bug closed at the one place it can be closed. **There is no fourth state name and `hasChecked` is why one was not needed** — before the first poll settles `status` reads `unreachable`, which is literally true and true for an uninteresting reason, and the boolean beside it is how Task 1.12.4 tells that apart from a check that ran and failed without leaking a client-lifecycle fact into a vocabulary defined entirely in terms of the wire contract. **Recovery and the surviving timestamp are structural rather than remembered**: every failing branch spreads the previous state, so a failure cannot clear `lastSuccessAt`, and the next successful poll returns the state to `healthy` on the same mounted hook. **There is no `try`/`catch` anywhere in the loop and its absence is deliberate** — `getHealth()` never throws, so a rejection would be a bug in the client rather than a backend that is down, and it is also what keeps an unreachable backend out of `ErrorBoundary` entirely. Two findings its brief did not anticipate: **the React Compiler rules said nothing at all** on the first stateful shipped code — see the paragraph below, because the reason matters more than the outcome — and **a lint error saying a guard is dead is not always evidence the guard is dead**, which cost one one-line function and no disabled rule. **Task 1.12.4 built what those states look like and answered the one-indicator-or-two question Story 1.4 posed and Story 1.5 put on screen: it is TWO indicators sharing one marker language, not one indicator whose meaning widens.** `apps/frontend/src/components/BackendIndicator/` is the second, and the argument is the one that made `BackendStatus` a second type rather than three more members on `FeedStatus` — `FeedStatus` is a fact the backend **reports** about the market data and `BackendStatus` is one this client **concludes** about whether the backend answered at all, they fail independently, and a live feed behind an unreachable backend is a real state a single indicator would have to pick between. What is shared is the visual language rather than the component, and **the cost is stated rather than discovered**: the two stylesheets now hold that language by imitation, so a change to the marker idiom means editing both. It is presentational — four props, no hook, no `fetch`, and it takes **four of `useBackendHealth()`'s five fields** rather than the result object whole, because a prop named after a hook's return type is how a presentational component acquires a dependency on a network loop; the fifth, `lastSuccess`, is not rendered because its only interesting member is `version`, which is `"0.0.0"` deliberately. **The not-yet-checked case is a neutral placeholder** — a dashed marker and the word `checking` — rather than nothing and rather than the honest-but-uninteresting `unreachable`: rendering `unreachable` flashes the client's own startup as a fact about the server on every page load, and rendering nothing collapses the region and shifts the whole chrome when the first result lands. **`degradedCause` selects a sentence rather than being hidden or shown as a slug**: `not-ok-status` and `unreadable-body` are engineering words nobody can act on, but they are genuinely different diagnoses, so each picks its own user-facing line — a `title` attribute was the obvious home and was rejected for the reason Task 1.4.5 rejected a tooltip, that it is unreachable by keyboard and by touch. **No request id is rendered at all**, which is Task 1.12.2's rule applied rather than re-taken: an id is a labelled reference beside a failure a user is being asked to report, and this reports a state. The last successful check is an **absolute 24-hour clock time formatted by hand** rather than through `toLocaleTimeString` — a locale-dependent string changes width when a meridiem comes and goes, which is exactly what `tabular-nums` cannot fix — and it is shown in every state **except** `healthy`, where it would be a second copy of a fact the word already carries; a client that has never once been answered gets its own sentence instead of a gap, which is what a wrong `VITE_API_BASE_URL` looks like from here. **The a11y panel produced a real violation rather than this repository's standing inconclusive**, which is the first time that has happened: the placeholder's `--ink-disabled` label measured **2.09:1** at 12px against the page ground where 4.5 is the threshold, so it is `--ink-secondary` now — disabled ink is for a control nobody can operate, and receding is a job for weight and hierarchy rather than for ink below the contrast floor. Scoped to `#storybook-root` as the addon does, the grid is **0 violations, 2 passes, 0 inconclusive** after the fix. measured, and deliberately not spent a second deploy on, because 1.12.6 already produced that cause from both producers and found they render identically. **`unreachable` has two latency signatures rather than one**: a CORS rejection fails at the **round trip** (270–764 ms) because the response arrives and the browser discards it, where a hung socket fails at the 5 s deadline and stretches the cycle to 36 s — Epic 3 inherits that distinction. **The three instruments disagree and that table is Story 1.13's acceptance criterion**: browser `TypeError: Failed to fetch` and `UNREACHABLE`, `curl` with the same `Origin` a **200 with the full body** carrying `access-control-allow-origin: https://marketpulse-wrong-origin.example` (the server asserting the *configured* origin unconditionally — Story 1.8's finding alive in production), and the backend's log **38 requests in 4 minutes, every one `statusCode: 200`**. the other 27 occurrences of "103 tests" across the tree are historical records correct in their own context, which is the distinction a naive grep-and-replace would have destroyed. **Three recorded claims had stopped being true**: `docs/adr/0011-*` §23 still described `health-probe.ts` in the present tense two tasks after Task 1.12.3 deleted it; `docs/adr/0009-*` and Story 1.7's STORY.md both said the React Compiler rules had never fired "because nothing shipped here has state", which is **wrong in its stated reason and right in its conclusion** — the repository now ships state, an effect and a network loop and all seventeen rules were still silent, because `set-state-in-effect` objects to a _synchronous_ update in an effect body. **And the coverage table moved for two of three packages** — shared 30.00% → **75.86%**, the frontend 68.25% → **85.80%**, the backend unchanged to the digit — while **Storybook's output is 63 files rather than the recorded 59**, a figure that had been carried as "unchanged" for four stories. The **non-atomic-upload scoping clause was applied to all 14 files** that carry the claim, as a scoping clause and never a strike-through, because the window is real on a changing artefact and Task 1.12.7 did not re-measure it there; and the **"1–4 requests per 30 s" probe baseline was refined to a precise and explainable 4** in the four files carrying it, one of which is Story 1.13's. **The epic's fourth exit criterion — backend health/status viewable from the frontend — is met**, on local evidence dated 2026-09-04 and deployed evidence dated 2026-09-04, recorded here for Task 1.13.6 to read rather than re-derive. Story 1.12 is closed and Story 1.13 remains. **Story 1.13 is COMPLETE (6 tasks) and with it Epic 1 is closed (2026-09-04).** Task 1.13.1 chose the browser tool, installing nothing permanent** — **Playwright 1.62.1**, with the specs in a **fourth workspace package at `e2e/`** and a root script named **`pnpm e2e`**; the full record is `planning/epic-01-application-foundation/story-13-.../BROWSER-TESTING.md`, which is to Story 1.13 what `HOSTING.md` is to Story 1.11. Both candidates were installed, run against real browsers and reverted, and the tree finished byte-identical with `pnpm verify` at exit 0. **The baseline this repository quotes for store entries was itself wrong and that is finding zero**: the working tree read **425** and a fresh install from the same lockfile reads **400**, because pnpm never prunes the virtual store — `pnpm prune` removes nothing — so the count drifts upward as packages come and go and `apps/frontend/node_modules` was 4.7 MB of orphans against 4.0 KB rebuilt. **A virtual-store count is only comparable across a fresh install — and that rule applies retroactively to every `+N entries` figure recorded here since Story 1.9, none of which stated it.** From that true baseline (400 entries / 272,324 KB / 4,591 lockfile lines), Playwright is **+4 entries** — +3 on Linux, since one is the darwin-only `fsevents@2.3.2`, the same asymmetry that makes the tree 398 packages on a Mac and 397 on the runner — **+18.3 MB**, **+38 lockfile lines**, `pnpm-workspace.yaml` byte-unchanged, at exit 0; Cypress 16.0.0 is **+170 entries**, **+48.4 MB**, **+1,150 lockfile lines** and **exit 1**. **`allowBuilds` fired, and only for the loser**, which nobody had assumed either way: Cypress trips `[ERR_PNPM_IGNORED_BUILDS]` and pnpm rewrote the tracked file with the invalid `cypress: set this to true or false` stub, reproducing the documented failure mode exactly, while **Playwright ships no install script anywhere in its chain** — read out of the published tarballs rather than inferred from a green install, and confirmed by a full sweep of the installed store finding **`esbuild@0.28.2` and nothing else**, so Task 1.4.5 is still the only time the policy has fired in the shipping tree. The mechanism is a design choice: Cypress downloads its browser in a `postinstall` and Playwright in an **explicit command**. **Of Cypress's 170 entries, 22 are second copies of packages this workspace already had** — `@typescript-eslint/*`, `@storybook/*`, `@eslint/*`, `@babel/*` — because its `supports-color@^8.1.1` dependency re-keys pnpm's peer resolution for everything that transitively reaches it, which a package count hides. **The binaries live outside `node_modules` and are the real cost, and they are Task 1.13.4's cache problem**: Playwright is **1.1 GB over 5 directories** by default, **554 MB** scoped with `install chromium` (three artefacts, not one — Chrome for Testing 356 MB, headless shell 196 MB, FFmpeg 2.5 MB) and **~199 MB** with `--only-shell`; Cypress is **640 MB**, one Electron, **not scopable**. That was measured cold through `CYPRESS_CACHE_FOLDER` because **this machine already held 1.1 GB of Cypress binaries** whose `postinstall` would have reported the download as free — the number a spike is most likely to get wrong in the tool's favour. **Browsers real rather than nominal: Playwright two of three here, Cypress one and not the one you download.** Playwright's WebKit `launch()`es in 129 ms and **`newPage()` never returns** on macOS 14.7.6/arm64, where Playwright itself warns the build is frozen; Cypress's bundled 640 MB Electron prints _"deprecated as a test browser and will be removed"_ and `cypress info` detects the machine's **own unpinned Chrome 152**, where Playwright pins Chrome for Testing 151.0.7922.34 through the lockfile — the reproducibility argument that pins every action to a SHA, one layer up. **`document.visibilityState` is `visible` in every candidate, so the hazard this story was written around does not exist in either tool and no override is needed.** Playwright goes further: a second page does **not** hide the first — both report `visible` at once and `bringToFront()` changes nothing — so several pages can poll simultaneously. **This file's recorded "an automated tab reports `hidden` and throttles React's scheduler" is a property of the browser-extension harness rather than of automation, and does not transfer.** Proved against the running pair rather than a blank page: the `Backend service` region goes `checking` → `healthy` and the poll fires unaided — and **the cycle reads 30.02 s, not the 31.00 / 31.05 s Tasks 1.12.6 and 1.12.7 recorded**, which confirms their attribution of the extra second to Chrome aligning timers in a backgrounded tab, by its absence; a Playwright check should expect **30 s**, and the difference is not drift. Page load produced **three** `/health` requests 0.12 s apart before the first gap — the dev server plus `StrictMode`, not the poll — so a spec must not count requests from load. **The specs' home is forced rather than chosen, which is the best outcome available.** A bare root-level directory fails twice: **`TS1295`**, because the nearest `package.json` is the root's, which deliberately has no `"type": "module"`, so every `import` is an error under `verbatimModuleSyntax`; and **`MODULE_NOT_FOUND` on `@marketpulse/shared`**, because pnpm links a workspace package only into packages that declare it — proved by contrast against `apps/frontend`, where the identical call resolves. `@types/node` fails a third way (`TS2688`). So it is a **fourth workspace package** with a new `pnpm-workspace.yaml` glob, and the strings it asserts on can be **imported** from `packages/shared` rather than written out, which is what `BACKEND_STATUSES` exists for. **ESLint and Prettier were proved to read it rather than assumed to** — `--print-config` reports **168 rules**, identical to a covered source file, and it was **made to fail** on a missing `await` with `no-floating-promises`, a rule that cannot fire without type information and the one a browser suite needs most; `prettier --file-info` reports `"inferredParser": "typescript"`, and the real root `eslint .` and `format:check` both named the file. **The third wiring is the one with no symptom: `tsc` does not reach it for free.** Without `{ "path": "e2e" }` in the root solution file a deliberate `TS2322` in a spec is **completely silent at exit 0** — the specs would lint, format and be typechecked by nothing. Root `build` needs no edit, because `e2e` emits nothing. **The package owes `lint`, `typecheck` and `clean` and must never have `test`, `dev`, `build` or `coverage`**: it joins every `pnpm -r` fan-out automatically — measured at **"Scope: 4 of 5 workspace projects"** for `test`, `coverage` and `test:process`, with the 189 fast tests unchanged — and **the only thing keeping the browser suite out of `pnpm test` is that absent script, which nothing checks**, a stated invariant of the third kind. **`e2e` was checked against `pnpm help -a`'s 44 built-ins and is free**, with the detection validated in the same run against `clean`, `test`, `start`, `config` and `env`, which it correctly identified as built-ins. It is a root script beside `ready`, `image` and `coverage` and deliberately **not** a `verify` step; where it sits relative to the pipeline's founding rule is Task 1.13.4's. Two Playwright costs stated rather than discovered later: **`@playwright/test` is CommonJS**, so an ESM script importing it needs a default import and a named import is a hard `SyntaxError`; and **`playwright` and `playwright-core` are not importable by name**, because only `@playwright/test` is declared — pnpm's strict linking doing the same thing it does to `pino` in `apps/backend`. What Cypress was better at is recorded for ADR 0013: per-command DOM snapshots make interactive failure diagnosis better by default where Playwright's trace is a separate artefact somebody has to remember to record, `cypress open` is a better first run, assertion retry-ability is built into every command rather than specific matchers, and testing in the machine's own Chrome is a defensible position read the other way. **Task 1.13.2 installed it and made one journey real, and every one of 1.13.1's predictions held on re-measurement.** The install reproduces to the byte from the shipping tree's 400 entries / 272,316 KB / 4,591 lockfile lines — **404 entries (+4), +18,756 KB, +38 lockfile lines**, `pnpm-workspace.yaml` md5-unchanged, exit 0 — `allowBuilds` **did not fire**, and the install-script sweep is a check rather than a formality now: it returns **`esbuild@0.28.2` and nothing else**, so Task 1.4.5 is still the only time the policy has fired here. What shipped is `@playwright/test@1.62.1` as a **root** devDependency, the fourth workspace package at `e2e/`, a root script **`pnpm e2e`** (`scripts/run-e2e.mjs`), and **`scripts/pair-addresses.mjs`** — the pair's addresses moved out of `check-ready.mjs` into one module with two readers, because a harness with its own copy of `5173` has forked the pair's definition on day one. **The three wirings behaved exactly as predicted and the third was made to matter rather than assumed to**: a deliberate `TS2322` in a spec fails `pnpm typecheck` with the reference present and is silent without it; ESLint reports **168 rules** on the spec, on `playwright.config.ts` and on `packages/shared/src/health.ts` alike — so the config needs **no** `disableTypeChecked` entry, which is bought by `include: ["**/*.ts"]` in `e2e/tsconfig.json` — and `no-floating-promises` was **made to fire** on a missing `await page.goto()`. `pnpm test` is untouched at "Scope: 4 of 5 workspace projects" and **189** tests. **The local browser install was free, and that is a hazard rather than a saving**: Task 1.13.1's 1.1 GB survived its own revert because the binaries live in `~/Library/Caches/ms-playwright/` and a `node_modules` revert does not reach them; the three artefacts re-measured on disk are **356 + 196 + 2.5 = 554.5 MB** and the pinned browser is **Chrome for Testing 151.0.7922.34**. **What the suite runs against is forced by the backend rather than chosen for the frontend, and that is the decision every later spec inherits**: `CORS_ORIGIN` holds exactly one origin, so a suite pointed at `vite preview` on 4173 or at a dumb static host drives a page every one of whose backend calls the browser refuses while the server logs a 200 — the exact failure this story exists to catch, arriving as a property of the harness. So it drives **the origin `CORS_ORIGIN` names**, and `playwright.config.ts` has **no default base URL at all**, because a literal fallback is the second copy of the port the arrangement exists to prevent; a bare `playwright test` throws naming `pnpm e2e`. The cost is stated rather than discovered: **Story 1.5's deep-link and `/assets/nope.js` criteria are not assertable against this target**, because the dev server answers both 200 — they are properties of a host and belong to Task 1.13.5. **Playwright's own `webServer` was rejected on measurement**: it judges readiness by one URL, and a busy 3000 leaves `pnpm dev` running and looking entirely healthy, so a frontend probe passes against half a system and the backend is the half this suite watches; `pnpm e2e` gates on `check-ready.mjs` **itself** rather than a copy. **The `expect` timeout is 10 s and not the default 5 s, because 5 s _is_ the request deadline** — the `checking` placeholder clears in 50.7 ms healthy and holds the full 5 s against a dead backend, so a timeout picked from the healthy figure is red on the failing path, which is the path the suite is for. Zero retries; **Chromium only, with WebKit excluded rather than omitted**, because a frozen build presents as a hanging test. **It was made to fail twice and the unplanned one is the finding**: deliberately, a renamed region fails at exit 1 naming `getByRole('region', { name: 'Market breadth' })` while the **count** assertion still passes, which is the layering working; accidentally, scoping the backend indicator with `toContainText(/\b(healthy|…)\b/)` fails because the region's text is **`Backend servicehealthy`** with no separator, so there is no word boundary — Story 1.9's "do not assert on a single element's text where a component splits it" arriving **from the other direction**, since the concatenation a screen reader is handed is not the string the elements read as. Exit codes are **1** through `pnpm run e2e`, through `node scripts/run-e2e.mjs` and through a bare `playwright test` with no base URL, and `run-e2e.mjs` handles the signal case explicitly because a child killed by a signal has a `null` status. **Warm, `pnpm e2e` is 1.26–1.74 s wall of which Playwright reports 677 ms–1.1 s, and ~200 ms of that is the browser** — launch 81–183 ms, `newPage()` plus `goto` 124–294 ms — a floor that does not shrink as specs are added; the readiness gate is **0.07 s** as `node scripts/check-ready.mjs` against **0.30 s** through `pnpm run ready`, so the recorded ~0.3 s figure is pnpm's startup rather than the check's. **A green run leaves one 96-byte `.last-run.json` and a failed one ~450 KB** — a 318,749 B trace, a 121,570 B screenshot and an 11,318 B page snapshot under `e2e/test-results/`, cleared at the start of the next run and ignored by all three tools, verified with `git status --porcelain --ignored=matching`. `pnpm verify` is **23.20 s at exit 0**, and **the frontend artefact did not move** — 348,124 B `d280e167…`, 12,128 B `134d5dd8…`, 1,101 B `177df27d…`, 300 B, **361,653 B over four files** — which is the check rather than a coincidence, because this task shipped no application source. **Task 1.13.3 wrote the journeys worth having — ~~five~~ **four** spec files, ten tests, every one seen to fail for its own reason — and one measurement shaped the whole suite: `route.fulfill()` bypasses the browser's CORS check.** Taken two ways against the running pair, a fulfilled response with the header stripped and one with **no CORS headers at all** are both accepted by Chromium and read normally by the page, so route interception can produce every state in this suite **except the one the story exists for**. That decided three things at once. **The failure states are produced in the browser and nothing mutates the pair** — `route.abort("connectionrefused")` is a genuine `TypeError: Failed to fetch`, both `degraded` causes are genuine `fulfill`s, and all three are installed before `goto()` so the first poll is the failing one and the state is on screen in **157–172 ms**. **So the workers question has an answer rather than a default**: there are no mutating specs, Playwright's default of 4 stands, and the reversal trigger is stated — a state that cannot be produced from inside the browser, of which there is already one. Killing the backend was rejected on Story 1.8's measurement rather than taste: freeing a port does not recover a `node --watch` loop, so a spec that stops it locally cannot reliably put it back. **And the cross-origin criterion is met by _catching_ rather than producing**: `backend-health.spec.ts` asserts the healthy path and its purpose is to go red on a wrong allowlist, which it was made to do — a backend started with `CORS_ORIGIN=https://marketpulse-wrong-origin.example` took **four tests red at exit 1** naming `getByText(/^healthy$/)`, while `curl`with the real`Origin`got a **200 with a full body** carrying`access-control-allow-origin: https://marketpulse-wrong-origin.example` and the backend's log recorded **10 `/health` requests, every one `statusCode: 200`**. One refinement to the recorded symptom: the **caught** error is `TypeError: Failed to fetch` and that is all the application ever sees, but Chromium's console additionally names the mechanism outright — two messages for one failure, and only the first is reachable from code. **A property of the harness that Task 1.13.5 must read first**: `pair-addresses.mjs` resolves the frontend's origin _from_ `CORS_ORIGIN`, so locally the allowlist and where-the-frontend-is **cannot disagree** through `pnpm e2e` — the break above had to be driven by hand — and run normally the failure is caught one layer earlier by `pnpm ready` (`✗ frontend … ENOTFOUND`, exit 1) so the suite refuses to start. Deployed, those are two independent values (a literal in `deploy.yml` against a Static Web App's hostname), which is why **1.13.5 is the only place that failure can be caught rather than pre-empted**. **Recovery was watched across two real poll intervals** — healthy at 219 ms, `unreachable` at 30.30 s, `healthy` again at 60.51 s, `Last confirmed 14:58:19` held across the outage and neither cleared nor advanced, `timeOrigin` unchanged and exactly **one** navigation entry — and the measured gaps are **30.018 / 30.012 s**, reproducing Task 1.13.1's 30.02 s rather than the 31 s a backgrounded tab reads. **Waits are derived and the copy of the interval is a _checked_ copy**: `e2e/support/poll-timings.ts` restates `HEALTH_POLL_INTERVAL_MS` and `API_TIMEOUT_MS` because importing them is structurally impossible — every path runs through `api-base-url.ts`, which reads `import.meta.env` at module load and throws `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'VITE_API_BASE_URL')` under Node — and moving them to `packages/shared` was rejected as relocating shipped code for a test's convenience, the shape Task 1.10.5 refused with `MIN_PORT`. The recovery journey measures the interval the application actually polls at and asserts it against the copy, made to fail by setting the copy to 20 s. **The axe question is decided and the answer is a GATE**: zero violations asserted on two pages — the landing route healthy and the not-found route with the backend unreachable — with `incomplete` attached as an annotation and unable to fail anything. It is an adoption rather than a reversal of Story 1.9's rejection, because that argument was about the workshop's compromises becoming fixtures and none of it reaches the assembled application; what decided it is that a browser is the only level here that can see **contrast** (structurally unrunnable below — no stylesheet, `getComputedStyle` returns nothing, `getTokens()` throws — and Task 1.12.4 found a real 2.09:1 violation on this exact component) and **whole-document rules** (`landmark-unique`, `landmark-one-main`, `page-has-heading-one`, `region`, which the addon's `#storybook-root` scoping structurally cannot judge — Task 1.7.6 measured 3 violations unscoped against 0 scoped). It cost **+0 store entries** (404 → 404), **+3 lockfile lines**, an md5-unchanged `pnpm-workspace.yaml` and a sweep still returning `esbuild@0.28.2` alone, because `axe-core@4.13.0` was already in the store through `@storybook/addon-a11y` — and it is `axe-core` directly rather than `@axe-core/playwright`, at the **same pin**, because two axe versions would make the workshop and the suite disagree about one page; **that pin pair is a stated invariant nothing checks**. The baseline re-taken against the **dev server** — the first time this measurement has been taken anywhere but a built artefact on a static host — is **0 violations / 37 passes / 1 inconclusive** on the landing route and **0 / 25 / 0** on the four others, reproducing Tasks 1.5.4, 1.7.6 and 1.12.5 exactly, with the one inconclusive still `color-contrast` over the two `aria-hidden` arrows. **Nine deliberate breaks, each reverted**, and two of them taught more than a confirmation: rewording all three detail sentences left the `every route stays usable` test **passing**, which is the layering working; and a throwing probe inside a `Region` reported `getByRole('alert')` count **1** rather than a broken page, which is Task 1.7.6's containment measured from the outside for the first time. **`getByRole("alert")` is how "nothing failed to render" is asserted** — by role rather than by title, so one query covers every boundary placement including any this application grows, and Task 1.13.2's title match was amended to it. **A render-failure journey is declined with the gap named**: this application contains no way to produce one, and a browser-side injection would mean either shipping a throwing route in the bundle every visitor downloads or a build-time flag that makes the artefact under test not the artefact that ships; the reversal trigger is Epic 4's first real data or Epic 6's canvas. A **hung socket** is declined too — 36 s to assert a distinction the user cannot see, against a deadline Task 1.12.6 already proved wired in a browser — with `API_TIMEOUT_MS`'s copy left explicitly **unchecked** and said so. **The rules live in `e2e/README.md`** rather than only in a task file, which is what the brief asked for: the must-not-assert list (including the two traps a browser makes newly writable — colour, which would work here and must not be written because greyscale separates this palette by 1.05:1, and `innerText()`, which reports the CSS-transformed `HEALTHY` where the DOM text and every Playwright matcher see `healthy`), the workers decision, the axe decision, the derivation rule, and **what a green run does not certify** in the shape ADR 0010 uses. **Ten journeys are 1:02.2 s wall of which nine are 3.4 s** — the recovery journey is the whole cost and the others run underneath it — `pnpm verify` is **26.14 s at exit 0**, `pnpm test` is untouched at "Scope: 4 of 5 workspace projects" and **189** tests, and the frontend artefact reproduces Task 1.12.5's four files and **361,653 B** to the byte, because this task shipped no application source either. **Task 1.13.4 put it in CI, resolved the tension with the pipeline's founding rule out loud, and the gate found a real accessibility defect on its very first run.** The position is a **second job named `e2e` in `.github/workflows/verify.yml`** — not a chain step, not a separate workflow — with the argument for each rejection beside it. A `verify` step is rejected on the property eight clean-clone runs have measured: **`pnpm verify` runs with nothing listening**, which is why `pnpm ready` is not a step, and a chain that needs two ports free stops being runnable from a cold tree. A separate workflow is rejected because **all three of `deploy.yml`'s reasons are properties of a deploy rather than of a check, and two of them argue the other way** — a browser journey going red _is_ a claim about the commit and the badge should report it, and a cancelled browser run is a cancelled check, which `verify`'s per-ref concurrency group already handles correctly. What keeps the second job honest against Story 1.10's rule is that it invokes **`pnpm build`, `pnpm dev` and `pnpm e2e` by name** and defines no port, no browser command and no readiness rule of its own. **It runs in parallel with `verify` rather than `needs:`-ing it** — the two answer different questions and serialising adds the chain's ~40 s for feedback that is not better — and **it gates a merge**: ruleset `main` (id 22160620) now requires **`verify` and `e2e`**, so a reader finding only `verify` should read that as the browser gate having been removed rather than never set. **Zero retries in CI, decided rather than defaulted**: a gate that retries cannot tell a flake from a defect. **The headline is that the axe gate went red on its first run for a reason nobody planned** — `scrollable-region-focusable` on the landing route, a **real WCAG 2.1.1 defect that had stood for five stories** and that no instrument here could ever have seen: a region is sized by the grid and takes its own overflow, and a scrolling container that cannot be reached by keyboard is unreachable content. It never fired locally because **the rule does not fire while the scrolling box happens to contain something focusable** — `Market topology` holds Story 1.4's render check, which holds a popover trigger — and the runner reported it on `Current investigations`, which holds a heading and one sentence. It **reproduces on the development machine at a viewport 160 px shorter**, so it is a real defect a taller window was hiding rather than a property of Linux; `tabIndex={0}` on every region is the fix, applied to all four rather than to the ones currently overflowing, because which one scrolls is a function of the viewport and of what Epics 4 to 7 put in them. The axe baseline is **unmoved at 0 violations / 37 passes / 1 inconclusive** after it, at 720, 560 and 480 px. **The `--only-shell` browser is not a lesser renderer and that was confirmed rather than assumed**, with a throwaway probe since deleted: on the runner it reports the page ground exactly (`rgb(244, 243, 238)`) and axe's `color-contrast` rule passes on **65 nodes**, the same number as macOS — the control the gate needed, because a shell that skipped style computation would turn it green by making it blind. **The Linux browser figures are not the macOS ones**: `playwright install --only-shell chromium` puts **267 MB** on disk in **two** directories (`chromium_headless_shell` 262 MB and **`ffmpeg` 4.9 MB — so `--only-shell` still fetches FFmpeg**), against Task 1.13.1's ~199 MB single artefact; compressed into the cache it is **108,075,781 B**, the largest single entry this repository stores against GitHub's 10 GB allowance (609,986,375 B across 9 entries when taken). **The browser cache is a third category and it is allowed where `dist/` is not** — a browser binary is a downloaded _tool_, immutable and addressed by version, and it cannot make a build wrong by being stale because a stale one is a different version Playwright refuses to launch. Its key carries the runner OS and the **Playwright version rather than the lockfile hash**, and it has **no restore-key**, which is the deliberate difference from the store cache: a partial store is a correct store that installs the rest, where a browser directory restored from another version _looks_ populated and holds the wrong build, and `actions/cache` reports a restore-key hit and a total miss identically. The download step **runs on a hit too and prints what it did**, which is the only thing that separates a restored cache from a silent re-download. The store cache is **restored and never saved** in this job — both jobs compute the same key and two savers race to a "Cache already exists" warning that reads like a fault — so `verify` owns saving it. **`ubuntu-latest` reports 2 workers where the laptop reports 4**, Playwright's default being half the CPU count, and the nine short journeys still finish underneath the recovery journey: the suite is **69.2–72.6 s** there against 62–64 s locally. **The whole job is 99 / 102 / 103 s across three green readings — a 4 s spread where the seven-step chain has ~9 s over five — so this is so far the more repeatable half of the run**; warm, its split is install **4,677–5,035 ms** on an exact store hit (`reused 400, downloaded 0`), browser **918–981 ms** on a hit against **5,582 ms** cold, `pnpm build` **6,430–7,468 ms** and `pnpm e2e` **69,208–72,569 ms**. **All ten journeys run, the one-minute recovery one included, and that is a stated decision rather than a consequence** — `--grep-invert "recovers on the next poll"` is the lever and it is not pulled, because skipping it would leave the story's recovery criterion asserted on a laptop and not in CI. **It was made to go red twice more, deliberately, on a throwaway commit with its own draft pull request**: a spec assertion, which uploads **872,142 B** (a 755,894 B trace, a 117,923 B screenshot, a 10,212 B error context and the 7,080 B pair log — larger than the local ~450 KB because the axe journey's trace carries the injected axe source); and **the failure a laptop cannot produce**, a deliberate `PORT=0` on the pair, which gives `✗ backend … ECONNREFUSED` beside a ticked frontend, exit 1 in **15.7 s**, and an artefact of **577 B** that is the pair's log and nothing else, because there is no Playwright output at all. That second one is the vindication of `pnpm e2e` gating on `check-ready.mjs` itself: a one-URL `webServer` probe would have driven the whole suite against a page with no backend. Failure artefacts are **7 days**, on failure only, and the upload path was corrected by measurement — `upload-artifact` roots an archive at the **common ancestor** of its paths, so a `RUNNER_TEMP` log beside `e2e/test-results` produced an archive nested at `marketpulse/marketpulse/e2e/…` beside a stray `_temp/`; the log is copied into `e2e/test-results` so there is one root. **WebKit and Firefox stay unmeasured on Linux deliberately** — naming an engine that has never been seen green means a second browser in the cache and roughly a doubling of the run, and Task 1.13.1's WebKit failure mode is a _hanging_ test rather than an error. **No new action was needed**: the job reuses `actions/checkout`, `actions/setup-node`, `actions/cache`, `actions/cache/restore` (the same repository at the same SHA) and `actions/upload-artifact`, so it is still **five distinct actions, now across thirteen uses and six distinct `uses:` references**. The install reads **`Packages: +400`** on the runner, which supersedes Task 1.10.1's 397-on-Linux/398-on-macOS pair — Story 1.13's three additions moved it. **The frontend artefact moved by 11 bytes and the runner reproduces it exactly**: 348,124 → **348,135 B** (`b98aeaa5…`) of JavaScript on an unchanged 12,128 B / `134d5dd8…` stylesheet, `index.html` 1,101 B at a new hash (`07983678…`), `staticwebapp.config.json` 300 B, for **361,664 B over four files at 278 modules** — the `tabIndex={0}`, and a Linux build byte-identical to a Mac one for the second story running. **Task 1.13.5 built the post-deploy browser check Task 1.11.7 declined, produced the failure it exists for against the live environment, and corrected two recorded claims doing it.** The decline was re-read rather than treated as an oversight, and both halves are stated: **what changed** is that Story 1.12 shipped a client that polls the backend, so the failure 1.11.7 named can now be produced; **what still stands** is that there is no preview environment and deliberately never will be one on this plan, so this runs **after** a merge and gates nothing — its output is a rollback decision. It is a second Playwright **config** over `e2e/specs-deployed/` rather than a second project (a project shares `use.baseURL` and the testDir sweep, and `pnpm e2e` must never reach production while `pnpm e2e:deployed` must never need a local pair), run by `scripts/run-deployed-check.mjs`, gated by `scripts/check-deployed.mjs`, and executed in CI as a **`check-deployed` job in `deploy.yml` with `needs: deploy`** — a job and not a step, because a red result must not read as "the deploy failed" when the deploy succeeded and the deployed system is wrong. `support/app.ts` and `support/axe.ts` transfer unchanged; `support/pair.ts` deliberately does not. **The addresses are two inputs and never one input and a derivation, and that is the whole architecture**: deployed there are **three independent values in three places** — `VITE_API_BASE_URL`, a literal in `deploy.yml` substituted at build time; the Static Web App's hostname, a fact about an Azure resource; and `CORS_ORIGIN`, an environment variable that exists **only in the platform** because `deploy.yml` uses `update` and never `create` — all of which must agree and none of which any file compares. **Both failures were made to happen and caught, and they are indistinguishable on screen, which is why each needs its own assertion.** `CORS_ORIGIN` was set wrong on the live Container App (revision `0000047`, `Activating` → `RunningAtMaxScale` in 44 s): the check went **red at exit 1 in 53.2 s, 3 failed / 7 passed**, while `curl` with the **real** frontend `Origin` got a **200 with the full 62-byte contract body** carrying `access-control-allow-origin: https://marketpulse-wrong-origin.example`, and Log Analytics recorded **15 `/health` requests through the 65-second window, every one `statusCode: 200`**. Restored (revision `0000048`) and **read back from the platform** — all five environment variables match the values captured beforehand — and green again. The `VITE_API_BASE_URL` failure was produced **at the artefact without touching production**: `vite build` with the variable unset **succeeds** and ships a bundle containing `http://localhost:3000` and no mention of the deployed backend, and the same spec driven against it went red naming both origins. The gap is stated rather than hidden — the mixed-content block itself is not reproduced, because a page served over plain HTTP does not block a `localhost` call and the only HTTPS host available is production — and it changes nothing, because the assertion is on the request's **origin** and needs no response at all. **That page read `unreachable` / `No successful check yet.`, byte for byte what the CORS break produces**, which is the two-assertion design watched rather than argued. **Two recorded claims are corrected.** First, **`curl` is not _structurally_ incapable of catching a wrong allowlist**: the status, the body and the log genuinely cannot, but `access-control-allow-origin` is a readable copy of `CORS_ORIGIN` — `@fastify/cors` with a string origin asserts the configured value unconditionally — so an instrument **told the frontend's origin** can compare them; that value is exactly what no server-side instrument has, and the comparison is a proxy for the browser's verdict rather than the verdict, saying nothing about the second failure where the backend is never asked. The suite makes it anyway, because two instruments disagreeing is diagnostic. Second, **the `e2e` gate caught a real flake in Task 1.13.3's health spec in its first week and it took a commit rather than a re-run**: every page load makes **two** `/health` requests and **one is aborted**, because `StrictMode`'s first mount cleans up and aborts its own in-flight request through the `AbortController` `api-client.ts` composes into every call — measured 5/5 as `["request GET", "request GET", "FAILED net::ERR_ABORTED", "finished"]`. Locally the abort lands _before_ the response headers, so the aborted request produces no `response` event at all; on a loaded runner it can land _after_ them, producing a `response` carrying a 200 whose body can never be retrieved. `waitForResponse` now requires the response to have `finished()`, and that is the zero-retries policy paying for itself. **Story 1.5's two host-level criteria are finally asserted by something** — four routes deep-loaded cold as a **200 that is not a redirect**, a made-up path rendering the not-found **route**, and `/assets/nope.js` a genuine 404 — with the not-found case asserted on what **rendered** rather than on a status, because a 200 is the correct answer there and a 404 is the failure. **The axe decision is asymmetric and stated: a gate before the merge, a REPORT after it.** A red post-deploy result is a rollback decision, a contrast ratio is not a rollback, and mixing them makes the one signal that sees what nothing else does indistinguishable from the one nobody would act on immediately; the same rules already gate the same source on a real renderer, and the deployed artefact differs from the one that gate judged by exactly one string literal. So it is the **comparison** — the deployed landing route reads **0 violations / 37 passes / 1 inconclusive (`color-contrast`)**, the pre-merge gate's numbers exactly — and the reversal trigger is a **divergence**. The report also refuses to wait for `healthy`, a correction the CORS break produced rather than a preference: written that way, a broken allowlist turned **three** tests red, one of them labelled accessibility, which tells a reader something false. **It gates nothing and is not in the ruleset**, which stays at `verify` and `e2e`, for the reason `deploy` is not: requiring it would gate on something that cannot have happened yet. **It is not a monitor and has no `schedule:`** — that would be uptime monitoring, which nothing in the roadmap owns, and it has a bill, since the Consumption plan's idle rate is conditional on under 1,000 bytes per second and platform probes are not billable while these requests are; measured against the log, **a whole green run costs the deployed backend +5 requests** against a re-measured idle baseline of a steady **4 per 30 s**. **The evidence a red result carries was decided rather than left open**: both halves are probed and reported separately, and **both** failing at once is called out as more likely to be the runner's link than a simultaneous outage of two independent Azure services in two regions — Task 1.11.7's 65-second laptop "outage" turned into a printed diagnosis — while one half red with the other green is a claim about that service. **What a red result means is written where the person seeing it will look**: the job writes a failed-test-to-cause table and both asymmetric rollback procedures (backend `az containerapp update --image <digest>` in **43 s**, silently undone by the next merge; frontend a revert commit at **3 min 42 s**; `workflow_dispatch` a re-deploy and not a rollback) into the summary of the run that went red, and names the case **no revert can fix**, because `CORS_ORIGIN` is not in this repository. **The job went red on its first real execution and that is the most transferable finding in the task**, because it failed on something a laptop structurally cannot see: **`packages/shared` is consumed as built output and a fresh checkout has no `dist/`**. The job's own comment claimed the specs needed nothing compiled because `pnpm install` links the workspace package — wrong, and wrong against this repository's oldest rule — and the failure arrived from Playwright as `Cannot find module .../@marketpulse/shared/dist/index.js` then `No tests found`, in **1.7 s**, _after_ the readiness probe had passed against a perfectly healthy production. The fix is the e2e package's **own documented verb** rather than a build step invented in a workflow: `pnpm --filter @marketpulse/e2e typecheck` is `tsc -b`, and `e2e/tsconfig.json` references `packages/shared`, so it builds exactly what the specs import and nothing else — no frontend bundle and no Storybook, which root `pnpm build` would also produce and which nothing in that job drives. `run-deployed-check.mjs` now guards on **presence** with a message naming the command, presence and not freshness for Task 1.10.5's reason. **Writing that guard found a new face of a recorded trap**: this file records that `tsc -b --clean` deletes the output of the sources that _currently_ exist, and the mirror is that **deleting `dist/` by hand and leaving `tsconfig.tsbuildinfo` makes `tsc -b` emit nothing at all**, because it still believes the output is current — so the guard's own suggested fix silently does nothing there and `pnpm clean` is what is needed. Reproduced twice. A fresh checkout has no `.tsbuildinfo` and is unaffected, which is exactly why CI cannot find that one and a laptop can. **The failure path was exercised for real in the same run** — `What to do about a red result` wrote its rollback table into the run summary and the artefact upload ran, on a genuinely red job rather than a rehearsal. **Its first green execution on the runner is 35 s for the whole job** — against the `e2e` job's 99–103 s — with the `pnpm e2e:deployed` step at 8,306 ms of which the suite is **6.5 s**, an exact-key browser-cache hit installing in 1,027 ms, and readiness coherent on the first poll. Three things to read off that: **the suite is FASTER on the runner than on the laptop** (6.5 s against 9.7–10.5 s), which is the opposite of every other timing recorded here and is geography rather than the machine — the runner and both Azure services are in the United States and the laptop is not; **the axe reading agrees three ways**, deployed-on-the-runner, deployed-from-macOS and the pre-merge gate all `0 / 37 / 1`, which is the comparison the report exists to be; and **the restore-only browser cache behaved as its rule predicts**, an exact-key hit because `verify`'s `e2e` job had already saved it under the same key on the same commit, with no second saver and no `Cache already exists` warning. Figures: **10 passed in 9.7–10.5 s** locally (12.5 s wall with readiness), `pnpm verify` **23.5 s**, `pnpm test` untouched at **189** across "Scope: 4 of 5 workspace projects", the local `pnpm e2e` **10 passed (1.0m)**, and **no new dependency and no lockfile change at all**. **Task 1.13.6 closed the story with `docs/adr/0013-*` and closed Epic 1**, re-running all six acceptance criteria and re-taking every figure from a clean clone. **It moved one of the story's six unchecked invariants into a check, and the one it chose is the one whose failure a green run cannot see**: the axe gate's value depends on the browser having computed real styles, and a renderer that skipped style computation reports **zero violations by being blind** — so `expectTheRendererComputedStyles` now asserts `color-contrast` appears in axe's `passes` with more than zero nodes, in **both** `expectNoAxeViolations` and `reportAxe`, because a check on one leaves the other blind. It asserts presence-with-nodes rather than the 65 the runner measured, because 65 is a property of this page and would fail on the next component, and it was **made to fail first**. The other five stay prose with their durable copy named, and **the line between the two lists is not effort — it is whether the thing being checked is reachable from an assembled instance**, which a repository ruleset, a manifest's missing key and a second copy of a hostname are not. **Three recorded claims had stopped being true, and one of them was wrong when it was written rather than stale.** ~~"Five spec files"~~ — there have only ever been **four** (`backend-failure-states`, `backend-health`, `backend-recovery`, `landing-route`), confirmed against the commit that created them; Task 1.13.3's own write-up said five and this file copied it, which is the class of error only a re-count catches. **`README.md`'s own `pnpm test` section still read "74 across 11 — 160 in total"**, stale by three increments, in a document Task 1.12.8's sweep did not reach because the sweep was written against the twelve convention blocks. And the **397-on-Linux / 398-on-macOS** pair is now **400 / 402**, with the difference being **two** darwin-only optional packages rather than one — `fsevents@2.3.3` via Vite and `fsevents@2.3.2` through Playwright. **The duplicated-sentence sweep confirmed Task 1.12.8's shape exactly**: twelve blocks, **ten byte-identical**, Story 1.13's own among the ten — and it needed **no** amendment, because this story adds nothing to `pnpm test`, which is the first time that sweep has come back clean. The **`curl` is structurally incapable** claim stands at **16 occurrences across 13 files**, every one read rather than replaced: **four** are live claims and carry a scoping clause (`CLAUDE.md`, `docs/adr/0012-*`, `EPIC.md`, `HOSTING.md`), **nine** are historical records of Task 1.11.7's decline and are correct in their own context, **one** (`story-10/TASK-06`) is about the badge and coverage and is not this claim at all — which is Task 1.10.8's lesson that a grep count is not a copy count — and **two** already carry the correction in this story's own code. The **install-script sweep was re-run rather than assumed** and returns **`esbuild@0.28.2` and nothing else**, so `allowBuilds` still has one entry and Task 1.4.5 is still the only time the policy has fired; Story 1.13's prediction that it would fire was falsified twice and is recorded as falsified rather than quietly dropped. **Criterion 3 was re-made rather than cited**: a local backend started with a wrong `CORS_ORIGIN` took the suite to **4 failed / 5 passed** at exit 1 while `curl` with the real `Origin` got a **200 with the full 62-byte body** carrying `access-control-allow-origin: https://marketpulse-wrong-origin.example` and the backend logged **12 requests, every one `statusCode: 200`** — and it cost one thing worth recording, because the local axe test waits for `healthy` and so went red too, one of the four labelled accessibility. That is **the shape Task 1.13.5 corrected in the deployed report, kept deliberately in the local gate**: a gate has to judge a page in a known state and its baseline is state-dependent, all four reds share one visible cause, and a red gate blocks a merge where a red post-deploy result is a rollback decision. **The clean-clone figures**: 402 packages cold in 31.2 s, **404 store entries / 280,732 KB / 4,641 lockfile lines**, `pnpm verify` **exit 0 in 29.28 s** from the clone and **24.33 s** warm (build 2.63 / lint 4.48 / `format:check` 4.41 / `stories` 0.24 / `env:check` 0.25 / `test` 3.21 / `test:process` 7.78), **10 components and 10 stories files**, and the artefact reproducing Task 1.13.4's four files to the byte — 348,135 B `b98aeaa5…`, 12,128 B `134d5dd8…`, 1,101 B `07983678…`, 300 B, **361,664 B at 278 modules** — with Storybook at 63 files / 9.3 MB. **`pnpm e2e:deployed` was run from that clean clone, which is the only place its built-output guard actually fires**, and it did: no `dist/`, no `.tsbuildinfo`, the guard stopped with the message naming `pnpm --filter @marketpulse/e2e typecheck`, that command built exactly what the specs import in **0.87 s**, and the check then passed **10 tests in 10.8 s** with the deployed axe reading **0 violations / 37 passes / 1 inconclusive** — the pre-merge gate's numbers exactly, three ways, for the second story running. The browser cache was re-measured on disk and Task 1.13.1's figures reproduce: **355.8 + 196.0 + 2.5 = 554.3 MB** for `install chromium`, inside a **1.1 GB** total that still holds the Firefox and WebKit builds Task 1.13.1's spike downloaded — the hazard that a `node_modules` revert does not reach the browser cache, still visible a story later. **`e2e/README.md` is where the suite's rules live and both root documents point at it rather than duplicating it**, which is the treatment that exists because duplicating a paragraph for legibility is how the twelve-block problem started.

**Epic 2 has started. Story 2.1 (Managed Postgres Provisioning & the Secrets Boundary) is in progress — Tasks 2.1.1 to 2.1.7 are complete, the managed database exists, the deployed backend queries it, and every open decision in the story is closed. Task 2.1.1 provisioned nothing, deliberately** — the full record is `planning/epic-01-application-foundation/story-11-.../HOSTING.md` under _The database — the creation decisions_, because Epic 1's habit is one document per subject and a second `DATABASE.md` is a copy waiting to disagree. **The headline is that the region decision was taken away rather than taken**: `HOSTING.md` said East US and Epic 2 inherited it, and East US returns **zero Postgres editions and zero versions** for this subscription with the reason `"Provisioning is restricted in this region"` and the mechanism named in the platform's own feature list as **`OfferRestricted: Enabled`** — `Disabled` in all seven other US and Canadian regions checked, every one of which offers 11–18 and B1MS. So Task 2.1.1 put the database in ~~**East US 2**~~ East US 2, where `marketpulse-frontend` already sits inside the East-US resource group, and the three price meters are **identical in both regions** — **both halves of which Task 2.1.5 falsified one day later: East US 2 is `OfferRestricted` for this subscription too, the database is in NORTH CENTRAL US, and the meters are identical for that pair and vary by up to 29% across US regions generally**, so the move costs nothing but co-location; moving the backend instead was rejected because a Container Apps environment cannot change region and it would take the FQDN, `VITE_API_BASE_URL`, `CORS_ORIGIN`, a frontend rebuild and both deployed addresses with it. ~~**The backend-to-database round trip is therefore cross-region and unmeasured — Task 2.1.5 owns it.**~~ **Measured by Task 2.1.5 from the deployed replica: East US → North Central US is a 19.1–27.8 ms TCP round trip and a 79.2–111.3 ms TCP+TLS handshake.** **The brief's list of four irreversible decisions is wrong in both directions**: exactly one of the four is genuinely irreversible (**networking mode**), version is forward-only, region is irreversible in practice, and **tier is fully reversible** — while **storage type, backup redundancy and the data encryption key are irreversible and were not on the list at all**, and **authentication turned out to be the most reversible decision in the set**, since `--microsoft-entra-auth` and `--password-auth` are both arguments of `az postgres flexible-server update`. **Networking is public access with the `0.0.0.0` rule, characterised in Microsoft's own words** — it "configures the firewall to allow all connections from Azure, **including connections from the subscriptions of other customers**" — and **"allow this one IP" was disproved by measurement rather than citation**: the deployed backend reports **321 distinct outbound IPv4 addresses across 58 /16 prefixes**, identical across two readings three minutes apart, with the environment's `staticIp` not among them because it is the inbound address. A stable outbound IP and private access are the **same** project, re-creating the Container Apps environment, so it is the whole reversal-cost list spent on an empty database; two permanent consequences are recorded, that **every restore is public forever** and the server's own outbound access "can't be restricted". **Authentication is Microsoft Entra only with password auth `Disabled` and no admin user created at all, so the platform ends this story holding no secret** — which means **`EPIC.md`'s prediction that ADR 0011's "nothing deployed holds a credential" expires in this epic is wrong about the story: it expires in 2.6, not 2.1**, and the app's `secrets` array was re-read and is still `null`. Creating no admin user also disposes of a sixth irreversible decision, since `--admin-user` "once set, cannot be changed". **The token trap worth knowing before Task 2.1.4**: Azure's own managed-identity-for-Postgres page is written for a VM and sends you to `http://169.254.169.254/...`, which is **not** how a container app gets a token — Container Apps uses `IDENTITY_ENDPOINT` with an `X-IDENTITY-HEADER` — and a system-assigned identity's token is valid **up to 24 hours**. **What Story 2.6 inherits is not what Story 2.1 promised**: an Alpaca key is a bearer secret from a party with no Azure identity, so the path does not transfer unchanged; what transfers is the identity, and the recommended (not decided) shape is a Container App secret sourced from Key Vault and read by this same managed identity. **Storage is 32 GiB because the floor and the offer's ceiling are the same number**, so the real decision is **autogrow, which is `Disabled`** — the CLI's `--storage-size` default is **128**, four times the offer, and autogrow's smallest step is a 2× jump to 64 GiB at **$7.36/month** that cannot be undone. ~~**Usable capacity is ~27 GiB, not 32**~~ **Usable capacity is ~22.5 GiB (corrected by Task 2.1.5 — the disk holds 3.74 GiB of filesystem overhead when the database is empty, so free space starts at 27.46 GiB rather than 32)**, because read-only mode triggers at under 5 GiB free; the ingestion arithmetic is **~1.18 GB per year of minute bars** at an assumed ~120 bytes/row, which is ~~~24 years~~ **~20 years** of headroom or ~~~5~~ **~4** at five times the estimate, and it is a **prediction for Story 2.7 to measure** rather than a replacement for measuring. Backups are **7 days with geo-redundancy off**, because included backup storage is 100% of provisioned storage, retention is the one knob that moves freely both ways, and geo-redundancy is creation-only for data re-derivable from Alpaca. **The version is 18** (supported to 14-Nov-2030 against 17's 2029, GA on Azure for eleven months), and **the extension question was checked and does not constrain it** — `timescaledb` is 2.24.0 on 15, 16, 17 and 18 alike; one correction to the brief is that an in-place major upgrade carries **no fee**, its costs being downtime, a precheck that can block, and no automated revert. **The prerequisite that would have failed the first create was found before it ran**: **`Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL` is `NotRegistered`** — Task 1.11.4's exact failure — and **the same asymmetry reproduced**, since `checkNameAvailability` answered `"nameAvailable": true` from an unregistered provider. `az provider register` is Task 2.1.5's first command, recorded rather than run — **it was run there and took 85 s**. **The cost prediction is $0.00 inside the offer** (conditional on B1MS, 32 GiB and under 750 hours, which is unconditional at 744 hours in the longest month) and **$16.09/month outside it** — $12.41 compute, $3.68 storage, $0.00 backup, all re-read from the Retail Prices API — so once the offer expires around **2027-09-03** the totals are **$25.30 idle / $35.13 active** against a re-read **$20** budget with 50/80/100% alerts. **The recommendation is to leave the budget at $20**, because while the offer holds a database-attributable alert _is_ the signal that an offer condition broke, and a budget raised to accommodate that cost cannot report it. **And the cost question is still refused with its shape unchanged since Task 1.12.7** — `az consumption usage list` returns `[]` at exit 0 and the budget reports `currentSpend` `0.0` — but the environment is now **~30 hours** old against a documented 8–24 hour lag, so the lag no longer explains it and Task 2.1.8 inherits a sharper question. **Task 2.1.5 found that refusal in a THIRD shape**: two records now come back, for the workspace and the registry, with **every cost field the string `'None'`**. **Task 2.1.2 gave a clean clone a local database and it is the first thing in Epic 2 that ships code** — two new files, `compose.yaml` at the root and `scripts/local-database.mjs`, one new root script `pnpm db` (checked against `pnpm help -a` and free, with the detection validated in the same run against six known built-ins), **no dependency and no
