lockfile change at all**, and no `pnpm verify` step. The record is in `HOSTING.md` under _The database — the local development database_, beside Task 2.1.1's, because a second `DATABASE.md` is a copy waiting to disagree. **The mechanism is a Docker container and the cost is stated rather than waved through: Docker becomes a prerequisite it was not before** — Epic 1 needed it only for `pnpm image`, which nobody runs on a first day — and what softens it is that the prerequisite is **narrow**, since `pnpm install`, `pnpm verify`, `pnpm dev` and `pnpm e2e` all still run without it and the script reports its absence as that narrow thing. A **native install** is the standing alternative for anyone who cannot run Docker (cheaper at run time, worse at reproducibility, and it puts the engine version outside the repository's control the day after 2.1.1 pinned one); **pointing developers at the deployed database is recorded as rejected on principle**, because somebody will suggest it during the first hour Docker is broken. **The version pin is the whole point and it is the major only** — `postgres:18`, from `LOCAL_DATABASE.version` in one file and interpolated into the compose file, reporting **PostgreSQL 18.6 (Debian 18.6-1.pgdg13+2)** read out of the running container; the **minor** is deliberately unpinned because Azure patches it under us and a pin it cannot honour is a pin that lies. **Nothing checks that the local pin and the deployed `--version` still agree** — a stated invariant of the third kind, in both gap lists, and a check would need Azure credentials that `pnpm verify` deliberately does not have. **The credential deliberately does not match and that is not a wart**: the deployed server is Entra-only with password auth `Disabled`, which a laptop structurally cannot be, so "match the deployed environment" applies to the **engine version** and not to the credential — and this task **refuses to narrow Task 2.1.3's choice**, printing the connection as parts rather than a URL and adding nothing to `CONFIG_VARIABLES`, precisely so a single `DATABASE_URL` with a password inside it is not chosen here by accident. The password is a fixture in the repository on purpose, authenticating a container published on **loopback only** (`127.0.0.1:5432:5432`, not the bare form that puts a database on every network the machine is joined to) holding an **empty** database — no seeding, because one invented here is one Story 2.2 unpicks. **`pnpm dev` is unchanged and the database sits outside it**, because a database is a fourth process with a different lifecycle: starting it per `pnpm dev` means stopping it per Ctrl-C, which throws away the data you were mid-way through debugging. **`pnpm ready`'s third check is the first probe in that script that is not a `fetch`, and it could not have been one** — a Postgres port answers HTTP by waiting, so the two existing checks report `NO_RESPONSE` against a healthy database. **The stated decision is to speak the protocol rather than settle for a TCP connect**, and both cases that decide it were made to happen: an **SSLRequest** (eight bytes, no credentials, no driver, no dependency) gets `S` or `N` from any Postgres, where a bare `net.createServer()` reads **`NO_RESPONSE`** and an HTTP server on 5432 reads **`NOT_POSTGRES`** — a connect check would have called both of them up. Its limits are written down: it does **not** prove the named database exists or that the credentials work (both need a driver — Task 2.1.4's pool), and **not that the server is ours**, since a native Postgres on 5432 answers identically. What it does report that is genuinely useful is **`no TLS offered`**, the real local-versus-deployed difference, against a managed server where encryption is enforced. **The third check REPORTS and does not gate, with a named trigger**: it prints `○` rather than `✗` and the exit code is unchanged, because that exit code answers "can the application run" and nothing opens a connection yet — and because `pnpm e2e` gates on this very script, so a failing third check would refuse to start a browser suite with no interest in a database, on a laptop and on the runner alike. **The reversal trigger is a CONDITION rather than a task number, and correcting that is the one thing this task got wrong about itself**: the sentence it first shipped named "Task 2.1.4" and "the day the backend needs a database to serve a request" in the same breath, and those are not the same day — 2.1.4's own brief keeps `pnpm verify` passing with no database and `test:process` passing both ways, so after it nothing in either chain fails. The trigger is **the first check in `pnpm verify` or `pnpm e2e` that fails without a database**, which is Story 2.2's migrations or Story 2.8's routes; the shape is `report-error.ts`'s, where `CLAUDE.md` already records that a trigger was never a story number but a condition. On that day the `e2e` job gains a service. **It is also checked once with no polling, and that is not the shape of the other two**: they are polled to wait out a cold tree, where `pnpm db` passes `--wait` and does not return until the healthcheck says the server is accepting connections, so there is nothing to wait for — measured rather than argued, since a five-second poll took `pnpm ready` to **5.1 s** against a stopped database instead of **0.093 s**, five seconds added to every browser run. **Two things measured that no documentation page predicted, and the first was a prediction of this task's own that was wrong in the reassuring direction.** The **PostgreSQL 18 image moved its declared volume and its `PGDATA`** (`/var/lib/postgresql/18/docker`, `Config.Volumes` `{"/var/lib/postgresql":{}}`), so every pre-18 snippet's `/var/lib/postgresql/data` mount is wrong — and it does **not** start and silently persist nothing, it **refuses to start at all on the very first run with an empty volume**, exiting with a twenty-line message naming the mount, the reason and the fix. The good kind of trap, and with `--wait` a failed `pnpm db` rather than a database that looks fine until somebody needs yesterday's rows; persistence was then confirmed positively on the shipping mount, a row surviving `down` and `up`. And **a bare `docker compose up` refuses by construction** — every interpolated value is required with no default via `${VAR:?message}`, so the file cannot keep a second copy of the port, exiting **1** naming `pnpm db`; the first draft used `${VAR:-default}`, which is worse than it looks, because a blank port makes the publish spec `127.0.0.1::5432`, which is **valid** and binds a random one. That required-with-no-default arrangement is `e2e/playwright.config.ts`'s `E2E_BASE_URL` decision reused. **`pnpm verify` passes with no database running** — 23.08 s, criterion 8 met by the chain not changing at all — and the browser suite is unaffected, nine journeys green with the database stopped, which is the non-gating decision confirmed rather than assumed. **Task 2.1.3 put the connection settings through the configuration boundary, and its real work turned out to be the one-definition problem rather than the shape decision.** Seven variables — `DATABASE_HOST`, `DATABASE_PORT`, `DATABASE_NAME`, `DATABASE_USER`, `DATABASE_AUTH`, `DATABASE_PASSWORD`, `DATABASE_SSL` — take `CONFIG_VARIABLES` from five to **twelve**, with `apps/backend/.env.example` and `pnpm env:check` following; **no dependency, no lockfile change, no new `verify` step, and nothing opens a connection**. **The shape is discrete variables and not a `DATABASE_URL`, and the decision was taken away rather than taken**: Task 2.1.1 chose Entra-only authentication with password auth `Disabled` and no admin user, so the deployed password field is filled at connect time by code minting a token per connection and there is no string to put inside a URL. **`DATABASE_AUTH` names the mechanism rather than letting it be inferred**, which is what makes both silent failures the brief predicted into startup errors instead — a deployment that forgot the password variable falling through to the identity path, and a laptop with a stale one sending a password to a server that refuses them. **The two cross-variable checks are the first in this module**: a password set alongside `entra` is rejected (the two readings of it are opposite, and guessing produces an auth error nobody can attribute), and `DATABASE_SSL=disable` under `entra` is rejected because an access token is a bearer credential and that puts it on the wire in the clear. They go through the same accumulator as the readers, so a run with `PORT`, `DATABASE_PORT` and both cross-checks wrong reports **four lines**, seen. **The password's redaction is now asserted rather than trusted**: `readInt` and `readEnum` quote what the operator typed, which is right for a port and wrong for a credential, so the one message naming `DATABASE_PASSWORD` names the variable and never the value — and the test uses a value that is deliberately **not** the local fixture, because the fixture is public and a test written against it would pass while leaking a real one. **The strongest result is one the task predicted might be available and it is: `config.ts` never holds the deployed credential at all.** The Entra token does not come from `process.env`, so the module that promised not to log a credential turns out never to receive one — the deployed half of that promise belongs to Task 2.1.4's pool, and the rule stays as written because Story 2.6's Alpaca key genuinely will arrive through here. **The one-definition problem is closed in the direction the brief called almost certainly right, and the inverse is rejected in writing**: `scripts/local-database.mjs` is now a **reader** of the built `dist/config.js` — the `pair-addresses.mjs` arrangement applied to a third service — so `DATABASE_PORT=5433` in `apps/backend/.env` moves the published container port, the database name, the client and `pnpm ready`'s probe **in one edit**, watched happening (`127.0.0.1:5433->5432/tcp` and `✓ database 127.0.0.1:5433`). The direction is the right way round because `POSTGRES_USER`/`POSTGRES_PASSWORD`/`POSTGRES_DB` **create** a database while `DATABASE_*` **connect** to one; the inverse would put a shipped module that runs in production behind a development script that starts a container. **The cost is stated: `pnpm db` now needs a built tree**, which `pair-addresses.mjs` already accepts, and an unbuilt one gets `run \`pnpm build\` first` rather than a resolver stack. Two refusals fall out of it that no brief predicted and both are correct — **`pnpm db`under`DATABASE_AUTH=entra`refuses rather than inventing a password**, because a laptop has no identity to be, and an invalid`DATABASE\_*`value stops`pnpm db` with the same multi-line message the server itself prints, indented per line for Task 1.8.7's reason. **`DATABASE_PASSWORD`'s default is the local fixture and it moved out of the script into `.env.example`**, which is where every other default lives. The `Config` interface gained its **first nested value** (`database`, frozen separately — `Object.freeze`is shallow, and the outer assertion passes either way, so that is asserted rather than assumed) and its **first conditionally present key**:`password`is spread in under`password`mode and genuinely **absent** under`entra`, which is the `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`idiom this file's own interface comment named in advance.`pnpm env:check`was **made to fail all four ways** — a key in the code and not the example, a key in the example nothing reads, a drifted default (both`DATABASE_PORT`and`DATABASE_AUTH`), and a non-`VITE_`name in the frontend example — before reporting **12 backend variables documented**. Three of the seven new backend tests were **seen to fail** against deliberate breaks: a message that quotes the credential, a`password`assigned rather than spread, and an unfrozen nested object.`pnpm test`is **196** (37 + **56** + 103),`pnpm verify`passes at **27.9 s with no database running**, and`pnpm ready`answers the healthy triple in **0.44 s**. **Task 2.1.4 opened the connection, and the finding that shaped the whole task is that`pg`'s two most dangerous defaults are both absences rather than wrong values.** The driver is **`pg`8.23.0** plus`@types/pg`, measured from a fresh install against a 404-entry baseline: **+14 store entries, +832 KB, +116 lockfile lines**, `pnpm-workspace.yaml` md5-unchanged, and the install-script sweep still returns **`esbuild@0.28.2`and nothing else**, so Task 1.4.5 is still the only time`allowBuilds`has fired.`postgres`(porsager) is **markedly cheaper — +1 entry, +380 KB, +9 lockfile lines**, ships its own types, and was rejected on the same argument that took`@fastify/cors`over a hand-rolled hook and jsdom over happy-dom: it is one package doing what thirteen do, and the failure mode of a re-implementation is a divergence that presents as an application bug. **The hard functional criterion Task 2.1.1 set was checked before the cost and both candidates pass it** —`password`may be`() => string | Promise<string>`in both — and it was checked **empirically rather than from the types**, which is what turned it from a box-tick into a real finding: three concurrent queries against a cold pool of three produce **three** credential calls, and three more against the warm pool produce **none**. So the credential is minted **per connection**, which is exactly the shape an Entra token needs and is not what "per pool" or "per query" would give. **The two defaults are the headline.**`pg.Pool`is an`EventEmitter`, and **an `EventEmitter`with no`error`listener throws** — so without`pool.on("error")`a dropped idle connection becomes an`uncaughtException`, which `index.ts`turns into a level-60 record and`process.exit(1)`, which on a platform whose liveness probe restarts the replica is **a crash-loop caused by a Postgres restart we had nothing to do with**. Produced rather than reasoned about, by terminating the process's own backend from a second connection: with no handler the process died on `terminating connection due to administrator command`; with one, the message is logged at `warn`, the dead client is discarded and **the next query succeeds**. And **`connectionTimeoutMillis`defaults to 0, meaning wait forever** — measured against a`net.createServer()`that accepts and never answers, where`pool.query()`was still pending after four seconds; at 5,000 ms it fails in 2,005 ms saying`Connection terminated due to connection timeout`. That is the third door into the same trap this repository has already met twice in `check-ready.mjs`, and it is worse inside a startup path than inside a check script. **The pool is created by `index.ts`and deliberately does NOT enter`buildServer()`**: nothing serves data yet, so putting it in `ServerOptions`would be a dependency declared for a route that does not exist and every test building a server would have to supply or fake one — the reversal trigger is Story 2.8's first route that needs data, at which point ADR 0002 §3's warning about the first`await` in the factory applies. **`new Pool()`is lazy and opens no socket**, asserted by reading`totalCount`, and that is precisely what lets `database.test.ts`sit in the **fast** suite: eleven tests about configuration, no build and no socket, so Story 1.9's property survives. **The probe runs once, after`listen()`, and never stops the process.** After rather than before, so a slow or absent database cannot delay the socket the platform's startup probe is waiting on; `warn`and not`error`, because the server is still healthy by `/health`'s own definition and Task 1.7.4 reserves 50 for a failure this server produced; and never an exit, because that is the crash-loop again — and Task 2.1.1 recorded that a Burstable server can make **itself** unreachable by exhausting its CPU credits, so this is a state the database can enter on its own under Story 2.7's backfill. **The pool closes AFTER `app.close()`resolves and INSIDE the ceiling**, and proving that ordering produced the task's most transferable lesson. **An ordering assertion needs a marker on each side of the step it is about, and the marker has to travel with the step.** The first version bounded the close by`signal received`and`shutdown complete`and **stayed green** when the close was moved to the wrong side of`app.close()`, because the whole drain happens between those two; a second `debug`record at the moment the HTTP side finishes fixed that — and then the break **passed again**, because the record was a separate statement further down that did not move with the close. Only when`app.log.debug("database pool closed")`was put immediately after`closeDatabasePool()` did the break fail (`expected 5 to be greater than 6`). Those two lines are the first records this application has ever emitted **below `info`**, which fills the half of `LOG_LEVEL`Task 1.7.1 recorded as empty, and at`info`they print nothing.`pool.end()`**does** wait for a checked-out client — measured,`end()` had not resolved 300 ms in and resolved 311.3 ms after it was called, the instant the client was released — so a route holding one is a slow shutdown the 5-second ceiling turns into a level-50 record. With an idle pool the drain is **0–1 ms in-process and 25–30 ms wall across five runs**, against the recorded ~100 ms baseline, so the pool costs the shutdown nothing measurable. **`test:process`is 14 tests and passes both ways with the same count and no`skipIf`** — a skipped test reports green, which this repository has already recorded twice as the worst failure available — so the one test that cares whether a database exists **asks the question itself** (the same eight-byte SSLRequest `check-ready.mjs` sends) and asserts the matching answer, making it a real assertion in both environments. **Eight deliberate breaks, each reverted**, and break 7 is the one worth remembering because it took three attempts to make it fail. **`pnpm ready`'s third check was re-taken and the answer is unchanged: it stays reporting.** Task 2.1.2's stated condition — *the first check in `pnpm verify`or`pnpm e2e`that fails without a database* — **did not fire**, confirmed by measurement rather than by reading the code:`pnpm verify`is **exit 0 with no database** and the browser suite is **9 passed in 3.4 s** with one stopped, so the`e2e`job in`verify.yml`still needs no Postgres service. **What a request that needs data gets is decided and written down rather than built**, because there is no route to give it to: a **503** (not a 500 — a dependency being unavailable is a different instruction from this server having failed) carrying a new`SERVICE_UNAVAILABLE`code, added by the story that can produce it, per`API_ERROR_CODES`' own rule that a member is added when a failure can be produced. `pnpm test`is **207** (37 + **67** + 103),`pnpm verify`is **exit 0 in 25.2 s with no database and 25.8 s with one**, and the frontend artefact reproduces Task 1.13.4's four files to the byte — 348,135 B`b98aeaa5…`, 12,128 B `134d5dd8…`, 1,101 B `07983678…`, 300 B, **361,664 B** — which is the check rather than a coincidence, because this task shipped no frontend source. **Task 2.1.5 provisioned the managed instance, and the headline is that the region decision was taken away for a SECOND time — one day after Task 2.1.1 took it.** East US 2 is now `OfferRestricted` for this subscription (`"Subscriptions are restricted from provisioning in this region"`, a different sentence from East US's `"Provisioning is restricted in this region"`), so the database is in **North Central US**, settled with the user because region is irreversible in practice. All three price meters there are **identical to East US 2** — `$0.017`/hr compute, `$0.115`/GB storage, `$0.095`/GB backup — and it is the closest unrestricted region to the East US backend; Central US is +13% compute and +14% backup, West US +29%, **so 2.1.1's "the three price meters are identical in both regions" is true of that pair and false as a general claim**. **This subscription now spans three regions and chose none of them.** **The finding that would have cost the most is an error message describing the tool rather than the platform**: `az postgres flexible-server create`**cannot create an Entra-only server**, failing its own documented flag combination with`MissingRequiredParameter: 'AdministratorLoginPassword'`— and the identical body sent to ARM as a`PUT`was **accepted**, with the created server reading back`administratorLogin: null`. A **CLI defect, not a platform requirement**; believing it would have bought the immutable `--admin-user`that 2.1.1's authentication choice had disposed of. **Create this server through ARM.** **The TLS question Task 2.1.4 handed over is answered and nothing ships**:`verify-full`—`{ rejectUnauthorized: true }`with **no`ca`** — connects, verified from the laptop *and from inside the deployed East US container*, because the chain runs `… <- Microsoft TLS RSA Root G2 <- DigiCert Global Root G2`and Node 24.20.0's **118 bundled roots** hold that root **plus both Microsoft 2017 roots** Azure's published migration moves toward. **So no CA file goes into`apps/backend/Dockerfile`**, and `verify-ca` stays out of the vocabulary as Task 2.1.3 asked. It was **made to fail before being believed**: an unrelated CA is refused (`self-signed certificate in certificate chain`) and dialling by IP is refused (`Hostname/IP does not match certificate's altnames`), so chain **and** host name are both checked. **Both TLS-unavailable directions are immediate named refusals rather than hangs** — 1.1–3.6 ms locally (`The server does not support SSL connections`) and 538 ms against the managed server (`no pg_hba.conf entry … no encryption`, SQLSTATE `28000`), the latter also proving the server **requires** encryption rather than merely offering it. **The connection ceiling was confirmed and came out one higher than documented**: `max_connections` 50 minus 10 superuser-reserved minus 5 reserved gives 35, and **36 opened before the 37th was refused** (`53300`) — design against 35, and note that **Azure's own sessions already hold 7–10** of them, so `POOL_MAX: 10`sits at ~12 of ~25 genuinely free slots. **The 5-second`connectionTimeoutMillis`is generous rather than tight**: the worst laptop connect (1194–1460 ms, against a warm`SELECT 1`of 224–521 ms) used **29.2%** of it, and from the container a full connect is ~150–250 ms, under 5% — **which is the figure Task 2.1.7 was promised**, since a pooled`/health`check pays ~23 ms and one causing a new connection pays ~150–250 ms. **Two of 2.1.1's figures were corrected by measurement**: usable storage is **~22.5 GiB rather than ~27**, because an empty server already reads`storage_used` **3.740 GiB** of filesystem overhead; and **`storage.iops`is 120, not the 640`list-skus`advertises** for`Standard_B1ms`, the SKU's ceiling and the P4 disk's entitlement being different numbers Story 2.7 should size against. **An idle Burstable server banks almost nothing**: `cpu_credits_remaining`sits at the **30** cap while`cpu_percent`reads 10.5–12.1% against a 10% baseline, so a backfill starts with roughly what it can earn rather than a reservoir. **Three things nobody had owned now exist** — this subscription had **no action groups and no metric alerts at all** —`ag-marketpulse-ops`, `psql-storage-80pct`(which is what replaces autogrow) and`psql-cpu-credits-low`; plus a **`CanNotDelete`lock** whose argument is not the data (all re-derivable from Alpaca) but that deletion destroys the backups **and** the`pgaadauth_create_principal`bootstrap that exists in no file here. Proven live on a recoverable child resource:`ScopeLocked`, and it **inherits** — `create`and`update`still work, any delete needs the lock lifted first. **Two traps to carry**: the Entra administrator's 65-character UPN is **silently truncated to 63** by Postgres's`NAMEDATALEN`, losing its final `om`, though connecting with the full UPN still works; and **`pnpm db exec`echoes its arguments**, so passing a token that way printed a live bearer credential into the terminal — use`docker exec -e`, and Task 2.1.6's leak check should count terminal echo as a place a credential lands. The guest (`#EXT#`) administrator risk 2.1.1 flagged **did not materialise** — it connected and ran `pgaadauth_create_principal`first try — and **the local and deployed versions match at 18.6**, which is the unchecked version invariant confirmed by hand for the first time. **No application code, no Container App environment variable and no credential in this repository changed.** **Task 2.1.6 put the credential on the platform and the strongest available outcome held: there is no credential on the platform.** The deployed backend executes`SELECT 1` against the managed database over TLS as its own system-assigned managed identity, and the app's **`secrets`array is still`null`, read back after the change** — so ADR 0011's "nothing deployed holds a credential" is **confirmed by this task rather than falsified by it**, and it expires in Story 2.6, which will be putting a secret on the platform for the first time rather than repeating something proven here. The code change is **one function body**: Task 2.1.4's seam held, `createDatabasePool`, its callers and `index.ts` are untouched, and the only signature that moved is a private helper taking the logger it needed. **`apps/backend/src/entra-token.ts`is the second file in the workspace that reads`process.env`, and it exists to protect the invariant rather than to break it** — `IDENTITY_HEADER`is itself a bearer credential, so routing it through`config.ts`would put one on the frozen`Config`object in the module whose own comment records that its no-credential guarantee is **structural**; neither identity variable is in`CONFIG_VARIABLES`or`.env.example`, because `env:check` would then demand they be documented as ours. **`@azure/identity`was measured before being declined at 32 packages and 46 MB** for one HTTP GET with one header and no cryptography — the opposite of the`@fastify/cors`decision, and for its stated reason: a hand-rolled CORS fails silently and dangerously where a hand-rolled`fetch`of a documented URL fails loudly at the first connection. **The trap`HOSTING.md`recorded is confirmed from inside the replica rather than cited**:`IDENTITY_ENDPOINT` reads **`http://localhost:12356/…`**, a local sidecar, and `169.254.169.254` — the virtual-machine recipe every Azure page for this offers — does not appear in it at all; the cost of copying it would have been a _hang_ inside connection establishment rather than an error. **The caching question was settled by measurement in the direction of no cache**: six consecutive calls from the replica read **461 / 19 / 76 / 5 / 94 / 5 ms** with an **identical `expires_on`**, so the platform's sidecar already caches, at **86,549 s — 24.0 hours**, which is Task 2.1.1's figure confirmed. What the application actually pays is the **first** call of a replica's life, **866 / 889 / 887 ms** across three cold starts — **85% of the 1,023 ms first connection**, and 29% of `entra-token.ts`'s 3-second deadline. **That deadline has to stay strictly below the pool's 5-second `connectionTimeoutMillis`** or a hung metadata endpoint is reported as a generic connection timeout and never names itself, which is the frontend's `API_TIMEOUT_MS`/`HEALTH_POLL_INTERVAL_MS` shape a third time — and it gets the same treatment, a **test**, because two constants in two files are invisible to `pnpm verify`. **The control was worth taking**: the six variables set against the _old_ image produced a replica that started, served `/health` 200, and wrote Task 2.1.4's "not implemented" message at level 40 **134 ms after listening** — the seam behaving on the platform exactly as on a laptop. **Observed at both ends**, and the second end had to be **raced**: `pg_stat_activity` shows `application_name` and `usename` both `marketpulse-backend` on `TLSv1.3` / `TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384` from `40.121.18.106`, visible for **exactly ten seconds** and then gone, because `pg`'s default `idleTimeoutMillis` is 10 s and **nothing queries the database after the startup probe** — so the deployed backend holds **zero** connections at rest, which is a fact Story 2.8 and Epic 3 should know before assuming a warm pool. **The leak check was produced rather than read and is clean in all four places.** Five failure classes were made to happen against the real server and every error read **whole** rather than by its message — malformed token (`28000`), **wrong audience** (`28000`, and Azure's message names the resource to acquire), a role that does not exist (`28P01`), a wrong host (`ENOTFOUND`), and a credential function that throws — and **not one carries the credential**, extending Task 2.1.4's local `pg` finding to the case worth far more. The repository holds two `eyJ`-shaped strings and **both are deliberate test fixtures** that exist so the leak assertions have something to fail against, exactly as the `marketpulse` password is; `apps/frontend/dist` and `storybook-static` are **zero** for every needle; and **Log Analytics is zero** for `eyJ`, `access_token`, `IDENTITY_HEADER`, `Bearer `, `ossrdbms` and the claim names `upn`/`oid`/`tid`, across the healthy revision, the degraded one and both restarts. One place a source grep would have got wrong: `apps/backend/dist` contains `eyJ` **four times**, entirely in the compiled test files `tsc -b` emits there, and **the shipping image contains zero**, because `files: ["dist", "!dist/**/*.test.*"]` keeps them out of `pnpm deploy`. **And terminal echo is now counted as a fourth place**, which is Task 2.1.5's own leak turned into a rule: every operator query here used `docker exec -e PGPASSWORD` with **no `=value`**, so the token came from the environment rather than a command line, and the in-container probe printed a token's _length_. **An unreachable database does not kill the replica, watched rather than argued**: pointed at `203.0.113.7` (RFC 5737, guaranteed unroutable, so a genuine packet-drop timeout), the replica logged `database unreachable, continuing without it` at level 40 and then held `ready: true`, `restartCount: 0` and `/health` **200 on every poll** for **3 min 30 s** — seven liveness intervals and twenty-one readiness intervals — with `uptimeSeconds` rising 128 → 217 and **never resetting**. There is **no token-mint record at all** on that revision, which is an ordering fact worth keeping: `pg` calls the credential function only after the socket is up, so an unreachable database costs no token and a token-endpoint outage is distinguishable from a database outage in the log. **The lever is not the firewall the brief named** — that command was refused by this environment's permission policy — and the app-scoped substitute is arguably better, since it cannot affect any other consumer of the database; what it does not exercise is the firewall path, stated rather than implied. **"Rotation" is the wrong word and what replaces it is written down**: nothing is stored, so nothing rotates; the token is re-minted per connection, `IDENTITY_HEADER` is rotated by the **platform** and is therefore read fresh on every acquisition rather than cached at module load; revocation is `DROP ROLE marketpulse-backend` or removing the identity, effective at the next connection. **Whether an open connection outlives its own token was NOT verified and saying so is the answer**: a token lasts 24 hours and `pg` closes an idle client after 10 seconds, so the case is **structurally unreachable** in this configuration rather than untested through neglect, and Epic 3's long-lived writer is the thing that makes it reachable. Two smaller findings. **An operator's own Entra token cannot authenticate as `marketpulse-backend`** — _"Service principals cannot generate AAD_AUTH_TOKENTYPE_APP_USER tokens for role"_ — because `pgaadauth_create_principal(..., false, false)` created a service-principal role, so a leaked operator credential cannot impersonate the backend and vice versa. And **`libpq` cannot do `verify-full` where Node can**: the local Postgres container has no CA store, so `psql` there refuses it, while the backend's Node image verifies the identical certificate with nothing shipped — Task 2.1.5's "no CA file in the Dockerfile" is a property of the **runtime** rather than of the certificate, and would not transfer to a `libpq` client. `pnpm test` is **218** (37 + **78** + 103), `pnpm verify` is exit 0 in **25.7–27.3 s** across two readings with no database, and **four deliberate breaks were each seen to fail and reverted**: echoing the response body on a non-200, the virtual-machine `Metadata` header, the token deadline above the connection deadline, and the token in the debug record. **Task 2.1.7 answered the story's fourth and last open decision, and the answer is no: `/health` says nothing about the database and is unchanged BYTE FOR BYTE — 61 bytes, three fields, same schema, same `satisfies` guard, same `HealthResponse`, same `isHealthResponse()`, same `BackendStatus`.** Reachability is `GET /diagnostics/database`, a new second file in `apps/backend/src/routes/` — the directory Task 1.2.3 created for a second route that took eleven stories to arrive. **All three probes were read off the live app rather than recalled and they all point at `/health`**: startup at 2 s period / 3 s timeout / 30 failures, readiness at 10 s / 5 s / 3, liveness at 30 s / 5 s / 3. **Shape 1 — a field on `/health` the probes do not fail on — was rejected on measured cost rather than principle**: the pool holds zero connections at rest, so a check there pays the **cold** path nearly every time at **~1,023 ms deployed of which 866 ms is the Entra token mint**, a third of the startup probe's 3-second timeout, on a route hit every 2 s at startup, every 10 s by readiness, every 30 s by liveness and once per 30 s per **visible browser tab** — and it would widen a contract with five readers for a field with no consumer. **Shape 2, the readiness surface the story itself guessed at, is refused for a DIFFERENT reason and that is the finding**: this app is `Single` revision mode at `minReplicas: 1`, so an unready replica is not a degraded service, it is **no** service, and a readiness probe failing on a database none of the current routes uses would take a working application entirely off the air. **Shape 3 — nothing at all — was rejected on a gap that is real rather than anticipated**: reachability is reported in exactly one place today, the level-40 record `index.ts` writes at **startup**, so a running replica whose database goes away afterwards reports nothing anywhere and cannot, because nothing queries it once the startup connection has aged out; and a laptop cannot answer the question either, since it tests a laptop's network and its own credential rather than the East US replica to North Central US path as `marketpulse-backend` with a sidecar-minted token. **The endpoint is the only instrument standing in that position, which is Story 1.12's lesson arriving from the other side.** **The bound is one query per 5 s and one in-flight query whatever the caller count, and it was measured rather than argued**: 300 sequential requests over 1.29 s produced **1** database query, **25 concurrent requests on a cold cache produced 1**, and 2,853 requests over 60 s of continuous polling produced **12 — exactly 60 divided by 5**. Single flight matters more than the TTL, because without it 25 simultaneous requests to a public unauthenticated endpoint on a cold pool are 25 connections and 25 token mints at 866 ms each, against 35 usable connections of which Azure's own sessions hold 7–10 with no PgBouncer. **The counter-intuitive half is that a SHORTER bound is CHEAPER**: cost steps at `POOL_IDLE_TIMEOUT_MS` rather than scaling with frequency — below it a check is a pooled ~23 ms round trip minting no token, at or above it every check opens a connection and mints one — so 10 s under sustained polling would cost 6 cold connections and 6 mints a minute where 5 s costs 12 warm round trips and one. `POOL_IDLE_TIMEOUT_MS` now **restates pg's own default explicitly** so a minor version cannot move it underneath that, and a **test asserts the ordering**, which is the third coupled pair `pnpm verify` cannot see after `API_TIMEOUT_MS`/`HEALTH_POLL_INTERVAL_MS` and `TOKEN_TIMEOUT_MS`/`CONNECT_TIMEOUT_MS`. The price is one connection held while polled and **zero at rest**, read straight out of `pg_stat_activity` — `1|idle` on three consecutive readings under 1 req/s and **0** twelve seconds after polling stopped. **There is deliberately no background timer**, because that is uptime monitoring, which Task 1.13.5 already declined, and it would be standing billable traffic against the Consumption plan's idle condition to answer a question nobody is asking; **a failure is cached exactly as a success is**, or an unreachable database becomes an _unbounded_ query rate at the moment the ceiling matters most. **Produced locally against a genuinely unreachable database** (`pnpm db down`): the endpoint reported `reachable: false` at **200** in 1–3 ms because a refused connection is immediate, `/health` answered 200 throughout with `uptimeSeconds` rising 214 to 354 and **never resetting**, `pnpm ready` printed its non-gating `○ database … ECONNREFUSED` at **exit 0**, and recovery was watched rather than inferred with the cache visibly ageing 0 → 2016 → 4042 → 0 across the 5-second boundary. **The correlation id was followed from the wire to the reason** — a body saying `reachable: false`, an `x-request-id` header, and a level-40 record carrying `connect ECONNREFUSED` under that same id — which is the whole mechanism the body's silence rests on: the endpoint says **whether**, the log says **why**, and Task 1.7.2's id makes that one investigation rather than two. **`BackendStatus` does not move, stated as a decision and watched in a browser**: with the database fully down the `Backend service` region read **`healthy`**, there were 0 error fallbacks and 4 navigation links, and the page made only `/health` requests. That is correct — `degraded` means _something answered and it was not a readable health report_, and a backend whose database is down is answering perfectly about itself, while nothing a user can do today needs the database. The reversal trigger is Story 2.8's first route that serves data, and consequently **no `e2e/specs-deployed/` assertion was added**, because there is nothing new for a browser to see. **Two things it found that the plan did not anticipate.** **The leak assertion is a check on the SCHEMA rather than on the handler, and it took two edits to make it fail**: adding the error message to the body left the test **green**, because `fast-json-stringify` strips a property the schema does not declare — Task 1.7.3's mechanism measured again on a new route — and it only went red once the field was declared in the schema too, so **a green run there is not evidence that a handler could not leak**. And **Fastify's `exposeHeadRoutes` default registers a HEAD route beside every GET**, so the route-table walk saw two entries for one declaration, which is the same class of surprise as the `OPTIONS *` preflight route `server.test.ts` found on its own first run. `buildServer()` is untouched and so is every test that calls it: the route is registered from `index.ts` because the pool takes `app.log`, so the app precedes the pool and the pool precedes the route — which means **`database.ts`'s recorded reversal trigger is deliberately NOT fired** and stays Story 2.8's, at the stated cost that `server.test.ts`'s route-table walk cannot see this route and its `500: apiErrorSchema` is asserted in its own test instead. `pnpm test` is **229** (37 + **89** + 103), `pnpm verify` is **exit 0 in 25.68 s with no database** and exit 0 with one, `pnpm e2e` is 10 passed with a database and 9 in 3.4 s with none, and the frontend artefact is untouched. **And the honest gap: every `az containerapp update` was refused by this environment's own permission policy**, in both the `--set` and `--set-env-vars` forms — one command wider than the firewall refusal Task 2.1.6 hit — so **the endpoint has never run deployed** and the deployed `DATABASE_HOST=203.0.113.7` break was not re-taken; both are ordinary observation once merged, and Task 2.1.8 owns them. **The readiness-probe 503 behind shape 2's rejection is reasoned rather than produced, and it is NOT handed forward** — it was designed and then dropped, because producing it costs a live outage to confirm a property already readable from `az containerapp show` (revision mode, replica count and the probe table, all three read off the live app) in support of a shape being _rejected_ rather than shipped. **A rejection does not carry the same evidentiary bar as something that ships**, which is the same call Task 1.13.3 made in declining a render-failure journey. What makes that tolerable is precisely that `/health` is unchanged, so Task 2.1.6's measured control — an unreachable deployed database leaving `restartCount: 0` and `/health` 200 across seven liveness intervals — still describes the deployed system exactly.

**Story 2.2 (Database Schema & Migration Mechanism) has started. Task 2.2.1 is complete: it chose the migration tool and the query layer, installed nothing permanent, and finished with the tree byte-identical.** The record is `planning/epic-02-security-universe-historical-data/story-02-.../DATA-LAYER.md`, which is to Story 2.2 what `BROWSER-TESTING.md` is to Story 1.13 and `HOSTING.md` is to Story 1.11. **The migrator is Kysely's `Migrator` driving plain SQL files through a ~15-line provider we own, the query layer is Kysely too, and `pg` and `apps/backend/src/database.ts` survive unchanged** — `PostgresDialect` takes our existing pool, so Task 2.1.4's per-connection credential measurement was **re-taken through Kysely rather than assumed to transfer** and reproduces exactly (3 concurrent on a cold pool of 3 → **3** credential calls, 3 more warm → **0**). **Nothing is generated, no build step is added and `pnpm verify` is untouched**, which was checked _before_ any candidate was liked, because `kysely-codegen`, `drizzle-kit pull/push` and `prisma migrate dev` all introspect a **live** database and criterion 7 says `pnpm verify` runs without one. **The costs, from a fresh install against a re-taken baseline of 418 store entries / 291,912 KB / 4,757 lockfile lines** (not Task 1.13.6's 404 — Story 2.1 added `pg`): **`kysely` is +1 entry, +3,444 KB, +9 lockfile lines and declares no dependencies at all**; `node-pg-migrate` is +28 / +22,576 KB / +124; `drizzle-orm` + `drizzle-kit` is +18 / +52,500 KB / +670; and **Prisma is +129 / +308,588 KB / +992 and does not install at all**. **Three findings the numbers alone do not carry.** `node-pg-migrate` **reproduces Task 1.13.1's Cypress shape exactly through a different package** — 13 of its 28 entries are second copies of this workspace's own toolchain (two ESLints, two Vites, two Vitests), re-keyed by `jiti`, which is a hard dependency and arrives even if every migration is a `.sql` file. **Drizzle Kit brings two more esbuild majors (0.18.20 and 0.25.12) whose postinstalls RAN and `allowBuilds` did not fire** — because **the allowlist names a package, not a version**, so this repository's recorded "the sweep returns one line" is a property of what is installed rather than of the policy, and one entry can admit any number of install scripts. And **Prisma reproduced Cypress's documented failure mode a second time**: `[ERR_PNPM_IGNORED_BUILDS]` at **exit 1** with pnpm rewriting the tracked `pnpm-workspace.yaml` with its invalid stub, while dragging in `mysql2`, a **WASM Postgres** (`@electric-sql/pglite`, 23.7 MB), **`@prisma/studio-core` at 43.2 MB with `@radix-ui/*`, `@visx/*` and eleven `d3-*` packages** — a React GUI behind `apps/backend` — plus a _second_ Postgres driver and `valibot`, the library Story 1.6 threw away; and its measured size is an **under**-estimate, because `@prisma/engines` reads 112 KB precisely because the postinstall that downloads the engines never ran. **The partial-migration question was produced rather than read, and the headline is that it separates NONE of the three libraries** — all of them roll the whole file back with an empty bookkeeping table, because **Postgres has transactional DDL** and all three put the bookkeeping row inside the same transaction. So the decision rests on the failure modes it _does_ separate. **Kysely returns `{ error, results }` and the process exits 0**, so a runner that does not read `error` and exit non-zero itself is a green migration step that applied nothing — the sharpest thing handed to Task 2.2.2, and it must be made to fail once and seen to do it. **`CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` is the sharper version of the question and it is the one comparison node-pg-migrate wins**: it fails outright under a transaction, and the escape hatch is `pgm.noTransaction()` **per migration** there against `disableTransactions` **per `Migrator`** in Kysely — accepted, with a second `Migrator` over a separate directory named as the answer and **Story 2.7's `market_bars` indexes** named as the trigger. What Kysely's migrator does catch, each produced: **a migration inserted out of order** and **a previously applied migration deleted from the tree** both fail by name as `corrupted migrations: …`, a re-run is `[]`, and a `kysely_migration_lock` table handles two migrators at once. **The one thing it does not do is checksum** — `kysely_migration` is `(name, timestamp)`, read out of `information_schema` — so a migration file **edited after it was applied** is skipped silently; handed to **Task 2.2.5** with `information_schema` named as the mechanism. **A hand-rolled runner was written both ways and that is what settled which precedent applies.** The correct one is ~30 lines and needs an advisory lock, a tracking table, deterministic ordering, a checksum, the change and its bookkeeping row in **one** transaction, and a non-zero exit. The **plausible** one differs only by moving the bookkeeping row outside the transaction, and it prints `applied 0002_partial.sql` at **exit 0** while recording a migration whose tables do not exist — permanent divergence, a later run a no-op, and nothing reporting a problem. **So a migration runner is the `@fastify/cors` case and not the `react-error-boundary` case**, and at +1 entry with zero dependencies it is not close. **The query-layer decision rests on one thing and it is architectural: the temporal seam was written for two candidates and only one of them is structural.** `pg` with hand-written SQL gives a module that owns the pool and never exports it — and the leak is one `pool.query` away, **produced**: `around the seam: 100,200 <-- LEAKED`. Kysely exposes the query AST to a plugin, so a call site asking for **no time filter at all** compiles to `select "close" from "market_bars" where "symbol" = $1 and "observed_at" <= $2` while a non-temporal query is untouched — and because the plugin is attached with `withPlugin`, the isolated handle and the raw one are different objects, so **the unplugged handle simply is not exported** (an instruction to Task 2.2.2, and the whole mechanism). **The hole is raw `` sql`…` ``, which reaches the plugin as an opaque `RawNode` it cannot rewrite — but it does reach it, so it can be REFUSED**, produced: `refused: raw SQL is not permitted under temporal isolation`. Rewrite what it can, refuse what it cannot, which is what invariant 4's "structurally impossible" asks for. **Drizzle has no equivalent hook**, and **PostgreSQL row-level security is recorded as the fallback nobody chose** — genuinely structural, database-side, and the only mechanism that would survive the query layer being replaced, so Epic 13 should read that paragraph before assuming the plugin is the only option. **The cost of Kysely as the query layer is stated rather than discovered: its `Database` interface is hand-written and nothing checks it against the schema**, a new gap of this repository's third kind, owned by Task 2.2.5 against `information_schema`. **Migrations are forward-only and there is no `down`** — a `down` that has never been executed is a claim rather than a mechanism, and reversing a migration that dropped a column with data in it cannot be written at all; it is cheap to decide now because the reversal for a mistake in this story is a `DROP`, which stops being true in Story 2.3. Kysely makes `down` optional, checked rather than assumed. **The reversal trigger is not "a migration we regret"** — that is always a new forward migration — it is a **deployment** that must roll schema and code back together, which is Task 2.2.7's. **Migrations are SQL files rather than TypeScript**, because a `.sql` file is reviewable as the thing that will run where a TypeScript migration compiles into `dist/` and the artefact reviewed is not the artefact executed — the shape this repository already refused in declining a workflow that builds the site on the deploy side; and the assumption that design rests on was **verified**, since a multi-statement body executes through `sql.raw()` inside the transaction. The tree finished byte-identical — 418 entries, 291,912 KB, a `diff`-empty lockfile and `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, the sweep still returning `esbuild@0.28.2` and nothing else, `pnpm verify` at **exit 0** — and the local database was reset to an empty `public` schema.

The pnpm workspace root, the shared TypeScript baseline and all three workspace packages exist, verified from a clean clone with an empty pnpm store (Tasks 1.1.8 and 1.3.5).

**Both apps now run.** `apps/backend` is a Fastify server — it starts on a configurable port, serves `GET /health`, restarts on source change, and shuts down gracefully on `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT`. `apps/frontend` is a React 19 application built with Vite — it renders a placeholder shell, hot-reloads components without losing their state, and builds to static assets that render from a plain static host. Root `pnpm dev` starts the pair, and there are no placeholder `dev` scripts left anywhere. **Story 1.8 closed that loop and wrote `docs/adr/0008-*`.** The pair is now legible in one terminal (a request is 2 rendered lines rather than 12), the browser boundary is real server-side CORS with one origin from configuration rather than a Vite proxy, the frontend's ports stay literals as a decision, `pnpm ready` answers whether the pair is actually up — which matters because a busy 3000 leaves `pnpm dev` running and looking healthy — and `README.md` was followed from a clean clone to a running application. Story 1.8 came to **one** new dependency, `@fastify/cors`, and **no change to the frontend artefact at all**: 271 modules / 343,658 B / 10,926 B / three files, unchanged since Task 1.7.7.

**Both are skeletons in scope, not in status.** The backend has no market data, no database and no domain logic — it **does** have CORS since Task 1.8.3, one allowed origin from configuration — it does now have a configuration module (Story 1.6) and a configured logger with a correlation id, and a wire contract for errors (Task 1.7.1 — level and rendering; Task 1.7.2 — the request record, the id and its `x-request-id` header; Task 1.7.3 — the `ApiError` shape in `packages/shared` and a response schema on `/health`; Task 1.7.4 — both error handlers, so every failure the server produces now answers in that shape; Task 1.7.5 — the two process-level crash handlers, which is the last backend task in the story). **Task 1.7.6 built the frontend half** — three error boundaries, a fallback component, and the three `createRoot` reporting options — and **Task 1.7.7 closed the story with `docs/adr/0007-*`**, re-running all seven criteria and re-taking every figure from a clean tree rather than inheriting them. Story 1.7 came to **one** new dependency across both halves of the stack (`pino-pretty`); the error contract, both handlers, both response schemas and all three boundaries cost zero packages. The frontend now has **routes and chrome** — Task 1.5.1 chose React Router 8.3.1, Task 1.5.2 mounted it (four routes for PRODUCT_SPEC.md §8's four experiences — `/`, `/investigations`, `/securities`, `/replay` — plus a not-found route), and **Task 1.5.3 replaced the temporary navigation strip with the real chrome**: `components/AppHeader`, rendered once outside `<Routes>`, carrying the product name, a market feed region, a reserved market clock region and the navigation. **Task 1.5.4 put PRODUCT_SPEC.md §9's regions under it** — four named landmarks on the landing route (market topology, unusual activity, market breadth, current investigations) in a 3:1 by 2:1 grid, with the three other routes deliberately a single area. **Task 1.5.5 added no source at all**: it measured deep-linking against a host with no fallback, handed that criterion to Story 1.11 with its constraints, and rejected route splitting on the artefact built both ways — so the frontend's shape is final for this story at three files. **Task 1.5.6 closed the story from a clean tree and wrote `docs/adr/0005-*`**, reproducing every figure and re-running every criterion rather than inheriting them; Story 1.5 came to +72 modules, +41.99 kB of JavaScript and +2.61 kB of CSS in total, still three files, one new dependency, and still exactly one file importing `@base-ui/react`. It has no state management and ~~does not call the backend (Story 1.12)~~ ~~**makes exactly one call to the backend since Task 1.11.5**~~ — **polls it, since Task 1.12.3**: `useBackendHealth()` is called from `App.tsx` and asks `/health` every 30 seconds through `src/api-client.ts`, which is still the **only** file in the application that calls `fetch`. It is the first stateful code in this repository. ~~The result is still rendered nowhere~~ — **since Task 1.12.5 the result is on screen on all five routes**, as a third region in the chrome's status strip beside the market feed and the reserved clock, and it is the first thing in this application whose rendering depends on a network call. Since Task 1.7.6 it also contains a render failure to the box it happened in, which is the first behaviour in this application that is not a static render. Its styling system is **decided, piped, designed at the token level, carrying the product's own vocabulary, and now built into components**: Story 1.4 picked CSS Modules plus CSS custom properties for styling and **Base UI** (`@base-ui/react`) for behaviour (Task 1.4.1 — Radix was the original choice and was reversed on 2026-08-31, on the intention to swap in a Base-UI-based shared component library later rather than on the measurements, which favour Radix by ~46 kB), Task 1.4.2 got the first stylesheet into the build with no dependency at all, **Task 1.4.3 defined the tokens and the theme**, **Task 1.4.4 layered the market semantics over them** — price direction, a four-band anomaly ramp, and feed status — **Task 1.4.5 built the first five components, the Base UI seam, and the Storybook workshop they are developed in**, and **Task 1.4.6 verified the story from a clean tree and wrote `docs/adr/0004-*`**. The story is closed. **Story 1.9 is closed** — Task 1.9.1 chose Vitest 4.1.11, Task 1.9.2 installed it and made `packages/shared`'s suite real, **Task 1.9.3 gave `apps/backend` 49 tests across 3 files** driving the assembled server with `app.inject()`, and **Task 1.9.4 gave `apps/frontend` 47 across 8** rendering the real component tree under jsdom. **There is no `echo` placeholder anywhere in the workspace any more** — ~~118~~ ~~137~~ ~~160~~ ~~170~~ ~~183~~ ~~189~~ ~~207~~ ~~218~~ 229 tests across three packages (118 when Story 1.9 closed; Task 1.12.1 took `packages/shared` from 7 to 26, Task 1.12.2 to 37; Task 1.12.3 took `apps/frontend` from 74 to 84 and Task 1.12.4 to 97). **Task 1.9.5 added `pnpm coverage`** — one root command, `@vitest/coverage-v8`, three reports, no threshold, and deliberately not a `verify` step. **Task 1.9.6 wrote the conventions down** — location, naming, the per-package config, the eight rules, the three-plus-one levels and the must-not-assert list — and closed this file's long-standing outstanding item by measuring how to run a single file and a single test by name. **Task 1.9.7 closed the story with `docs/adr/0009-*`**, re-running all seven criteria and re-executing all eight command forms from a clean tree rather than reading them, and re-taking every figure. Story 1.9 came to **five** new dependencies — `vitest` and `@vitest/coverage-v8` at the root, `jsdom` and the two Testing Library packages in `apps/frontend` — and **no change to the frontend artefact at all**: still 271 modules / 343,658 B / 10,926 B / three files / md5 `cba2825c…`, unchanged since Task 1.7.7 and now for the fifth story running. It also found that the "a green `pnpm test` means no tests exist" warning stood in **thirteen** places rather than the three that were recorded, and amended all of them. **Story 1.6 then gave both apps a configuration boundary and closed with `docs/adr/0006-*`** — one module reading the environment on the backend, `envPrefix`/`envDir` stated as decisions on the frontend, a `basename` derived from `base`, two `.env.example` files and a sixth `pnpm verify` step checking them. It came to **+0.01 kB** on unchanged module and file counts, and no dependency at all. Stories 1.8–1.12 can all proceed from here.

**The visual direction is now settled and written down, and it reversed one of the story's original constraints.** `planning/epic-01-application-foundation/story-04-.../VISUAL-LANGUAGE.md` is the design input to Tasks 1.4.3–1.4.6: MarketPulse should read as an internal application at a large financial institution — dense, sober, desktop analyst tooling. Four decisions, all taken 2026-08-31 with the user: **a light theme only in V1** (this reversed "dark theme is the primary theme", which was an inference about market-monitoring software in general rather than a measurement); **neutral chrome with no brand accent**, so colour appears only where it carries market meaning; **the system font stack, no webfont**; and colour never being the sole encoding of anything. The consequence to carry into any styling work: with no brand hue and no distinctive typeface, **the identity is entirely structural** — a warm `#f4f3ee` ground under white surfaces, 1px near-black hairlines rather than grey borders, a single 2px radius, a 4px spacing grid, and right-aligned tabular numerals. Substituting a neutral grey for the warm one, or a light border for the near-black rule, looks like nothing in isolation and produces a generic admin panel. One measurement recorded there and owed to Task 1.4.4: the positive green `#498100` is 4.27:1 on the page ground and so fails AA there while passing at 4.75 on white. **Task 1.4.4 resolved it by darkening the green to `#427400`** (5.07 / 5.63 / 5.34 on the three grounds) rather than by constraining positive values to white surfaces, and recorded the divergence in `VISUAL-LANGUAGE.md` as that document's own rule requires.

`README.md` is the human-facing setup and command reference, and since Task 1.8.5 it goes further than that — it documents the **running** application: the four routes and what is deliberately empty in each, and ~~the five things~~ **the seven things** a correct first run shows that read as faults (the `DISCONNECTED` **market-feed** indicator — which since Task 1.12.5 sits beside a **second** indicator reading `HEALTHY`, so the label has to be read before the word — the `BACKEND SERVICE` region's `CHECKING` placeholder that every load renders for a round trip, the `--:--:-- ET` clock, the render check's deliberate `STALE` and `DISCONNECTED` rows, the sample failure block, the Ctrl-C noise, and the `GET /health` every 30 seconds from nobody you can see). **Task 1.8.6 followed it from a clean clone and it works end to end**, correcting three claims that did not survive the run: the startup block said the output ends with Vite's address, where the interleaving of the three loops is a race and only the `Server listening at` line is reliably last; the dev-loop paragraph gave a component edit as "about 100 ms", which is the _stylesheet_ figure and contradicted the document's own opening section; and `ready in 96 ms` was quoted twice as what you will see, where the clone said 121 ms. Everything else in it held, the ten distinct intra-document anchors included — and the double-hyphen one is correct, so a link checker that collapses whitespace reports it broken. **Task 1.8.7 re-took its figures rather than reading past them and they all hold**: thirteen startup lines, a 15-second poll window, a stylesheet at 10.93 kB against an actual 10,926 B, seven Vite lines for a busy 5173 and sixteen for a busy 3000; the anchors were re-checked independently at 34 headings, 11 links, 10 distinct and **0 broken**, reproducing the double-hyphen trap a third time. The one claim it corrected is the Ctrl-C transcript's: the spurious `node_modules missing` warning is **intermittent**, not guaranteed. Prose figures and intra-document links are now recorded as a `pnpm verify` gap in their own right — see that paragraph below. `docs/adr/0001-*` records why the toolchain is shaped the way it is, `docs/adr/0002-*` why the backend is, `docs/adr/0003-*` why the frontend build is, `docs/adr/0004-*` why the styling, the component library and the workshop are, `docs/adr/0005-*` why the routing, the layout and the deployable shape are, `docs/adr/0006-*` why configuration and the secrets boundary are, `docs/adr/0007-*` why the logging, the error contract and the failure containment are, `docs/adr/0008-*` why the development loop is legible the way it is, why the browser boundary is CORS rather than a proxy, why the frontend's ports are literals and why readiness is a script, `docs/adr/0009-*` why the test runner, the test conventions and the coverage command are what they are, and `docs/adr/0010-*` why the pipeline runs `pnpm verify` by name and defines nothing of its own, why the per-step split is derived rather than declared, why the store is cached and the build never is, why coverage runs in CI and gates nothing, and what the green tick does not certify — including the required status check, which is the only durable record of a gate no file here can hold. and `docs/adr/0011-*` why both halves are hosted where they are, why the two artefacts need different answers, what a commit-SHA tag and a digest each mean, why the probes are HTTP, what a green deploy certifies and what it cannot, and the four-minute keep-alive Epic 10 inherits, `docs/adr/0012-*` why client-side status is a second vocabulary rather than a widening of the first, why `degraded` is defined structurally and not by latency, why there are two indicators rather than one, why the poll interval is a literal coupled to the request deadline, and what a green indicator certifies and what it cannot, and **`docs/adr/0013-*` why the browser tool and the specs' home are what they are, why the suite is a second CI job rather than a `verify` step, why there are two suites sharing one tool, why axe is a gate before a merge and a report after one, and — in two lists rather than one — what each green run certifies and what it does not. ~~All **twelve** were written~~ — **that count was wrong when written and is accidentally right now: there were eleven, and Task 1.12.8 made it twelve (2026-09-04); Task 1.13.6 makes it THIRTEEN.** All thirteen were written from the facts below, so a change here usually needs a change there.
