**The Rolldown binding was read by name out of the runner's tree rather than inferred from a green install.** Exactly one is installed — `@rolldown+binding-linux-x64-gnu@1.2.6`, carrying a 19,324,672-byte `rolldown-binding.linux-x64-gnu.node` — against `@rolldown+binding-darwin-arm64@1.2.6` locally, out of the fifteen variants the lockfile records as optional dependencies. The workflow asserts both the count and the name, so a second binding or the wrong one is a red step naming what it found.

**The tree is not the same size on the two platforms, and the difference is not the thing the story told you to watch.** ~~The runner installs **397 packages** against the Mac's **398** recorded in Task 1.9.7. The one package is **`fsevents@2.3.3`** — `os: [darwin]`, optional, reached through Vite — not Rolldown, whose binding is one on each side.~~ **Superseded by Task 1.13.6 (2026-09-04): it is 400 on Linux against 402 on macOS, and the difference is now TWO packages rather than one** — `fsevents@2.3.3` via Vite and **`fsevents@2.3.2`** reached through Playwright, both `os: [darwin]` and optional. Both re-read rather than inferred: `Packages: +400` off a runner install, 402 off a clean clone with an empty store. So a package-count difference between a laptop and the runner is expected and is not evidence of a broken install; quote **400 for Linux and 402 for macOS**, and re-read them rather than citing this line, which has now been wrong once.

**Both failure modes were made to happen rather than assumed, on a throwaway branch that was deleted afterwards.** A hand-edited `package.json` (`prettier` 3.9.6 → 3.9.5, lockfile untouched) fails with **`ERR_PNPM_OUTDATED_LOCKFILE`** at exit 1, naming the package and both versions. And `.nvmrc` set to `22.20.0` fails with **`ERR_PNPM_UNSUPPORTED_ENGINE`** — the instructive part being that everything upstream of the install went green: `actions/setup-node` installed Node 22 as asked, and the workflow's own version assertion _passed_, because `.nvmrc` and the installed Node agreed with each other. **`engineStrict` is what caught it**, at install, which is exactly the mechanism Story 1.1 recorded. A workflow assertion can only catch a runner that disagrees with the pin; it cannot catch a pin that is wrong.

**The cold install on the runner is 5,318 / 5,640 / 6,127 ms across the three green runs taken, and most of it is not the network.** `reused 0, downloaded 397` both times, from a store at `/home/runner/.local/share/pnpm/store/v11`. The dominant line is pnpm 11's own supply-chain check: `✓ Lockfile passes supply-chain policies (492 entries in 4.5s)`, `in 4.6s` and `in 5.2s` on the three green runs, and `in 2.5s` on the run that then failed the lockfile check — so it runs **before** `--frozen-lockfile` is enforced and its cost is close to the whole install. It never appears locally on a warm tree, where `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile` is `Already up to date` in **210 ms**. **Task 1.10.3 confirmed both halves of that.** With a full cache hit the check still reports 2.7 / 3.2 / 3.4 / 3.9 s against 3.5 / 4.5 s uncached, because it verifies the lockfile rather than the store — so it is uncacheable in principle and measurably so. **`--trust-lockfile` would remove it**, and was rejected: a pull request may change the lockfile and this runner is the machine meant to check it, which is what the flag's own help text excludes. The 2.5–4.5 s spread on the same 492 entries is still the noisiest number in the job.

**The pnpm store is cached, nothing else is, and the honest finding is that it buys less than it looks like (Task 1.10.3).** The key is `pnpm-store-v1-<runner.os>-node<major>-<hashFiles(pnpm-lock.yaml)>` with a restore-key dropping the hash; the path comes from `pnpm store path` rather than a literal, and **`v1` is the manual bust — bump it in both the key and the restore-key, and that is the whole procedure.** The OS component is load-bearing: 397 packages install on Linux against 398 on macOS from one lockfile. Both halves were exercised. An exact hit is `cache-hit: true` and `reused 397, downloaded 0`; a **genuinely changed lockfile** (a throwaway `type-fest` devDependency, added and reverted) misses the exact key, hits the restore key, and reads `cache-hit: false` with `Packages: +399` and `reused 397, downloaded 2` — installing correctly under `--frozen-lockfile` and saving a new cache under the new hash. A genuine miss is `reused 0, downloaded 397` (**397 was the figure when that was measured; it is 400 now** — see the platform paragraph above). Measured across three tasks: uncached installs are **4,455–6,127 ms (n=8, median ~5,610)** and cached ones **3,895–4,597 ms (n=4, median ~4,000)**, so the cache saves **~1.6 s and the two ranges overlap** — while the chain totals over seven runs of one tree were 18,589 / 18,612 / 24,450 / 24,568 / 24,693 / 31,274 / 32,210 ms, a **13.6 s spread on identical work**. It was kept because it is correct and costs one pinned action, not because it moves the clock. **Never read a CI total as a regression; read the install summary and the per-step split.**

**Two traps around that cache, both measured rather than reasoned about.** **`actions/cache` declares `cache-hit` and nothing else** — `cache-matched-key` belongs to `actions/cache/restore` and reads as the empty string here, printed three times before being read out of the action's own `action.yml` at the pinned SHA; so a restore-key hit and a total miss are both `cache-hit: false`, and pnpm's `reused`/`downloaded` counts are what separate them. And **a cache saved on a `pull_request` run is invisible to a `workflow_dispatch` run on the same branch**: the dispatched run reported `Cache not found` for the key the pull request run had just reported `Cache saved` for, because a pull request's cache is scoped to its own ref. So Task 1.10.2's "`workflow_dispatch` covers a branch with no pull request open" does not extend to anything cached — a warm reading has to come from re-running the pull request run itself.

**Nothing under `dist/`, `storybook-static/` or any `.tsbuildinfo` is cached, and the number is what decides it rather than the principle.** Story 1.9 measured what a stale `packages/shared/dist` does — 13 failing backend tests whose messages name nothing about staleness — `tsc -b` decides what to rebuild from `.tsbuildinfo`, and `tsc -b --clean` deletes the output of the sources that _currently_ exist, so a restored `dist/` can carry orphans from a branch where a source was deleted. Task 1.10.2 measured the prize: running the chain twice in one job with the build outputs present moves **exactly one step**, `build` 6,010 → 3,477 ms, because lint, Prettier, both checks and Vitest start from nothing either way. So it is **~2.5 s against a 13.6 s spread** — a correctness risk taken for a saving smaller than the measurement noise. The rule and that number are written in the workflow beside the cache step.

**Coverage runs in the pipeline as a step in the `verify` job that cannot fail it, and there is no threshold (Task 1.10.4).** It sits after `Verify` with `continue-on-error`, outside the chain exactly as Story 1.9 left it. **A step and not a second job**, because a second job is a second runner — its own checkout, setup-node, Corepack, cache restore, install and then, unavoidably, its own `pnpm build`, since `packages/shared` is consumed as built output; as a step it reuses the build `verify` just did and costs **8,279 ms on the runner** against **2.60–3.06 s locally (n=3)**, which is 2.7–3.2× and well inside the 13.6 s spread two runners already show on identical work. A second _workflow_ was never a candidate: the triggers and the concurrency group are properties of the workflow, so a second file forks both. The per-package table is **derived from `pnpm -r`'s own line prefixes**, the same property the per-step split has, so a fourth package appears without an edit — and the runner reproduced the laptop's twelve figures to the digit.

**`continue-on-error` makes a step's `conclusion` read `success` however it exited, and that is the trap to know.** The whole point is that no coverage outcome can turn the tick red, so "the green tick certifies the fast suite and not coverage" is structural rather than documented. But the failure is then visible **only as annotations** — the step's own `::error::` and `Process completed with exit code 1` — and `steps.<id>.conclusion` is `success` in both the API and the UI; the real result is `steps.<id>.outcome`. **Never write a later step's `if:` against a `continue-on-error` step's `conclusion`**, and never read the absence of a marked step as "coverage was fine".

**There is no threshold, and the decision was argued rather than omitted.** Three reasons, in order of weight: a minimum over nine components, one route table, one configuration module and no application state is a number invented before there is anything to hold it to, and the cheapest way to meet it is testing what is easy; **Task 1.10.5 is about to move the backend's denominator for a real reason**, so a threshold set now would be re-set a task later, which is a threshold that tracks the number rather than constraining it; and the cheapest way to meet any threshold here is to exclude the two 0% entrypoints. **That last one is measured rather than feared** — temporarily adding `src/index.ts` to the backend's `coverage.exclude` took it **64.33% → 91.08%** on the runner, +26.75 points for describing less. The reversal trigger is a number somebody would defend: after 1.10.5, against a denominator that includes the process half, and per package, because Vitest configures coverage per package and there is no root config.

**One invariant is now checked rather than stated, and it checks presence rather than a percentage.** The step asserts that `apps/backend/coverage/src/index.ts.html` and `apps/frontend/coverage/src/main.tsx.html` exist — i.e. that both 0% entrypoints are still **in** the denominator. Not that they are at 0%: Task 1.10.5 is expected to make `index.ts` reachable, and an assertion pinned to 0% would fail on the task that fixes it. This is a direct answer to the third class of `verify` gap — the stated invariant that quietly stops being enforced. Both halves were made to happen on a throwaway commit that was reverted: the assertion fired naming the file, and **the run conclusion was still `success`**.

**One artefact is uploaded, two are declined, and all three carry a number.** The three `coverage/` directories — 956 KB across 73 files, **211,427 B uploaded** — at **7 days** rather than the 90-day default, because a coverage report is read within days of the run that produced it or not at all; the retention was confirmed on the runner from the artefact's `expires_at`. **`apps/frontend/storybook-static/` is declined** at 9.3 MB per push for a bundle nothing downstream consumes — publishing it _as a site_ is a different question and is Story 1.11's. **`apps/frontend/dist/` is declined too**, in favour of a fingerprint step that prints every file with its size and md5 into the job summary: an upload would be downloaded to answer "did the artefact move", and a fingerprint _is_ that comparison, greppable across runs at no storage cost. A coverage-reporting service is declined outright — a token, a second definition of the number, and an external dependency for a repository whose whole coverage story is three local HTML reports; the reversal trigger is a reviewer needing per-PR diff coverage.

**The fingerprint proved something five stories of clean-clone measurement had not: the frontend artefact is byte-identical on Linux.** The runner reports `index-C-Puqfnm.js` at **343,658 B, md5 `cba2825c87721779927b2f385df406e9`**, the stylesheet at 10,926 B and `index.html` at 1,101 B — **355,685 B over three files**, matching the laptop and the figure unchanged since Task 1.7.7. Every previous re-measurement of that identity ran on macOS. It is deliberately a **record and not a check**: nothing asserts the hash, because the artefact is supposed to change when the frontend changes and a pinned hash would fail for the right reason.

**The backend's process half is tested now, and it is a fourth level of test with its own runner (Task 1.10.5) — the fifth arrived in Story 1.13 and is `pnpm e2e`.** `apps/backend/src/index.process.test.ts` is **ten tests** run by `apps/backend/vitest.process.config.ts` under `pnpm test:process`, and every one spawns `dist/index.js` as a real child on a real port: `SIGTERM` and `SIGINT` each drain, exit **0** and release the port; a second signal during a shutdown exits **1** with the level-40 record and no `shutdown complete`; a drain that outlives the ceiling is forced out at exit 1 with the level-**50** `shutdown timed out, forcing exit`; a busy port exits 1 with `err.code: "EADDRINUSE"` on a level-50 `server failed to start` and **no** `signal received` beside it; `PORT=0` exits 1 with a plain stderr line and **zero** log records; and both crash handlers write one level-**60** record carrying the right `event` and exit 1 — including at `LOG_LEVEL=silent`, where the whole log is that one record, and including a crash **during** a drain, which writes the record, does not start a second shutdown, and exits **0**. Every behaviour was **seen to fail**: ten deliberate breaks in `index.ts` and `config.ts`, one per test, each producing a failure naming the behaviour (an unhandled SIGINT reads `expected null to be +0`, a missing level restore reads `expected [ …(5) ] to include 'shutdown complete'`, `MIN_PORT = 0` reads `the server was still running 20000 ms after it was asked to exit`).

**It is a seventh `pnpm verify` step and not a CI step, and that follows from the workflow's own rule rather than from taste.** The pipeline runs `pnpm verify` **by name** and defines nothing of its own, so a suite CI runs beside the chain is a second definition of "verified" — the exact fork Story 1.10 exists to prevent. Putting it _inside_ `pnpm test` was the other candidate and Story 1.9 had already argued it down: the 49 injected tests are what developers run all day, they need no build and no socket, and they must not become conditional on either. So there are two configs, two commands and one chain. **It cost the workflow no edit at all** — the per-step split is derived from pnpm's own `$ pnpm run <name>` announcements — which is the third time that property has paid, after `stories` and `env:check`. The price is real and is stated: `pnpm verify` went **11.78 s → 21.30 s** warm (build 2.58, lint 3.88, `format:check` 3.25, `stories` 0.26, `env:check` 0.27, `test` 2.97, **`test:process` 7.60**), and **5 s of that 7.6 is the shutdown ceiling being what `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` says it is** — the one test in the repository whose duration is a property of the application.

**`PORT=0` is unavailable, and the finding is recorded as an assertion rather than as a comment.** The ephemeral-port strategy every process suite reaches for first — bind 0, read back what the kernel chose — is rejected by `config.ts`, whose range is `MIN_PORT = 1` to `MAX_PORT = 65535`. Widening it was the third option and was **not** taken: it is a change to the shipping application made for a test's convenience, and 0 is a real value meaning "any free port" that `PORT=` in a `.env` file would then produce by accident, which is the case `present()` exists to prevent. What replaces it is a probe — bind an ephemeral port, read it, close it, hand the number to the child — and **the race that leaves is answered rather than ignored**: if something takes the port in between, the child fails to bind and exits 1, and `waitForReady` notices the **exit** rather than waiting out its deadline and fails immediately with the child's whole log attached, naming the port. A port held by a previous run is therefore a diagnosable failure and not a flake, which is the failure a shared runner actually produces.

**Nothing in the suite waits on a log line, and there are four separate reasons.** At `LOG_LEVEL=warn` and above a healthy server writes **zero** lines, `Server listening at …` included, so a readiness grep hangs rather than fails — the worse failure. Fastify writes one such line per bound interface address with loopback first, so `HOST=0.0.0.0` still reads `127.0.0.1` on the first line and the line is not evidence of the bound interface (refined in Task 1.11.2 — see below). `pretty` and `json` render the same record two different ways, and Task 1.8.2 changed the `pretty` clock format under a matcher that would have been written against the old one. Readiness is a `GET /health` poll at `127.0.0.1` — named explicitly, because this server binds IPv4 only and Node's `fetch` trying both families for `localhost` would make the probe pass for the wrong reason if the bind address ever moved. Log records are read **after** the fact, as assertions about what was written, which is a different thing from waiting for one. **No timing is asserted anywhere**: the ~100 ms signal-to-exit and the sub-millisecond drain are baselines for a human reading a regression, and CI's runner-to-runner spread on identical work is 13.6 s.

**This application contains no way to crash it, so the crash tests inject one — and the injection is one line of `node -e` rather than a route.** An error thrown in a route never reaches the process-level handlers at all: Task 1.7.4's error handler catches it, answers an `ApiError` and logs the stack under that request's `reqId`. What reaches them is by definition detached from any request. Tasks 1.7.5 and 1.9.3 both produced one by adding a temporary throwing route to the shipping application; `startCrashableServer()` instead runs the real entrypoint through a two-line ESM wrapper passed on the command line, which imports `dist/index.js` and listens on an **IPC channel** for a message telling it to `throw` from a detached timer or to float a rejection. That leaves the real handlers, the real logger and the real drain in place, ships nothing, and makes the crash **deterministic rather than timed** — no `setTimeout` guess about when the server is ready. The wrapper is written in the test file rather than kept on disk on purpose: a fixture under `src/` compiles into `dist/`, and `tsc -b --clean` deletes the output of the sources that _currently_ exist, so a fixture added and later deleted by hand orphans its `dist/` files permanently.

**Holding a drain open needs an incomplete request, and the obvious mechanism does not work.** An idle keep-alive connection does **not** delay `app.close()` — Fastify closes idle connections and waits only for active ones, measured at under a millisecond in Task 1.2.4 — so the second-signal path, the ceiling and the crash-during-drain path are all unreachable without one. What works is a socket that writes `GET /health HTTP/1.1` and its `Host` header and then **stops**, with no terminating blank line: the connection is active, the drain waits for it, and the 5-second ceiling becomes reachable. **One finding came out of building that, and it corrects a plausible reading of Task 1.7.5.** Completing that request after `close()` has been called gets a **503**, not a 200 — Fastify answers `request aborted - refusing to accept new requests as server is closing`. So **what holds the drain is the connection, not a request in flight**, and this suite cannot produce a 200 during a drain without adding a slow route to the shipping application. 1.7.5's "the in-flight request is answered 200" was measured against a deliberately slow route it had added; it is not reproducible from outside, and the assertion that replaced it is the one that matters anyway — `shutdown complete` appears **after** the level-60 record, which is the log level having been restored.

**A staleness check was built here and removed, and the reason is worth more than the check was.** `dist/index.js` is what has the process behaviour in it, so a stale `dist` is a suite testing the previous commit — silently, and green. Comparing the newest mtime under `src/` against the newest under `dist/` looks like it answers that and does not: **`tsc -b` decides what to re-emit from the content hashes in `tsconfig.tsbuildinfo`**, so a `git checkout` — which rewrites every source file's mtime and changes not one byte — leaves a perfectly current `dist/` looking stale. Measured on this branch: every `src/*.ts` was newer than every `dist/*.js` immediately after a green `pnpm build`, and the check failed a correct tree on its first run. What is left is an existence check with a message naming `pnpm build`, and the ordering is `pnpm verify`'s job — which is one more argument for the suite being a step in that chain rather than something run on the side.

**The coverage figure did not move, and that was predicted, measured and is the headline finding rather than a footnote.** `apps/backend` is **64.33% / 75 / 72.72 / 63.82** with `src/index.ts` at **0%** — identical to Task 1.9.5's to the digit, on the day that file got ten tests. V8 coverage accounts for the code **the runner's own process loads**; this suite's subject runs in a _child_ process the runner never instruments, and the file it runs is **built output** while `coverage.include` is `src/**/*.ts`. Measured rather than reasoned about: running the process suite under the backend's own coverage settings reports **0% of 354 statements, 0 of 100 functions** — it instruments nothing at all, its own test file included. `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` on each child plus a merge is the mechanism that would move it, and it was **not** built: it means a coverage directory per spawn, a merge step, and a second definition of what the backend's number means, for a figure nobody gates on. **So the number and the coverage are two claims now, not one** — `src/index.ts` at 0% means "no runner instruments it", not "nothing tests it", and anyone reading that cell without this paragraph will draw the wrong conclusion. `pnpm coverage` deliberately still runs the fast suite only, so the two numbers cannot drift apart by accident.

**Task 1.10.4's threshold decision was revisited against the new denominator and the answer is still no — but one of its three arguments is gone.** 1.10.4 declined a threshold for three reasons: nothing to hold a number to yet, the cheapest way to meet one is excluding the two 0% entrypoints, and **1.10.5 was about to move the denominator**. The third is now spent, and it turned out to be wrong in the interesting direction: the denominator did not move at all, because a spawned child is invisible. The first two stand unchanged, and there is now a fourth that is stronger than any of them — **a threshold over this package would be a threshold over a number that is provably not a measure of what is tested here.** `src/index.ts` is the best-tested file in the backend by behaviour and the worst by instrumentation. The reversal trigger is no longer "after 1.10.5"; it is **a merged-coverage mechanism, or a package whose coverage figure and test coverage are the same claim**. And the workflow's denominator assertion is untouched and still passes: it checks that `apps/backend/coverage/src/index.ts.html` **exists**, not that it reads 0%, which is exactly why Task 1.10.4 wrote it that way.

**Four pinned actions now** — `actions/upload-artifact@043fb46d…` (v7.0.1) joins checkout, setup-node and cache, all on commit SHAs and all bumped by hand.

**The `cache: pnpm` ordering question is closed.** `corepack enable` stays **after** `actions/setup-node` so the shims land in the pinned Node, which means `setup-node`'s own `cache: pnpm` cannot see pnpm when it runs; the price is the third pinned action. The alternative — Corepack first — keeps one action and pins pnpm to whatever Node the runner image ships for the length of one step, which is a second invisible toolchain. An action that _installs_ pnpm was never a candidate: `packageManager` is the pin.

**The workflow is `verify` and so is its job, and both names were settled in Task 1.10.2 rather than in 1.10.6.** `CI` is generic and anything containing "tests" or "coverage" would be dishonest — the chain is seven steps of which `test` is one, and coverage is deliberately not gated. The reason to settle them a task early rather than at the badge: a badge URL and a required status check are both keyed on these names, and a rename silently un-requires a check. The **step** is one `pnpm verify` and the file contains no second copy of the chain, which is the whole of Story 1.10's argument: `stories` and `env:check` both reached the chain before a workflow existed, and a workflow that re-listed the tools would have missed both.

**The file is `verify.yml` too since Task 1.10.6, and the rename had to happen before the badge rather than after.** Three identifiers now agree, and they are independent of each other: the badge URL keys on the **file** name (`/actions/workflows/verify.yml/badge.svg`), the required status check keys on the **job** name, and the badge's rendered label comes from `name:` — which is why it read `verify - passing` for four tasks while the file was still `ci.yml`. The badge endpoint has **three** states and all three were measured anonymously: a workflow file GitHub has never seen answers **`404 text/plain`** — a broken image, not the "no status" badge the task predicted; a file that exists but has no run on the tracked branch answers 200 with **`verify - no status`**; and the old path keeps answering 200 with **`verify - passing`** after the file is gone, serving the last conclusion it ever had. So the failure mode of renaming a workflow without moving its badge is not a red badge or a broken one — **it is a badge frozen green forever**, which is the one outcome nobody checks for. Renaming this file means editing the badge URL in the same commit.

**The badge is at the top of `README.md`, and the paragraph beside it is the part that took the work.** It links to the workflow's run history rather than to a branch's last run, so a red badge is one click from the reason. **What branch it reports is not what the documentation says**, and this was watched rather than assumed: GitHub documents the badge as the default branch's status, and this one went from `verify - no status` to `verify - passing` on a **pull request** run, while `main` still held the file under its old name and had never run `verify.yml` at all. So the badge is not a reliable statement about merged state, and the README says so rather than repeating the documented claim. What green certifies is written out as the chain: both bundlers, lint, format, stories, `env:check`, ~~**118**~~ **137** fast tests and the ten-test process suite, with the counts taken from a real run rather than copied from the previous sentence, which is how "103 tests" would have been quietly wrong the moment 1.10.5 landed. What it does **not** certify is coverage, and that clause is load-bearing rather than pedantic now: the pipeline publishes a per-package table and a 211,427 B artefact on every run and gates on neither, so a reader seeing those figures under a green tick has every reason to connect them unless the sentence disconnects them. The mechanism behind the disclaimer is structural — the coverage step is `continue-on-error`, so no coverage outcome can turn the badge red, and `conclusion` reads `success` however it exited.
**Task 1.10.8 re-took the badge's states and one of them had expired, which is the whole argument for re-measuring rather than citing.** Task 1.10.6 recorded the old path — `ci.yml/badge.svg` — answering **200 `verify - passing`** after the file was gone, frozen at its last conclusion. It now answers **`404 text/plain`**, and GitHub's workflow list holds exactly one entry (`verify.yml`, id 348954620). So "freezes green forever" is wrong: it freezes green for a **window** and then becomes a broken image. That is worse than either failure alone, because the wrong signal arrives first and the obvious one arrives after nobody is looking. The rule is unchanged and its reason is sharper — renaming this file means editing the badge URL in the same commit. `verify.yml` itself answers 200 `verify - passing`, and an unknown file answers 404.

**`verify` is a required status check on `main`, through a repository ruleset — and a ruleset is invisible in a diff, which is why it is written down here.** Ruleset `main` (id 22160620) requires a pull request and the `verify` check on the default branch, with `strict_required_status_checks_policy` **off** because a `pull_request` run already verifies the merge commit rather than the branch tip. It keys on the **job** name, so renaming the job silently un-requires it — that is the whole reason 1.10.2 settled the names early. What it binds is the chain and **nothing about coverage**, because the job is green whatever the coverage step did, and that is the intended shape rather than an oversight. Two settings were changed from GitHub's defaults after the first creation: `require_extra_approval_for_unattributed_changes` is **off** (it defaults on and, with `required_approving_review_count: 0`, would block a solo maintainer's own pull request on commits carrying a co-author that resolves to no account), and repository **admin bypass is retained**, so a red run is a decision to override rather than a wall.

**The job summary has three sections and their order is a decision.** The per-step split first — it _is_ the chain, so a reader who opens a run meets the thing the badge claims; the frontend artefact's fingerprint second, as a record of what the run produced; coverage last, because it is the one section the tick does not certify and a figure that gates nothing should not be the first number under a green tick. The split in the summary is a **redirect of output that already existed**, not a second report: it reads the same derived file the log table does, so the names still come out of pnpm's own announcements and a step added to `verify` appears with no workflow edit. **No count is written anywhere in the summary** — the coverage section's hand-written "103 tests and the 10-test process suite" was removed in the same change, because a hand-listed chain in a nicer typeface is the same fork this story exists to prevent. The runner-to-runner spread is printed beside the table for the same reason it is recorded here: 31,075 ms against 21,989 ms on one commit, and 18,589–32,210 ms over nine runs of one tree — both **six-step** figures, against the seven-step chain's two readings of 43,263 ms and **42,664 ms** (Task 1.10.6's own run: build 8,895 / lint 8,598 / `format:check` 6,854 / `stories` 496 / `env:check` 501 / `test` 7,222 / `test:process` 9,069), so quoting the old spread beside a new total reads as a ten-second regression that did not happen.

**Everything more expensive than that was declined, and the list is the record.** Inline annotations, a comment bot and a coverage-diff service each add a token or a dependency to a repository with three packages and no external contributors; a coverage-reporting service was already declined in Task 1.10.4 for the same reason. The reversal trigger is an external contributor or a reviewer needing per-PR diff coverage.

**Per-step timings are derived from the chain's own output, so the workflow names no step.** pnpm announces `$ pnpm run build && pnpm run lint && …` once and then a root-level `$ <command>` line as each step starts; the names are read out of the first line and the boundaries are the timestamps of the rest, with every line stamped with milliseconds since the step began. A step added to `verify` therefore appears in the split with no workflow edit — and on a failure the stamps run out and **the last name printed is the step it died in**, which was seen four times rather than reasoned about. `set -euo pipefail` is what keeps the exit code intact through the `| while read | tee` pipeline.

**Push is restricted to `main` and everything else is verified through its pull request, and the reason is not only the duplicate run.** A pull request from a branch in this repository fires **both** events, so a bare `push:` beside `pull_request:` runs the whole chain twice for one change and the answer arrives from whichever finishes first. The half worth knowing is that they are not the same check: a `pull_request` run verifies the **merge commit** (`refs/pull/N/merge`), which is what would land on `main`, while a push run verifies the branch tip, which is not. The cost is real and stated — **a branch with no pull request open is not verified at all**, measured rather than assumed: pushing this task's branch produced **no run**, because the trigger is read from the workflow file **on the pushed ref**. `workflow_dispatch` covers that case and is what made Task 1.10.1's second cold-install reading possible without an empty commit.

**Superseded runs are cancelled everywhere except `main`.** `concurrency.group` is `${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}` and `cancel-in-progress` is the expression `${{ github.ref != 'refs/heads/main' }}` — a run on the default branch is the record of what that commit does, and a cancelled one leaves a commit on `main` with no verdict against it. Confirmed by pushing twice in six seconds: the first run's conclusion is `cancelled` and the second ran to completion.

**Four failure classes were made to happen on the runner, one per step class, and all four ended as a run conclusion of `failure` rather than a green tick with red text inside it.** An **unformatted Markdown file** in `planning/` (Prettier owns it) died at `format:check` with exit 1; a **type error** in `packages/shared` died at `build` with **exit 2** — tsc's own code, so the propagation is the child's code and not a flattened 1; a **stories file moved aside** died at `stories` with `1 of 9 components have no stories.`; and a **deliberately failing test** died at `test` with `[ERR_PNPM_RECURSIVE_RUN_FIRST_FAIL] @marketpulse/shared@0.0.0 test: vitest run` — which is the deepest nesting this repository has (package script → `pnpm -r` → `verify` → the runner) and it arrived intact. GitHub reported `##[error]Process completed with exit code N` on every one. So Tasks 1.2.5 and 1.2.6's exit-code measurement is now confirmed **on the runner** rather than cited from a laptop, which is the one claim this story leans on.
**Task 1.10.8 made it happen a fifth way and re-proved all five on the SHIPPING workflow, five edits later.** The probes ran on a throwaway branch with its own draft pull request, because `push` is restricted to `main` so a bare branch push runs nothing — and because a deliberate red probe on the story's own branch would then be blocked by the required check, which is the one thing that gate was set to prevent. Build died at `build` with **exit 2** (`TS2322`), format at `format:check`, a moved stories file at `stories` (`1 of 9 components have no stories.`), a failing unit test at `test` (`ERR_PNPM_RECURSIVE_RUN_FIRST_FAIL`), and — **new, and red on a runner for the first time** — a failing process assertion at **`test:process`**, `2 failed | 8 passed (10)`, the seventh step reporting through the same nesting. All five ran to a run conclusion of `failure`, and the derived split named the step it died in every time. **One thing the probes taught that the plan did not anticipate**: the first attempt at the `test` probe was a failing test that was also unformatted, and it died at `format:check` without ever reaching `test`. A chain reports its first failure and nothing after it, so a probe has to be surgical or it proves the wrong step.

**The runner's split has a different shape from the laptop's, and the runner-to-runner variance is larger than the warm/cold difference — which is the finding.** Cold, on a fresh checkout: **install 5,606 ms and the chain 31,075 ms** — build 8,527 / lint 8,116 / `format:check` 6,186 / `stories` 466 / `env:check` 476 / `test` 6,437. A second job on a different runner, same tree and same commit, was **21,989 ms** cold (build 6,010 / lint 5,677 / `format:check` 4,430 / `stories` 347 / `env:check` 338 / `test` 4,517) — **9 s apart for identical work**. Running the chain a second time inside that same job, with `dist/`, `storybook-static/` and the `.tsbuildinfo` files already present, gives **18,953 ms** — and **only `build` moves** (6,010 → 3,477 ms); every other step is inside its own variance, because lint, Prettier, the two checks and Vitest all start from nothing either way. Two things follow. The runner is **2.2–3.5× the laptop's 11.78 s warm** per step, so do not read a CI number as a regression against a local one. And a caching change (Task 1.10.3) has to be measured more than twice against **that** spread, not against a single reading — the honest baseline is the install figure and the per-step split reported separately, which is why the workflow prints them separately.
**The seven-step chain has five complete readings now and the spread is back to the six-step shape.** 43,263 / 42,664 / **41,019** / 39,441 / **34,278 ms** — a **~9.0 s spread**, against Task 1.10.7's note that three readings looked like ~3.8 s. That was n=3 against the six-step chain's n=9, and it did not survive two more readings; the pipeline is not getting more consistent. (The 41,019 ms run is Task 1.10.8's fifth probe: every step ran and the last one failed, so it is a complete-chain timing that happens to end red.) **One step is not like the others.** On the `main` push run 33710087090 — build 6,759 / lint 6,706 / `format:check` 5,326 / `stories` 386 / `env:check` 394 / `test` 5,619 / **`test:process` 8,375** — against the laptop's warm build 2,088 / lint 3,642 / `format:check` 3,078 / `stories` 261 / `env:check` 267 / `test` 2,439 / **`test:process` 7,777**, the runner is 1.5–3.2× per step and `test:process` is **1.08×**. Five of its seconds are `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` elapsing in wall-clock time and a faster machine cannot shorten them, so the seventh step raises the total and _compresses_ the relative spread: a runner-versus-laptop ratio taken over the whole chain now means less than it did. Local warm chain total is **19,989 ms** against Task 1.10.5's 21.30 s.

**And there is a third kind of gap that this list does not describe, found by Task 1.6.4 and worth more than the two above.** The gaps here are _files no tool reads_. The other kind is a file every tool reads carrying a guarantee that has quietly stopped being enforced: `apps/frontend`'s explicit `types` array was written down in three places as making `process` a compile error in browser code, and it stopped doing so in Task 1.4.5 — when `.stories.tsx` files entered the program and dragged `@types/node` in through a triple-slash reference — and stayed wrong for two stories. Nothing caught it, because nothing was checking; it was a stated invariant rather than a checked one. Two of this repository's checks now exist for exactly that reason — `env:check`, and the `no-restricted-globals` block over `apps/frontend/src/**`. **The general form: re-measure a stated invariant rather than citing it, especially one in this file.** Story 1.10 inherited this alongside the two gaps, and Task 1.10.7 re-checked and enlarged it — see the two paragraphs below.

**Task 1.10.7 re-measured that invariant rather than citing it, and it is still exactly where Task 1.6.4 left it (2026-09-03).** A probe under `apps/frontend/src/` reading `process.env` and importing `node:path` **typechecks at exit 0** — `tsc -b apps/frontend` says nothing — while `eslint` reports both by name, `no-restricted-globals` on `process` and `no-restricted-imports` on `node:*`. So the documented "the `types` array makes `process` a compile error in browser code" is still false, the lint rule is still the only thing standing there, and the general form holds: **re-measure a stated invariant rather than citing it, especially one in this file.**

**Task 1.10.5 added two more of this third kind, and neither is worth a check while both are worth a line, because each fails silently and green.** **The two-runner partition is a naming convention with nothing behind it.** `apps/backend` has two Vitest configs whose globs partition `src/**/*.test.ts`: the unit config `include`s `src/**/*.test.ts` and `exclude`s `src/**/*.process.test.ts`, and the process config `include`s exactly that excluded glob. Nothing enforces the naming. A process-style test written as `src/thing.test.ts` runs in the **fast** suite — making the suite developers run all day conditional on a build and able to bind ports — and a `*.process.test.ts` file added to `packages/shared` or `apps/frontend` runs **nowhere at all**, because no other package has a second config. Both are green. This is Task 1.9.4's `.tsx` glob trap in a new place, and the only mitigation is that the two globs are written as one decision with a comment in each file saying so. **And the process suite's dependence on a fresh `dist/` is enforced by `pnpm verify`'s ordering and by an existence check, and by nothing else.** `pnpm test:process` against a stale `dist/` tests the previous commit and passes. What stands there is `fs.existsSync(ENTRY)` with a message naming `pnpm build` — presence, not freshness. A staleness check was **built for exactly this and removed after it was measured**: `tsc -b` re-emits from the content hashes in `.tsbuildinfo`, so a `git checkout` makes every source newer than every output without changing a byte, and the mtime comparison failed a correct tree on its first run. That is a decision with a measurement behind it rather than an omission, and it is one more argument for the suite being a step in the chain rather than something run on the side.

**Task 2.1.2 added one of this kind, and it is the reason the local database exists at all (2026-09-04).** `LOCAL_DATABASE_VERSION` in `scripts/local-database.mjs` is `18` because Task 2.1.1 chose PostgreSQL 18 for the managed server, and **nothing compares those two numbers**. Task 2.1.3 moved every other value in that file out to `CONFIG_VARIABLES` and left this one, deliberately — nothing in an application's configuration should name a Postgres version. The deployed version lives in an `az` argument and in `HOSTING.md`, neither of which any tool here reads, and a check would need Azure credentials — which `pnpm verify` deliberately does not have, so building one would fork the definition of "verified" in exactly the way the pipeline's founding rule exists to prevent. It is the same structural refusal that declined a deployed-configuration diff in Story 1.11, and the drift is silent in both directions with the symptom class "works locally, wrong in production", which is the whole thing the pin was for. The one-liner is `docker compose exec postgres postgres --version` against `az postgres flexible-server show --query version` — **run for the first time by Task 2.1.5 on 2026-09-05, which read 18.6 on both sides**. That is a hand comparison and not a check: the gap is unchanged, and the two numbers agreeing today says nothing about tomorrow.

**Task 1.13.2 added a third of this kind and it is the largest of the three, because what it protects is `pnpm test` itself.** The `e2e` package joins **every** `pnpm -r` fan-out the moment its directory matches a `pnpm-workspace.yaml` glob — measured at **"Scope: 4 of 5 workspace projects"** for `test`, `coverage` and `test:process` — and **the only thing keeping the browser suite out of the fast suite is that `e2e/package.json` has no `test` script.** Nothing checks that it stays absent, and adding one would make `pnpm test` need two servers, a build and a browser binary, which is exactly the argument Story 1.9 used to keep the process suite out and Task 1.10.5 honoured with `test:process`. It fails silently and green in the worst direction: the day somebody adds it, `pnpm test` starts failing on a clean clone for a reason that has nothing to do with the change. The mitigation is the same as the two above — the reason is written beside the scripts that _are_ there, in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`'s glob comment and in this paragraph, because a `package.json` cannot hold a comment.

**Story 1.13 added four more of this kind and moved a fifth OUT of the list by building a check, and the interesting claim is the line between them rather than the count (Task 1.13.6, 2026-09-04).** The four that stay prose, each with its durable copy named. **The two `axe-core` pins must match and there is no manifest for them to be compared in** — `e2e` declares 4.13.0 to match the version `@storybook/addon-a11y` resolves, `apps/frontend`'s arrives transitively, and two versions would make the workshop and the browser suite report different numbers for the same page, which makes both untrustworthy rather than one of them wrong; the one-liner is `ls -d node_modules/.pnpm/axe-core@*`, which must print exactly one entry and printed exactly `axe-core@4.13.0` when this was written. **The ruleset now requires two checks keyed on two job names**, so renaming either job in `verify.yml` un-requires it silently — Task 1.10.2's failure mode doubled — and **two jobs restore a cache without saving it**: `verify`'s `e2e` job restores the pnpm store that `verify` saves, and `deploy.yml`'s `check-deployed` job restores the browser cache that `e2e` saves, so removing either saver leaves the other jobs installing cold forever, silently and slowly. **And the deployed check cannot detect both its addresses being wrong in the same direction** — pointed at a stale frontend origin with a matching backend origin it passes green against the wrong site entirely, which is a real limit of an instrument whose whole value is catching values that disagree; the only check for it is a fourth independent copy of the address, which is the thing the two-inputs design exists to avoid.

**The one that became a check is the one whose failure a green run structurally cannot see.** The axe gate's whole value depends on the browser having computed real styles: a renderer that skipped style computation reports **zero violations by being blind**, indistinguishable from success. Task 1.13.4 confirmed the property with a throwaway probe on the runner (the page ground exact, `color-contrast` passing on **65 nodes**, the same number macOS reports) and deleted the probe. `e2e/support/axe.ts`'s `expectTheRendererComputedStyles` now asserts that `color-contrast` appears in axe's `passes` with **more than zero nodes**, called from **both** `expectNoAxeViolations` and `reportAxe`, because `reportAxe` injects the same axe into a second browser and a check on one leaves the other blind. It asserts **presence with nodes** rather than 65, because 65 is a property of this page at this moment and would fail on the next component where "axe evaluated contrast on something" is the claim that matters. Made to fail first, by pointing it at a misspelled rule id: `Expected: > 0 / Received: 0`, with the message naming the consequence.

**The line between the two lists is not effort, and it is worth stating because it will be asked again.** It is whether the thing being checked is **reachable from an assembled instance** — this repository's own rule, the one `apps/backend/src/server.test.ts` was written on. The renderer's styles are; a repository ruleset, a manifest's missing key, a cache saver in another job and a second copy of a hostname are not. **Two counter-examples from the same story belong beside them, because both went the other way**: `scripts/run-deployed-check.mjs` now **guards on the presence of `packages/shared/dist`** — presence and not freshness, the shape Task 1.10.5 settled after building a staleness check and removing it — and the **three-values-must-agree** invariant (`VITE_API_BASE_URL` in `deploy.yml`, the Static Web App's hostname, `CORS_ORIGIN` on the Container App) has existed unnamed since Story 1.11 and is **the first invariant of this kind in the repository that a check closes rather than prose describing**.

**And two files that were expected to join the fifth kind and did not, which is a more interesting entry than another one that is.** `e2e/playwright.config.ts` and `e2e/playwright.deployed.config.ts` are the first tool configuration files here that are **not a gap of any kind**: `e2e/tsconfig.json`'s `include` is `**/*.ts`, so both sit inside the project, `--print-config` reports **168 rules** on them identical to a covered source file, `no-floating-promises` applies, and Prettier formats them. Two instances make that a property of that one `include` line rather than a coincidence. The two new `scripts/*.mjs` files are not gaps either, for the reason the other three are not: ESLint and Prettier read them. What did grow is the fifth kind itself — `deploy.yml` now carries **two** jobs with its schema still checked by nothing.

**Both halves are hosted on Microsoft Azure — the backend and the registry in East US, the frontend in East US 2, and ~~the eventual database in East US~~ **the database in East US 2 as well since Task 2.1.1 (2026-09-04), because East US is `OfferRestricted` for Postgres on this subscription** — and Task 1.11.1 took that as a decision without deploying anything.** The full record — quoted platform limits, rejected alternatives, cost envelope, reversal cost and account facts — is `planning/epic-01-application-foundation/story-11-.../HOSTING.md`, which is to Story 1.11 what `VISUAL-LANGUAGE.md` is to Story 1.4. The frontend is **Azure Static Web Apps** on the Free plan; the backend is **Azure Container Apps** on the Consumption plan with `minReplicas: 1`; the database is **Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server**, named now and provisioned in **Epic 2**. One provider serving both is a decision with three reasons — the database coupling, one identity boundary for the deploy credential, one free-tier envelope — and deliberately **not one service**, because Story 1.3's asymmetry is real: `dist/` needs a file server and the backend's package directory needs a process supervisor. Nothing exists on Azure yet: no resource, no account link, no credential, and no file outside `planning/` was touched, in the same spirit as Task 1.10.1 installing and stopping.

**The frontend host was chosen on Story 1.5's fallback constraint rather than on price, CDN or build integration** — every candidate has those, and only some can express a fallback that is not a blanket catch-all. Static Web Apps' `navigationFallback` takes an `exclude` array, and its documentation carries the worked table: an unmatched path returns `index.html` with a **200**, and a missing file under an excluded folder returns **404**. That is Story 1.5's three constraints and Task 1.6.5's hand-built fallback as a product feature, so `exclude: ["/assets/*"]` is the whole configuration — Task 1.11.4 owns writing it and verifying it against a made-up asset, which is the check that separates it from a catch-all. **It did, and the documentation's table transferred exactly** (2026-09-03): all four routes and a made-up path are 200 with the 1,101-byte `index.html`, `/assets/nope.js` is a 404, and the fallback's own file, `/staticwebapp.config.json`, is not served as content — it comes back as the fallback, so the platform consumes it rather than publishing it. **The one thing the table does not say is where the scope ends**: the exclude covers `/assets/*` and a file added to `apps/frontend/public/` lands at the artefact's root outside it, where a miss is answered with `index.html` and a 200. That is the `vite preview` trap alive in production, and it is one `exclude` entry away whenever somebody adds a favicon. **Cloudflare Pages was rejected on the constraint**: `_redirects` supports only 301/302/303/307/308 plus a 200 rewrite and **no 404 status**, so its fallback is necessarily blanket. **Netlify expresses it as cleanly** (`/assets/* /404.html 404` above `/* /index.html 200`, existing files shadowing rules) and lost only to the one-provider decision, so it is the standing alternative. Two ordering traps for Task 1.11.4, both from the same page: **"Route rules aren't applied on requests that trigger `navigationFallback`"**, so a cache header hung on a route the fallback answers does not apply; and cache policy itself is `globalHeaders` plus a per-route `headers` block, which is configuration rather than code. **Both were confirmed on the deployed site rather than trusted.** `/` and `/investigations` — the second served by the fallback — both carry `Cache-Control: no-cache` from `globalHeaders`, and both hashed assets carry `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` from the route rule. Had the document's policy been written as a route rule it would have applied to `/` and to none of the four routes, which is the worst shape available: configured-looking and inert. **And one property of this host that no documentation page names: a deploy is not atomic.** Measured on two deploys with a continuous poll, the outgoing hashed asset is withdrawn **while the outgoing `index.html` is still being served**, so for about 1.5 seconds a cold load gets a document whose script is a 404; propagation is not even monotonic, with the old asset flicking back to 200 after the new document went live. There is no flag to fix it. **Task 1.11.7 re-confirmed it on the shipping pipeline and found the window holds a SECOND broken state that this reading missed**: before the withdrawal, the **incoming `index.html` is served while the incoming asset is still a 404** — so the document moves ahead of its own asset in both directions, the window is ~2 s over both states, and it **opens at the exact second the `Deploy the frontend` step reports success**, which is why anything checking the deployed page afterwards has to poll rather than check once. Anyone already on the page is unaffected, and `no-cache` on the document means their next navigation revalidates. Uploads also **replace rather than merge**, so a file dropped from the build is gone from the site.

**A merge to `main` deploys both halves and nothing about it re-defines the build (Task 1.11.6).** `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` is triggered by `workflow_run` on `verify` completing and gated on that **run's** conclusion — never a step's, because `continue-on-error` makes a step's `conclusion` read `success` however it exited — plus `event == 'push'` and `head_branch == 'main'`, which together close the hole a fork pull request from a branch called `main` would otherwise open. Every build in it is invoked **by name**, `pnpm build` and `pnpm image`, so there is no `tsc`, no `vite build`, no `docker build` and no `--build-arg` in the file; the platform's own generated workflow was declined outright because it takes an `app_location` and an `output_location` and **builds the site on the deploy side**, which is Story 1.10's forbidden shape wearing a different hat. It is a separate workflow rather than a job in `verify.yml` for three reasons that are all properties of a workflow rather than of a job: the badge would otherwise report the deployment, which `README.md`'s own paragraph disclaims; a cancelled deploy is a half-done rollout, so it needs cancellation **off** and a queue of its own; and the required status check keys on the job name `verify`. Both behaviours were made to happen. A **red `verify` on `main`** left deploy run `33732126041` created and **`skipped`** — and it is a skipped run rather than an absent one, so the gate firing leaves a visible record rather than a silence indistinguishable from a broken trigger. And **two merges 95 s apart did not interleave**: the second run was created at `08:19:02` and its job started at `08:20:17`, three seconds after the first finished, having waited 75 s in the `deploy` concurrency group. The whole deploy is **~2 min 50 s**, so a merge is green in ~90 s and live in ~4 min 20 s; `pnpm build` builds Storybook too, which is time spent on an artefact this workflow does not publish and was **not** narrowed to `vite build`, because that would be this file defining its own build.

**The deploy holds no secret, and that is a stronger statement than "the secret is scoped".** `gh secret list` and `gh variable list` are both empty. GitHub authenticates to Azure with a **federated identity credential (OIDC)** on app registration `marketpulse-github-deploy`, holding `AcrPush` on the registry and `Contributor` on the two apps — scoped per resource rather than to the resource group, which is tighter than `HOSTING.md` originally intended. The container app still pulls with its **own** system-assigned managed identity and `AcrPull`, so CI never needs pull credentials at all, which is the payoff of Task 1.11.3 choosing ACR over the free GHCR arriving one task later. The Static Web Apps **deployment token** is fetched with `az staticwebapp secrets list` at the moment of use and passed through one command's environment with `--no-use-keychain`; that was only reachable because `Azure/static-web-apps-deploy`'s own `action.yml` was **read rather than recalled** — it declares `azure_static_web_apps_api_token` as `required: true` and offers no Azure-credential input, so the official action cannot take an OIDC login and is declined with a reason rather than adopted because every example does it. The three ids in the workflow are literals on purpose: they are identifiers, not credentials, all three are already published in a public repository, and none authorises anything without a token whose claims match the federated subject.

**Two findings from it that will cost somebody a day if they are not read first.** **GitHub presents an ID-qualified OIDC subject for this repository** — `repo:theSmaw@429802/marketpulse@1351035456:ref:refs/heads/main` — and not the documented `repo:owner/repo:ref:…`. The first deploy failed at `azure/login` with `AADSTS700213`, and **the presented subject is readable only from the Azure rejection**: nothing on the GitHub side prints it. Both credentials are kept, and the numeric ids are stable across renames, which is the format's point. And **the pipeline's image is a plain `application/vnd.docker.distribution.manifest.v2+json` with no attestations**, where a local `pnpm image` produces an OCI index carrying a buildx provenance/SBOM attestation — the cause is the image store rather than the recipe, since `docker build -t` uses the `docker` exporter and attestations need one that can carry them, which is Docker Desktop's containerd store locally and nothing on `ubuntu-latest`. Same Dockerfile, same arguments, same runnable bytes, a different envelope. **The stated reversal trigger — a tool refusing the index — did not fire**, because the builder never made one, so "the index digest is the provenance record" is true of a local build and not of a pipeline one; what `deploy.yml` pins and prints is _the digest the tag resolves to_, which is what the platform pulls either way. Accepted rather than chased: the fixes cost a sixth pinned action plus a `--push` mode on a recipe whose "this pushes nothing" is a stated property. The reversal trigger is anything that actually consumes the attestations.

**The backend has an artefact fingerprint now, and the frontend's is finally the deployed one.** Both are records rather than checks — nothing asserts a digest or a hash, because both artefacts are supposed to change — and both `tee` into the log as well as the job summary, because being greppable across runs is the whole argument for having one. The backend's is digest, media type, layer bytes, `/app` size and `dist/` file count read back **from the registry** rather than from the build: `60,247,220 B` of layers and config, `/app` 16,700 KB, 32 files in `dist/`. That is a **fourth** correct size for one image beside `docker save`'s 60,266,496 B, the platform manifest's 60,239,874 B and the pull event's 59,768,832 B — different boundaries, not different images; say which one you mean. On the frontend side, `verify` still builds without `VITE_API_BASE_URL` and **now says so in its own summary**, while the deploy builds with it and fingerprints that build, and the deployed bundle downloaded back from the CDN is byte-identical to Task 1.11.5's hand deploy from a Mac. **The divergence is two files rather than the one that was recorded**: the JavaScript differs by 72 bytes, and `index.html` differs at an **identical** 1,101 B because it carries the hashed script filename — so a size comparison reports the document unchanged and it is not. Read the hash.

**Preview environments are declined as an action, and the frontend's 1.5-second transient is accepted as one.** Nothing in `deploy.yml` creates a preview and the deploy only runs on a push to `main`; a preview would get its own origin, `CORS_ORIGIN` holds exactly one string, and a wildcard admitting them would admit every Static Web App in the region — so a preview is a page that loads perfectly and cannot call the backend. The non-atomic upload now happens once per merge instead of once per hand deploy, and **deploying the frontend only when `apps/frontend` changed was rejected on correctness rather than effort**: the bundle inlines `packages/shared`, carries `VITE_API_BASE_URL` from the workflow file, and is produced by a `vite.config.ts` and a lockfile that both live outside `apps/frontend`, so a path filter naming that directory would skip deploys that genuinely changed the artefact and leave the site silently stale. A bounded 1.5-second window beats an unbounded silent one.

**The deploy does not gate a merge and the ruleset is unchanged**, which is recorded here because nothing in the tree would show it either way. It runs _after_ a merge, so requiring it would gate on something that cannot have happened yet, and `workflow_run` runs are not attributable to a pull request as checks in the first place. A future reader finding the deploy job absent from ruleset `main` should read that as the decision.

**Epic 3 and Epic 10 are constrained by different mechanisms, and conflating them is the mistake the decision record exists to prevent.** The Alpaca WebSocket is **outbound** — our server dials Alpaca, so the connection never traverses the ingress proxy in the direction a request timeout governs, and no ingress limit reaches it. What threatens it is the replica not existing: Container Apps' documented default is `minReplicas: 0` with an HTTP trigger, so **`minReplicas: 1` is a required setting and not a tuning knob**, and left at the default the socket dies with the replica. Epic 10's agent stream is **inbound** SSE, so the ingress limit is exactly what applies: default HTTP ingress is **"Request time out is 240 seconds"**, and the premium-ingress table names the same number as an _idle_ timeout (minimum 4 minutes, maximum 30), which is what establishes it as a ceiling on silence rather than on connection age. **So Epic 10's SSE stream must emit something at least every four minutes** — one keep-alive line, cheap because it was written down before the stream existed. Premium ingress would raise it to 30 minutes and requires a dedicated workload profile at a minimum of two nodes; recorded and declined. Story 1.2's 5-second shutdown ceiling fits inside Container Apps' 30-second default `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`, and **PID 1 signal delivery is still Task 1.11.2's to close** — the platform choice does not settle it.

**The free-tier compute that sleeps was rejected on measurement rather than reputation, and the numbers are the reason.** App Service **Free F1** has three independent disqualifications, any one fatal: **5 web sockets per instance**, **60 CPU-minutes per day**, and no Always On, since "In the Free and Shared tiers, an app receives CPU minutes on a shared VM instance and can't scale out". **App Service Basic B1** (~$13/mo) is genuinely viable — Always On from Basic up, **350 web sockets per instance** — and is the standing alternative if the 240 s idle timeout ever binds, at which point two traps apply: web sockets must be explicitly enabled (`WEBSOCKETS_ENABLED`) and the idle timeout is raised via `WEBSITES_CONTAINER_IDLE_TIMEOUT` up to 1800 s. Off Azure, **Render Free** spins down after **"15 minutes without receiving any inbound traffic"** — of which an outbound socket to Alpaca is none, so it is fatal at Epic 3 exactly as F1 is — while paid Starter at $7/mo is the best non-Azure answer and has the most explicit WebSocket documentation of any candidate. **Fly.io** is the cheapest always-on compute ($2.02/mo for `shared-cpu-1x` at 256 MB) and states the hazard plainly — "The proxy looks at inbound traffic. It does not look inside the container" — but its managed Postgres starts at **$38/mo**, which is what the database coupling removes it on. **Cloud Run** was rejected on a hard cap: the request timeout maxes at 60 minutes and "WebSockets streams are HTTP requests, which are still subject to the request timeout".

**This is not a free deployment, and the estimate carries a stated expiry.** The Consumption free grant is **180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds and 2 million HTTP requests per subscription per calendar month**; a 30-day month is 2,592,000 seconds, so an always-on 0.25 vCPU / 0.5 GiB replica consumes **648,000 vCPU-seconds — 3.6× the grant**. What keeps the bill to a few dollars is the **idle rate**, which this configuration is eligible for because it is at its minimum replica count, and whose conditions include the replica "receiving less than 1,000 bytes per second of network traffic". **Epic 3 breaks that condition on purpose**: a replica holding a live feed bills at the active rate through every market session. **Do not carry the figure into Epic 3 — re-take it there.** Health probe requests are not billable, which matters because Task 1.11.3 adds one. Static Web Apps is Free with 100 GB of bandwidth a month and **"Overage bandwidth: Unavailable"**, so exceeding it is a cut-off rather than an invoice — a different failure shape from the paid plans, against a 355,985 B artefact.

**The database question was answered by naming the service now and provisioning it in Epic 2, and the provider half is what had to be settled here.** Deferring is only free because the backend's platform has a managed Postgres adjacent to it; a backend chosen with no database in scope would have made Epic 2 a second vendor and a cross-network hop. The subscription is a **new Azure free account**, so the offer is 12 months of Burstable **B1MS** at up to 750 hours a month plus 32 GB of storage and 32 GB of backup — and the cost of deferring is stated rather than hidden: **that window starts at signup**, so every month before Epic 2 spends part of it. Two things Epic 2 must not rediscover: the tier stays **B1MS** to stay inside the offer, and the **networking mode has to be decided before creation** because the service cannot change it afterwards — public access with a firewall rule is the cheap path, private access via VNet integration is the correct one and costs the Container Apps environment a custom VNet, which is not something to retrofit under a running environment.

**The reversal cost is seven items and explicitly not "one YAML file".** Story 1.10 could say that only because the pipeline runs `pnpm verify` by name and defines nothing of its own; deployment cannot be that cheap. Moving hosts means: the container definition Task 1.11.2 produces; **two platform configurations**, of which `staticwebapp.config.json` is the least portable thing in the tree because `navigationFallback`, `globalHeaders` and the route `headers` are that product's own vocabulary and every host spells the same three intentions differently; two deploy jobs with two actions and two credentials; the federated credential and its role assignment, neither of which is in this tree; `CORS_ORIGIN`, because it names the frontend's origin; **a rebuild of the frontend**, because `VITE_API_BASE_URL` is statically substituted at build time so "point it at the new backend" is a build rather than a setting; and two published URLs. The sixth is the one that surprises people, and it is Story 1.6's finding arriving in production.

**The deployed environment is public, and that is a decision with a reason beyond cost.** IP restrictions and private endpoints are marked "Unavailable" on the Static Web Apps Free plan, and **Task 1.11.5's cross-origin check cannot be done with `curl`** — Story 1.8 measured that a string origin makes the server assert `access-control-allow-origin` unconditionally and the browser is the only party that compares — so anything standing between a browser and the frontend makes that criterion unverifiable. What makes public acceptable is that nothing deployed holds a credential: no authentication, no user data, and a backend whose entire surface is `GET /health`. **That stops being true the moment Epic 2's Alpaca key is on the platform.** Both hostnames are documented and stable: the backend is `<APP_NAME>.<ENVIRONMENT_UNIQUE_ID>.<REGION>.azurecontainerapps.io`, where the identifier belongs to the _environment_ and so survives revisions and deploys, and Static Web Apps preview environments are `<DEFAULT_HOST_NAME>-<BRANCH_OR_ENVIRONMENT_NAME>.<LOCATION>.azurestaticapps.net` with two documented limits — custom domains do not work with them, and they are not geo-distributed.

**Rollback is asymmetric between the two halves, and Task 1.11.7 executed both — correcting this paragraph's mechanism in the process.** ~~Container Apps keeps revisions, so a backend rollback is shifting traffic to the previous revision, and revision label FQDNs (`<APP_NAME>---<LABEL>.…`) are the mechanism.~~ **That is refused on this app**: `az containerapp ingress traffic set` reports _"configured for single revision. Set revision mode to multiple in order to set ingress traffic"_, so traffic splitting and the label FQDNs both require reconfiguring the app before they can be used — during an incident, on the thing that is already misbehaving. What works is **`az containerapp update --image <the previous digest>`**, which needs only a digest that every deploy run prints into its own summary, and which was executed in **43 s**; it creates a new revision rather than reactivating the old one, and **the next merge silently undoes it** (watched happening: the rolled-back image was replaced with the merge's, with nothing warning anywhere). So the backend's rollback buys time and the durable fix is a revert commit, exactly as the frontend's is. `workflow_dispatch` on `deploy.yml` is a **re-deploy and not a rollback** — it checks out `main`, so pressing it after a bad merge deploys the bad merge again. Static Web Apps has no revision history at all on the Free plan, so the **frontend's rollback is a revert commit through `verify` and the pipeline**, measured at **3 min 42 s** from pressing merge — making the cheap half ~5x the slower one to recover, while the fast half is the one that expires. The deploy credential's intended shape is a **federated identity credential (OIDC)** scoped to this repository with a role assignment on the resource group, rather than a long-lived service-principal secret or a per-service deployment token, on the same principle that pins every action to a commit SHA. Static Web Apps' default GitHub integration issues a long-lived deployment token instead; if Task 1.11.4 ends up using one it is a **divergence to record with its reason**, not a default to adopt silently. **The subscription and tenant ids, the resource group and environment names, both published URLs and the credential's subject are owed by Task 1.11.3** — `HOSTING.md` holds the table and says that finding that paragraph still standing means the work was not done, which is the same shape as Story 1.10's ruleset being invisible in a diff.

**The backend has a container image and it was run outside the workspace before any platform saw it (Task 1.11.2).** `pnpm image` is the command — `docker build -f apps/backend/Dockerfile --platform linux/amd64 --build-arg NODE_VERSION="$(cat .nvmrc)" -t marketpulse-backend:"<tag>" .` — and it is a root script rather than a documented incantation because three of its arguments are load-bearing and all three are easy to omit. (**Task 1.11.3 moved it into `scripts/build-image.mjs`**, which computes the tag rather than reading `HEAD` blindly; the flags are unchanged.) `image` was checked against `pnpm help -a` before being claimed and is **not** a pnpm built-in, unlike `clean`, `env`, `config`, `start` and `test`. It builds and tags locally and **pushes nothing**; the registry, the resource group and every Azure fact are Task 1.11.3's. Two files are new, `apps/backend/Dockerfile` and a root `.dockerignore`, and neither is read by any tool in `pnpm verify` — see the gaps above. No dependency was added, `pnpm-workspace.yaml` is byte-unchanged, `allowBuilds` still has its single `esbuild` entry, and the frontend artefact is byte-identical at 343,658 B / md5 `cba2825c…`.

**`pnpm deploy` needs `--legacy` on this workspace, and the modern alternative would have cost the dev loop.** Without it, `pnpm deploy --filter @marketpulse/backend --prod <dir>` fails outright with `[ERR_PNPM_DEPLOY_NONINJECTED_WORKSPACE]`: pnpm 10 and later only deploy from workspaces with `inject-workspace-packages=true`. Setting `injectWorkspacePackages: true` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` is the path pnpm is steering towards and it changes **every developer's install** — `@marketpulse/shared` stops being a symlink into `packages/shared` and becomes a hard-linked copy taken at install time, so a `tsc -b --watch` rebuild of shared would stop reaching the backend until the next install, which is exactly what `pnpm dev`'s three watchers exist to do. `--legacy` is a flag on one command in one file and changes nothing for anyone not deploying. It prints `[WARN] Shared workspace lockfile detected but configuration forces legacy deploy implementation.` on every run, which reads like something to fix and is not. The reversal trigger is pnpm removing the flag, at which point the injected mode needs a dev-loop answer **before** it is adopted.

**`pnpm deploy` copies the whole package directory, and a `files` field is what stops it — this is new since anyone last looked.** Read from the output rather than assumed: the first artefact was 16 M holding `node_modules/` (15 M), the **`coverage/` HTML report** (324 K), `dist/` (316 K), `src/` (144 K), `scripts/dev.sh`, both Vitest configs, `tsconfig.json`, `tsconfig.tsbuildinfo` and `.env.example`; `@marketpulse/shared` arrived the same way at 360 K with its own `src/`, `coverage/` and configs. **The recorded "12 files for 3 test files" is now 16 for 4** — Task 1.10.5's `index.process.test.ts` arrived after that figure was written, and `tsc -b` emits four files per test — with 10 more in shared. None of it is a correctness problem; "the deployment ships the coverage report and the test sources" is simply not a thing to discover in Epic 2. Both manifests now carry `"files": ["dist", "!dist/**/*.test.*"]`, which `pnpm deploy` honours, negation included: `apps/backend/dist` went **316 K and 16 test files → 188 K and zero**, and shared went **360 K → 112 K** holding `dist` and `package.json` only. `package.json` is included whatever `files` says, which is what `/health` depends on. The `.d.ts`, `.d.ts.map` and `.js.map` files are **kept** on a measurement rather than by omission — 6,739 B of a 96,265 B `dist` for the declarations, and source maps are worth 22 KB for a stack trace that names a `.ts` line. **`files` affects `pnpm deploy` and `pnpm pack` and nothing else**: verified that `apps/backend/node_modules/@marketpulse/shared` is still the workspace symlink, that `pnpm verify` exits 0, and that the frontend bundle did not move.

**`@marketpulse/shared` arrives as real files, and one dangling symlink survives into the image.** In the artefact it is a **relative** symlink into `node_modules/.pnpm/@marketpulse+shared@file+packages+shared/…`, and that path holds real content — 1,257 bytes of `dist/index.js` inside the image. Outside the image the same files are hard links to the workspace's own (identical inode, link count 2), which is still a real file and survives a `tar`, a `COPY` and the workspace being deleted. What does **not** resolve is `/app/node_modules/.pnpm/node_modules/@marketpulse/backend -> ../../../../../repo/apps/backend`, the deployed package's own self-reference in pnpm's hoist directory pointing at the builder stage's `/repo`. It is inert — nothing resolves `@marketpulse/backend` by name, and the only occurrence of that string in `dist/` is a comment in `config.js` — and it is recorded rather than repaired, because repairing it means deleting a file out of pnpm's own layout. So "the artefact is self-contained" is true of everything that runs and not true of every file in it.

**The start command is `node dist/index.js` and `pnpm start` is the named alternative.** It works — Task 1.2.5 measured `pnpm run` forwarding `SIGTERM`, waiting for the child and propagating its exit code, so the wrapper is signal-transparent and nothing is being avoided. It is rejected because it puts a package manager, a Corepack fetch and a script-resolution step into a runtime image to run the command it would have run anyway, and a second process between the platform's `SIGTERM` and the server for no gain. The runtime stage therefore carries no Corepack and no pnpm.

**PID 1 was the open question Story 1.2 could not close, and the received wisdom about it is wrong on this image.** The expected finding was that a shell-form `CMD` makes `/bin/sh` PID 1 and swallows `SIGTERM`. Built and signalled: **it is false here**, because busybox `sh` `exec`s a lone command and the Node base image's own `docker-entrypoint.sh` ends in `exec "$@"`, so the chain collapses and `node` is PID 1 either way. What actually swallows the signal is a shell with work left after the server. Three forms, one image, `docker stop -t 15`:

| `sh -c` argument               | PID 1  | exit    | stop took  | drained |
| ------------------------------ | ------ | ------- | ---------- | ------- |
| `node dist/index.js`           | node   | 0       | 0 s        | yes     |
| `node dist/index.js; echo bye` | **sh** | **137** | **15.0 s** | **no**  |
| `exec node dist/index.js`      | node   | 0       | 0 s        | yes     |

The middle row is the predicted symptom exactly: a whole grace period of nothing, then `SIGKILL` (137 = 128 + 9), with no `signal received` and no `shutdown complete`. On Container Apps' 30-second grace that is 30 seconds per replica per revision, reading as slowness rather than as an error. **So the shell form here is accidentally right rather than right**, and it stops being right the moment anyone appends a second command or wraps the start in a script that does not `exec`. `CMD ["node", "dist/index.js"]` is right by construction. With the shipping image `pid` is **1** in every log record.

**The Node version is tied to `.nvmrc` by a build arg with no default, and `engineStrict` is a backstop rather than the tie.** `ARG NODE_VERSION`, fed from `.nvmrc` by `pnpm image` and used by **both** stages, so builder and runtime cannot disagree and the version is written once in the repository. A default value would be a second copy that silently wins whenever the flag is forgotten, which is the drift the arrangement exists to prevent — the cost is BuildKit's `InvalidDefaultArgInFrom` warning on every build, which is the arrangement working. Both failure modes were made to happen: no `--build-arg` is `failed to parse stage name "node:-alpine": invalid reference format`, and `NODE_VERSION=22.20.0` is `[ERR_PNPM_UNSUPPORTED_ENGINE] … Expected version: >=24.20.0 <25` at `pnpm install` in the builder.
