It has been seen to fail — move a `.stories.tsx` aside and it exits 1 naming the file and the path it wanted. That matters: a check that has never failed is a check that has never been tested, which is the same problem as the placeholder `test` scripts one paragraph up.

**`pnpm ready` is the seventh root script and deliberately not a seventh `verify` step (Task 1.8.4).** `scripts/check-ready.mjs`, 317 lines, no dependency, polling both services in parallel for up to 15 s at 250 ms — warm it answers in **0.29 s** (median of five; Task 1.8.4 read 0.30 s and Task 1.8.6 0.33 s), and started in the same breath as `pnpm dev` it answers in **0.87 s** having waited out the startup, which is Task 1.8.4's figure reproduced exactly twice and Task 1.8.6's 0.86 s. That is the same number three times across three tasks; treat a first run of 1.4 s as the outlier it was. Against a **half-pair** — a squatter on 3000 — it prints `✗ backend … NO_RESPONSE` beside a ticked frontend, says "Something is holding 127.0.0.1:3000 and not answering. That is not this server", and exits **1**. It exists because of the half-pair above: a backend that fails to bind leaves `pnpm dev` running and green-looking, so "look at the terminal" is not an answer. It is **not** in `verify` and must not become one — `verify` runs with no servers up, where "nothing is running" is the honest answer rather than a failure — and it is `.mjs` in `scripts/` so ESLint and Prettier read it, which `apps/backend/scripts/dev.sh` still is not. `ready` was checked against `pnpm help -a`'s full command list before being claimed: it is not a built-in, unlike `clean`, `env`, `config`, `start` and `test`.

**Four things about that check that each broke an earlier version of it, and three of them are traps for anything else probing these servers.** **The backend's address comes from the built `dist/config.js`**, exactly as `check-env-example.mjs` does, so `PORT` and `HOST` are respected — `HOST=0.0.0.0` is rewritten to `127.0.0.1` for dialling, the same rewrite Fastify's startup line performs, because a wildcard is bound and not dialled; an invalid value is reported as the plain line the server itself would print and treated as "not ready". **The frontend's origin is `CORS_ORIGIN` rather than a second copy of `5173`** — the pin is already there, `env:check` already keeps its default honest, and dialling it means a dev server moved without the allowlist moving with it is reported by name instead of appearing in the browser as `TypeError: Failed to fetch`. **Vite's dev server never 404s, so no HTTP status can judge the frontend**: with `packages/shared` moved aside, `GET /` is 200 (Task 1.8.1), **`/src/main.tsx` is also 200**, and so is `AppHeader.tsx` — because Vite transforms one module per request and `AppHeader`'s `@marketpulse/shared` import is **type-only and erased**; the first module with a _value_ import of the shared package, `src/routes/MarketOverview.tsx`, is the one that 500s. Worse, a module path that does not exist comes back **200 `text/html`**, the SPA fallback, which passed an earlier version of this check outright — so the discriminator is the **content type**, `text/javascript` for a real module and anything HTML for the fallback. And **a socket that accepts and never answers hangs `fetch` forever**, so an overall deadline never fires; a 2 s `AbortSignal.timeout()` per attempt is what makes "something is on the port and it is not this server" a distinct diagnosis from `ECONNREFUSED`. One cosmetic defect in it, found by Task 1.8.6 and **fixed in Task 1.8.7** rather than inherited: with two invalid configuration keys the reported error indented its first line and not its second, because `config.ts`'s multi-line message was interpolated after a single fixed indent. It now indents per line. The single-key case is unchanged.

**Node's `fetch` is not caught by the address-family split and `curl` is, so the script and the README have different correct answers (Task 1.8.4).** From `fetch`, `http://localhost:5173/` and `http://[::1]:5173/` both work while `127.0.0.1:5173` is refused; `http://localhost:3000/health` and `127.0.0.1:3000` both work while `[::1]:3000` is refused. So `localhost` is safe from Node for **both** services — undici tries both families — while `curl` takes what it is given. Any documented `curl` names the family explicitly; anything written in Node does not have to.

**The workshop loads the same cascade the application does, and this is deliberate rather than incidental.** `.storybook/preview.tsx` imports `tokens.css` → `market.css` → `base.css` in that order — not alphabetical, same as `main.tsx` — sets `data-theme="light"` on the document element, and calls `getTokens()` as the same startup assertion. So `getTokens()` now runs in two places, and a token declared in TypeScript but missing from the stylesheets throws in the workshop exactly as it does in the application. A component that renders correctly in one and wrongly in the other is a cascade problem, and it should not be possible to have one without seeing it.

**The one place the workshop and the application deliberately differ is the router, and it is one decorator in one file.** `preview.tsx` wraps every story in a `MemoryRouter` — memory rather than browser, because the workshop is an iframe with no address bar and a story handed the browser's history could navigate the whole Storybook UI. Its entry comes from a `route` story parameter, which is what lets `AppHeader`'s current-route states be stories; they cannot be rows in a permutation grid, because the active link comes from routing context rather than a prop and two `MemoryRouter`s cannot be nested. Task 1.4.5 reused `vite.config.ts` untouched so there was exactly one place the build lived, and this decorator is a second place where the application's context is described — keeping it to one decorator in one file is the containment. Note the decorator is **named** (`const withRouter: Decorator`) rather than inline: that is the TS2883 idiom, and nothing here would catch a violation since the file is outside every tsconfig.

**The a11y panel reports, and its tab badge does not mean what it looks like.** `@storybook/addon-a11y` runs axe per story and never fails a build — the `test` parameter that would do so drives Storybook's Vitest integration, which Story 1.4 deliberately did not adopt. On `SecurityRow`'s 36-row grid: **0 violations, 17 passes, 1 inconclusive** — and the `1` on the tab is the _inconclusive_, not a violation. The inconclusive is the interesting part: it is `color-contrast` over 24 nodes, every one of them a direction arrow (`<span aria-hidden="true">▲</span>`), with axe's reason "Element content contains only non-text characters". **Automated tooling declines to judge the exact element carrying the non-colour encoding.** That is why Task 1.4.4's manual contrast and colour-blindness measurements are the record, and why Epic 15's review cannot be an axe run.

**The panel produced its first real violation in Task 1.5.3, and the permutation-grid convention caused it.** `AppHeader` renders a `<header>` and a `<nav>` — a banner landmark and a navigation landmark — so its `AllPermutations` grid put six banners on one page and axe reported `landmark-no-duplicate-banner` and `landmark-unique`, both moderate, while every single-state story reported **0 violations, 13 passes, 0 inconclusive**. The application renders exactly one header, so the finding is the grid's rather than the component's, and both rules are disabled **on that one story and nowhere else** via `parameters.a11y.config.rules` — a permanent badge trains the next author to ignore the badge, which is worse than the finding. Expect this for every landmark component: **the permutation grid and landmark uniqueness are in direct conflict, and the grid is the one that gives.**

**Task 1.5.4 took the landmark decision the regions forced, and it is the first time this application has been measured by axe outside Storybook.** Every region is a named `region` landmark — `aria-labelledby` pointing at the heading it already has, with the id from `useId()` so two regions of the same name cannot collide. The alternative was plain `<div>`s, leaving the landmark set as the chrome's banner and navigation, which is cheaper and gives a keyboard or screen-reader user nothing to jump between on the screen the product opens on. Measured with axe 4.13.0 against the built page on a static host: the landing route is **0 violations, 37 passes, 1 inconclusive** and the other four routes are **0 / 25 / 0** each. `landmark-unique` passes. The one inconclusive is the known one and has not changed — `color-contrast` over two `aria-hidden` direction arrows, "Element content contains only non-text characters".

**A render failure is contained to the box it happened in, and there are three boxes (Task 1.7.6).** One boundary around `AppHeader`, one around the route outlet, and one **inside** each `Region`, wrapping the content slot. React uses the nearest boundary, so a failure in a region's contents never reaches the outlet; the outlet's boundary is for the four routes that are deliberately a single area, where the outlet _is_ the affected region, and for a failure in the landing route's own frame. Inside rather than around the `<section>` is the decision that matters: a boundary outside it replaces the heading along with the contents, so a failed box loses its name, its landmark and its place in §9's grid. Inside, all three survive and a failed region is a labelled box with a problem in it. Every placement was exercised with a temporary throwing probe and the tree rebuilds to the same hash with the probe gone. The header's boundary is the one that would look optional and is not: without it, a throwing `AppHeader` leaves `#root` with **zero children** and an empty `<body>`, because it is the one component with nothing above it. Its cost is stated rather than discovered — the fallback replaces the `<header>`, so a broken chrome takes the banner landmark and the navigation with it.

**`ErrorBoundary` is the codebase's only class component and it does two things, neither of which is reporting.** `getDerivedStateFromError` is handed the error and deliberately does not keep it — the state is a boolean — which is what makes "the fallback never shows the error" structural rather than a habit, the same move `apiError()` makes on the backend. And the reset increments a counter used as the children's `key`, so recovery **remounts** rather than re-renders: clearing the flag alone re-renders a child still holding the state that broke it, and the user clicks a button that visibly does nothing. Recovery is never a page reload, because a reload discards the rest of a working screen. Verified in the browser: `performance.timeOrigin` unchanged, one `navigation` entry, the alert gone and the region rendering its real content. **`react-error-boundary` 6.1.4 was built before it was rejected** — +932 B and +1 module — and the deciding finding was not the bytes: its `resetErrorBoundary()` clears the error state without remounting too, so a real reset still means supplying `resetKeys` with your own counter. It is a well-tested wrapper around a `key`-based remount you still have to write, plus a second vocabulary beside four props.

**What a boundary does not catch is the frontend's half of Task 1.7.5's split, and the answer went the other way.** A boundary catches errors thrown during render, in lifecycle methods and in constructors. It catches nothing thrown in an event handler, a `setTimeout` or a promise callback — and neither does React's `onUncaughtError`. Measured: a button whose `onClick` throws leaves every region rendering normally, no fallback anywhere and **no report at all**; a `window` `error` listener added from the console was the only thing that saw it. That listener is deliberately **not** installed. The backend's `process.on("uncaughtException")` earned its place by moving a crash out of raw stderr and into the log stream every other record goes to — the change was the _stream_ — and a browser has no second stream: an uncaught error is already in the console with its stack, which is exactly where a report would go. So a listener would repeat what is there while also catching every browser extension and third-party script on the page. Story 1.12's server endpoint is the reversal trigger.

**All three `createRoot` error options are wired, to `src/report-error.ts`, and wiring them replaces React's own message rather than adding to it.** Measured: one console entry per caught error, ours, with the full component stack, and React's "The above error occurred in ..." absent. So it is a choice of wording, not an extra line of noise, and what it buys is one place to change when there is somewhere to send a report. An `uncaught` report means a boundary is missing rather than a fallback being ugly. **The `StrictMode` double-report that Story 1.7 warned about did not happen** — a render throw caught by a boundary produced exactly one `onCaughtError` report in development with `StrictMode` on, because the first of the two constructor calls aborts that render pass and React reports the failure once. The warning still stands for anything counting renders; it does not stand here, so no de-duplicator was written.

**The landmark conflict was predicted three times and does not exist, and the reason is the useful part.** Story 1.5, Task 1.7.6's brief and the first draft of `Region.stories.tsx` all expected `landmark-unique` to fire on a grid of regions the way six banners did for `AppHeader` — the disable was written before it was measured, and then removed. Three `region` landmarks in one grid report **0 violations** and `landmark-unique` is in the _passes_ list on all three nodes. `landmark-unique` keys on role **and accessible name together**, so it fires on landmarks that are indistinguishable, not on landmarks that repeat; `AppHeader`'s six banners were six copies of one anonymous thing, while a grid reviewing regions gives each cell a different name because that is what a region is. **The permutation grid and landmark uniqueness only conflict for a component whose landmark has no name.** So Task 1.5.3's `AppHeader` disable is still right and the general rule stated there was too broad.

**axe against the built page is unchanged by any of it, and the failed states were measured too.** axe-core 4.13.0 on a dumb static host: the landing route is **0 violations, 37 passes, 1 inconclusive** and the other four routes **0 / 25 / 0**, identical to Task 1.5.4. The same page with a region failed is **0 / 41 / 1** — four more passes, no violations — and with the header failed **0 / 37 / 1**, with `<header>` and `<nav>` gone from the document and all four regions still there. The one inconclusive has not moved in three tasks: `color-contrast` over two `aria-hidden` direction arrows.

**`pnpm --filter @marketpulse/frontend dev` needs `packages/shared` built first, and it does not say so at startup at all.** The `Failed to run dependency scan … Are they installed?` line this file used to record is **stale** — Task 1.8.1 re-measured it. After a `pnpm clean` the filtered dev server starts, reports `ready in 96 ms` and serves `/` as a clean **200** with nothing wrong in the terminal; the failure appears only when a browser requests a module that has a **value** import of the shared package, as a `vite:import-analysis` pre-transform error: `Failed to resolve import "@marketpulse/shared" from "src/routes/MarketOverview.tsx"`. Root `pnpm dev` never shows either message, because the shared watcher is one of its three loops. Build, then filter — and check it with `pnpm ready` rather than a request of your own, for the reasons in `scripts/check-ready.mjs`.

**Stories are inside `tsc -b`'s program and outside the bundle, and the invariant is worth re-checking after anything that touches the entry graph.** `.stories.tsx` files sit under `src/`, so they typecheck, they lint under the full type-aware pass and the React Compiler rules, and the `.js` import-extension rule applies to them. They are unreachable from `index.html`, so `vite build` still emits **no chunk of its own for them** — the artefact's file count is four and every one of the four is accounted for elsewhere — and no story string appears in the output — verified by grepping the emitted JavaScript for `AllPermutations`, the story titles and the workshop stylesheet's class names, all zero. `src/components/stories.module.css` is the file to watch: it is workshop furniture, and the day it appears in `dist/assets/*.css` something has imported a story from application code.

**The Base UI seam is a popover and not a tooltip, and that is a finding rather than a preference.** `src/components/Popover/Popover.tsx` is the only file in the application that imports `@base-ui/react`. It was written as a tooltip first; measured against the built workshop, Base UI's tooltip renders **no `role="tooltip"`** and wires **no `aria-describedby`** — deliberately, and its own documentation says as much: a tooltip is for sighted users, and "if the description is important to understanding the element, don't hide it behind a tooltip". The first thing behind this seam is an anomaly score's explanation, which PRODUCT_SPEC.md §11 makes mandatory and therefore important by definition. The popover measures `role="dialog"` with both `aria-labelledby` and `aria-describedby` present. The cost is that an explanation is a click rather than a glance; if a genuine _hint_ is ever needed, it comes back as a **second** wrapper rather than as a looser version of this one. The general lesson, which Epic 15 should inherit: "accessible primitives" is a property of each primitive, not of a library.

**The wrapper rule now stands on its own reason, because the check behind it was retired.** Base UI was chosen over the lighter Radix on an intended later swap to an external shared component library, and that library's exports have never been read — Task 1.5.1 asked directly and they are **not available**, so the check is closed as unresolvable rather than deferred a fourth time. What replaces it: build against Base UI's interfaces and our own, keep every usage behind a thin wrapper in `src/components/<Name>/`, and make a wrapper's props **our** vocabulary rather than a re-export of the primitive's — `Popover` is the model at 30 lines of assembly with nothing of `@base-ui/react` in its public type. The count of files importing `@base-ui/react` is the figure worth watching; it is **one**. The swap cost is then the wrapper files rather than the application, which is the property the wrapper layer was bought for. Radix and react-aria-components remain the standing alternatives if Epic 15 finds against Base UI.

**Base UI's real cost landed here, and it is larger than the story's headline number.** The application bundle went from 196.36 kB to **300.09 kB across 193 modules** when the popover arrived — about 104 kB — against Task 1.4.1's measurement of Radix being lighter by 37 kB for one primitive. That reversal was taken knowingly and on a constraint no spike could produce; the number is recorded so nobody re-derives it or reads the growth as a regression. The stylesheet went 6.22 kB → **7.21 kB** (1.94 kB gzipped — the whole design language, both token layers and five components), and the artefact is still three files. Note Task 1.4.5's own record says 293.06 kB and 7.05 kB: those were measured mid-task, before the last edits it shipped. **Task 1.4.6's clean-build figures above are the story's baseline**, and they reproduce byte-for-byte across rebuilds.

**The router is React Router 8.3.1, and two things about it are traps.** `react-router-dom` is **not** the package — it is stranded at 7.18.3 and v8 ships everything from `react-router` itself, so installing the familiar name gets a major version behind. And it peers `react >=19.2.7` against our 19.2.8, which is the **narrowest peer margin in this repository**: a React downgrade is now a router failure too. It is a dependency of `apps/frontend` and there is **no plugin and no root tooling** — declarative mode is a library import and nothing else, and `@react-router/dev` (framework mode) is deliberately not adopted; if it ever is, it is root tooling beside Vite. Task 1.5.1 measured the cost against the Story 1.4 baseline before installing it, so the number is attributed to the decision rather than discovered later: **+37.73 kB and +60 modules** (300.09 → 337.82 kB, 193 → 253), against TanStack Router's +74.58 kB and +83 modules. `allowBuilds` did not fire — `esbuild@0.28.2` is still the only install script in the tree. **Task 1.5.2 then mounted it and the real figure is slightly above the spike's:** 193 → **261 modules**, 300.09 → **340.10 kB** (111.35 kB gzipped), stylesheet 7.21 → **8.62 kB**, still **three files**. The +8 modules and +2.28 kB over the spike are the five route modules, `NavLink` and the path table. **Task 1.5.3's chrome added +2 modules and +0.73 kB** — 261 → **263 modules**, 340.10 → **340.83 kB** (111.59 kB gzipped), stylesheet 8.62 → **9.13 kB** — which is `AppHeader.tsx` and its stylesheet and nothing else: no new dependency, and the chrome is a `<nav>` and four links rather than a menu primitive, so the count of files importing `@base-ui/react` is still one. **Task 1.5.4's regions added +2 modules and +1.25 kB** — 263 → **265 modules**, 340.83 → **342.08 kB** (111.97 kB gzipped), stylesheet 9.13 → **9.82 kB**, still **three files** — which is `Region.tsx`, its stylesheet and the grid. Declarative mode only — `<BrowserRouter>`, `<Routes>`, `<Route>` — and no `React.lazy` anywhere, which is what keeps the file count at three. **Task 1.5.5 owned splitting and rejected it, with the artefact built both ways, so 265 / 342.08 kB / 9.82 kB / 3 is also this story's final figure — and it was right.** ~~Two corrections to that line, both found by re-measuring rather than citing.~~ **Both of those "corrections" were themselves wrong, and Task 1.7.7 rebuilt four old commits to find out.** Task 1.7.3 saw its own tree at 342,017 B, compared it against Story 1.5's recorded 342.08 kB and concluded the record was 0.07 kB high; Task 1.7.6 saw 267 modules, compared it against the recorded 265 and concluded the count had been mis-recorded in Task 1.5.4. Neither had moved by mistake. Rebuilt:

| Commit                | Modules | JS bytes    | Hash                |
| --------------------- | ------- | ----------- | ------------------- |
| Story 1.5.6 `a22b13a` | 265     | 342,080     | `index-z9p5vXHu.js` |
| Story 1.6.7 `ebf495d` | 265     | **342,017** | `index-BAidohu3.js` |
| Pre-1.7.3 `dd043cd^`  | 265     | 342,017     | `index-BAidohu3.js` |
| Post-1.7.5 `1c2b6f9`  | **267** | 342,017     | `index-BAidohu3.js` |

So **Story 1.6 changed the bundle by −63 bytes** with the module count unchanged (its lockfile is byte-identical to Story 1.5's, so the change is in source), and **Task 1.7.3 added +2 modules while changing zero bytes and keeping the same hash** — `api-error.ts` and `request-id.ts` enter the graph through the shared barrel and are tree-shaken entirely out of the output, so `INTERNAL_ERROR` appears in neither `dist/` nor `storybook-static/`. Two lessons, and the second is the one with teeth. **A module can join the graph and cost nothing**, so "modules transformed" and the emitted bundle are two numbers rather than one. And **a figure that has moved looks exactly like a figure that was mis-recorded** — only rebuilding the old commit tells them apart, which is what neither correction did. **Do not cite this line — rebuild and read it.**

**Three figures that only Task 1.7.7's re-measurement caught, and they are the kind that a citation would have carried forward.** **Per-request latency has a baseline now and it is "no cost"** — the shipping server against a Fastify instance running the same route on Fastify's own defaults, 20 000 `app.inject()` calls each after a 2 000 warm-up, is **13.8 µs against 14.1 µs**, i.e. the shipping server is 0.25–1.44 µs _faster_ across four runs. That is inside run-to-run noise, in the direction of the narrowed serialiser saving more fields than the UUID costs. Task 1.7.2 measured `randomUUID` in isolation (34 ns against the counter's 11 ns) and never took this, so do not present its incidental `responseTime` numbers as a before-and-after. **The other four routes now report 26 axe passes and not 25** — no violations either way, so passes rising is not a regression, but the figure in Task 1.5.4 and Task 1.7.6 is stale. **And `pnpm verify` is 8.77 s** against Task 1.6.7's 9.3–9.8 s and Task 1.7.6's 10.1 s on the same six steps with more code in the tree; the total is variance and the per-step split is the thing to compare.

**axe's scope changes the answer in the workshop, and the difference is not a finding.** Run against a whole story iframe, `Region`'s `AllPermutations` reports **3 violations** — `landmark-one-main`, `page-has-heading-one` and `region`. Scoped to `#storybook-root`, which is what `@storybook/addon-a11y` does, it reports **0**. All three are page-level rules a story fragment structurally cannot satisfy. Do not "fix" them, and never compare a whole-document axe run against an addon figure — they are measuring different things.

**Counting the files that import `@base-ui/react` needs an import-aware grep.** The figure worth watching is **one**, and `grep -rl "@base-ui/react" apps/frontend/src` answers **two**: `AppHeader.tsx` names the package in a comment explaining why it deliberately does _not_ import it. Count `import` lines.

**Task 1.7.6's boundaries were the figure for four stories: 267 → 271 modules, 342,017 → 343,658 B of JavaScript, 9,825 → 10,926 B of CSS, still three files** — ~~the current figure~~, until Task 1.11.4 made the artefact four files and Task 1.11.5 moved the bundle; see the `apps/frontend/dist` paragraph above for what it is now. That is +4 modules and +1.64 kB for `ErrorBoundary.tsx`, `ErrorFallback.tsx` and its stylesheet, and `report-error.ts`, with no dependency. `react-error-boundary` 6.1.4 was installed and built before it was rejected — **+932 B and +1 module** over hand-rolling — and the tree rebuilt to the same hash afterwards.

**`base` and `basename` are one input with two readers, and until Task 1.6.5 they were two inputs that failed silently.** `<BrowserRouter basename={import.meta.env.BASE_URL}>` in `App.tsx`, and that is the whole change. Vite's `base` fixes the asset URLs; React Router matches against the full pathname regardless, so setting `base` alone gives an application that loads its JavaScript perfectly and then **renders the not-found route at its own address**. Measured at `base: "/marketpulse/"` on a plain static host, not reasoned about: `<h1>No such page</h1>` at `/marketpulse/`, zero of the landing route's four region landmarks, and a header that looks entirely healthy. **The half that is worse and is easy to miss: every link in the chrome pointed off the deployment** — `/investigations` rather than `/marketpulse/investigations` — including the not-found route's own recovery link, so there was no screen a user could recover from. `BASE_URL` is exempt from `envPrefix` because it is one of Vite's own built-ins rather than a `.env` variable, and it was checked in the artefact rather than assumed — `` basename: `/marketpulse/` `` is a real literal and `basename:void 0` appears zero times, which matters because a `void 0` there is a `basename` of `undefined` and looks exactly like the bug. React Router strips Vite's trailing slash itself, so the two conventions need no reconciling. Cost at the default `base`: **+0.01 kB**, 265 modules and three files unchanged, and `` basename: `/` `` is what ships. The workshop is unaffected — `preview.tsx` uses `MemoryRouter`, which reads no `BASE_URL`.

**A subpath deployment still needs the host's fallback, and Task 1.6.5 built the smallest correct one so Story 1.11 has an example rather than only a constraint.** Scoped to the subpath, declining to rewrite anything whose last segment contains a dot: 200 on `/marketpulse/` and all four routes and `/marketpulse/nonsense`, **404** on `/marketpulse/assets/nope.js` and on `/other/`. Deep-loaded cold, `/marketpulse/replay` renders Market Replay with the stylesheet applied. `basename` does not touch deep-linking and was never going to — on a plain `python3 -m http.server` every deep link is still a 404 before React exists.

**Route splitting is a decision that was taken and can be re-taken, not an omission.** Built with a `React.lazy` per route module and one `<Suspense>` boundary, `dist/` goes from **3 files to 12**, total JavaScript from 342.08 kB to **343.52 kB** and gzipped total from 114.36 kB to **115.62 kB** — splitting costs bytes rather than saving them. The eager chunk does shrink by 105.37 kB, and **not one of those bytes is saved on the route the product opens on**: they move into a 108.85 kB `MarketOverview` chunk, which is Base UI arriving on a second round trip instead of the first. First paint of `/` is 351.90 kB in 2 files and one round trip unsplit, against 351.39 kB in 7 files and two round trips split. React and React Router stay eager either way, because the chrome renders on every route including the not-found state. The four placeholder chunks are 0.34–0.64 kB each — splitting them is close to free and close to worthless. **The reversal trigger is Epic 4**, which replaces Story 1.4's render check and so removes the only reason Base UI is on the first-paint route; if one route later becomes genuinely large, split _that_ route rather than all five. Related and worth knowing before anyone tries: a `fallback={null}` blanks the whole of `<main>` — four named region landmarks and the 70vh grid — under a header that still looks healthy, because `AppHeader` is outside `<Routes>` and eager. Observed against a host delaying each chunk, not reasoned about.

**The one thing React Router does not give us is a checked path.** `to=` is a string, and a typo is silent until someone clicks it. That is the **third** silent-failure class in this frontend, alongside the misspelled CSS Module class and the missing `.js` import extension — and it is the one alternative that would have closed it: TanStack Router's typed routes make a bad path a `TS2322` naming the valid set, verified in the spike. It lost on twice the bundle for four routes, on a generated `routeTree.gen.ts` needing a `.gitignore` entry and a staleness answer, and on half its value being a data layer Epic 2 owns. If mistyped routes become a real recurring defect, that is the reversal trigger and 37 kB is the price. **Task 1.5.2 narrowed it rather than closing it:** every path is declared once in `apps/frontend/src/routes/paths.ts` as an `as const` object, read by both the `<Route path>` declarations and every `to=`, so a typo is an unknown property that `tsc -b` catches. That is a mitigation and not the guarantee — nothing stops a future author writing the literal out by hand, and nothing checks a declared path has a route. Add a path there, never in a component.

**Focus is the token layer's, not each component's.** `base.css` carries one global `:focus-visible` rule and no component declares another. Measured on a real control for the first time in Task 1.4.5: `Tab` reaches the popover trigger, which is a genuine `<button>`, `:focus-visible` matches, and the computed outline is `rgb(28, 28, 28) solid 2px` at `outline-offset: 2px`. Re-measured across the chrome in Task 1.5.3 — all four navigation links, same outline, same offset, none of them declaring one. A component that adds its own focus style is answering a question the tokens already answered.

**The React Compiler rule set fired for the first time in Task 1.5.1, and it was state rather than the router.** It had never fired through five components — nothing in the tree was stateful and there were no hooks anywhere, and Task 1.4.5's prediction that components would be the first test was wrong because CSS Modules compute nothing during render. The router spike's `:symbol` route synced state from a URL parameter the obvious way and `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` failed `lint` at **error**. Neither router candidate provoked a rule on its own: `useParams`, `Link`, `Outlet` and `RouterProvider` are all silent under `strictTypeChecked` and all 17 rules, and the same route written without the effect lints clean. So the rule set is not hostile to routing — it has an opinion about a very common shape, `--max-warnings 0` makes that opinion a failing build, and a route module that derives from the URL rather than mirroring it into state will not meet it. Task 1.5.2 is the demonstration: five route modules and the shell, zero hooks and zero state between them, and the rules said nothing. Task 1.5.3 is the second: the chrome's clock is a reserved region and its feed status is a prop, so a header that could easily have held a `useEffect` ticking a clock holds nothing, and the rules said nothing again. Task 1.5.4 is the third and the closest anything has come: `Region` calls `useId()` — the **first hook in this application** — and the rules said nothing, because `useId` is not state. ~~**They have still never fired outside a spike**, and the reason is that nothing shipped here has state yet — not that the tree is compatible with them.~~ **Task 1.12.3 shipped the first stateful code in this repository and they still said nothing**, which is a finding rather than a pass: `useState` plus a `useEffect` whose `setState` calls happen in async continuations and event handlers is simply not the shape `set-state-in-effect` objects to — that rule is about a **synchronous** update in the effect body, which is the mirror of what Task 1.5.1's spike wrote. So the conclusion is unchanged and its reason is sharper: **the rules are still untested against anything they dislike.** What did fire on that task was type-aware lint, and it is worth carrying — `if (stopped) return;` after an `await` is `no-unnecessary-condition` at error, _"value is always falsy"_, because TypeScript narrows a `let` from an enclosing scope and does not widen it again across an `await`, and the only assignment to `true` is in a teardown closure the analysis cannot see running. It is not always falsy; it is exactly the case teardown produces. Reading the flag through a one-line function closes it, because **a call expression is never narrowed** — no assertion and no disabled rule. The general form: **a lint error saying a guard is dead is not always evidence the guard is dead.** The current route is read by `NavLink` and rendered as `aria-current="page"`, so the accessible state and the visible one are one fact rather than two.

**Two toolchain behaviours that only appear when writing stories.** `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` makes "prop absent" and "prop present as `undefined`" different types, so a permutation grid mapping over a table of optional values does not compile and has to be written out — the compiler drawing the same distinction the component's API does. And a decorator written inline in a `Meta` object makes the inferred type unnameable: `TS2883`, "cannot be named without a reference to `PartialStoryFn` … this is likely not portable". Naming the decorator (`const withTable: Decorator = …`) fixes it.

**`apps/frontend`'s `target` has a second reader.** `build.target` in `vite.config.ts` must equal it: tsc's `target` decides what the language allows, Vite's decides what ships after downlevelling. Vite 8's default is `baseline-widely-available`, which is _lower_ than es2024 — so leaving it unset is a silent disagreement, not a neutral default. The baseline itself is evergreen desktop per PRODUCT_SPEC.md §3; see ADR 0003 §4.

Relative imports inside a package carry explicit `.js` extensions from `.ts` files (`./ticker.js`, and `./App.js` for an `App.tsx`). It looks wrong; `nodenext` resolution requires the _emitted_ filename and errors (TS2835) without it. Every package also needs `"type": "module"`.

**In `apps/frontend` the convention has exactly one enforcer, and it is `tsc`.** Both producers accept the extension, for different reasons — tsc maps the emitted name back under `nodenext`, Rolldown tries TypeScript source extensions for a `.js` specifier — but they do not agree on the _negative_ case. Drop the extension and `tsc -b` fails with TS2835 and exit 1 while `vite build` resolves `./App` to `App.tsx` and emits a **byte-identical** bundle (measured in Task 1.3.5). So a violation is caught by `build` and by nothing the dev server or the bundler does. Re-check this on a bundler upgrade rather than trusting it.

Every shared compiler option lives in `tsconfig.base.json` and nowhere else. Packages extend it and add only `include`, `outDir`/`rootDir`, project `references`, and the frontend's `target`/`lib`. Each option in that file carries a comment explaining why it is there — if you change one, change the comment. `lib` is intentionally unset so it follows `target`.

Prettier follows the root-only tooling rule above, and there is one `prettier.config.mjs`. `.mjs` rather than `.prettierrc.json` so each option carries the reason it is set. Every option in it is explicit even where it restates a Prettier default — a Prettier upgrade must not quietly restyle the tree.

**Formatting is Prettier's, correctness is ESLint's, and the two do not overlap.** `eslint-config-prettier` is deliberately **not** installed: of the 138 rules the lint config enables on a `.ts` file, zero are formatting rules, and the only `eslint-config-prettier` "special rule" enabled is `no-unexpected-multiline`, which guards hand-written code rather than fighting Prettier's output. Measured with `eslint --print-config`, not assumed — re-run it rather than trusting this paragraph, and if a real conflict ever appears, `eslint-config-prettier` goes **last** in the flat config array.

`tsconfig.base.json` has no Prettier `overrides` entry and does not need one. Prettier infers the plain `json` parser for it (the `.base.` infix misses Prettier's JSONC filename list), and that parser preserves comments anyway — verified. Don't add one.

LF is stated in three places and all three must agree: `endOfLine: "lf"` in the Prettier config, `end_of_line = lf` in `.editorconfig`, and `* text=auto eol=lf` in `.gitattributes`. `.editorconfig` binds editors only — git still normalises on checkout by its own rules, so `.gitattributes` is what actually prevents CRLF diffs.

Prettier owns the Markdown in `planning/` too, not just code — the docs were normalised in Task 1.1.6 (`*` bullets to `-`, `*emphasis*` to `_emphasis_`, padded tables). Write prose however you like and let `pnpm format` settle it; `format:check` covers those files.

WebStorm needs no per-machine setup: `.idea/prettier.xml` is checked in with `AUTOMATIC` mode plus format-on-save and format-on-reformat, so WebStorm resolves the same `prettier` package and the same config file the CLI does.

All of the above was verified from a clean clone in Task 1.1.8 — fresh `git clone`, empty pnpm store, empty Corepack home — and again in Task 1.3.5 with the frontend in place: 200 packages installed cold in 1.3s, `pnpm verify` exits 0 in 7.6s, and the emitted bundle is **byte-identical** to the working tree's (md5 `e3fa3b5e…`). **Task 1.4.6 re-ran `clean`/`install`/`verify` with the workshop in the chain and it is 10.5s** — warm, the steps are build 2.2s, lint 3.3s, `format:check` 1.4s, `stories` 0.24s, `test` 0.45s, and cold the build splits `tsc -b` 1.54s / `vite build` 0.49s / `storybook build` 1.38s. **Task 1.5.6 re-ran it with the router, the chrome and the regions in the tree and it is 11.0s** — build 3.2s, lint 2.9s, `format:check` 1.6s, `stories` 0.25s, `test` 0.45s. **Task 1.6.7 re-ran it with the configuration module in the tree and a sixth step in the chain and it is 9.3–9.8s** — build 3.60s, lint 2.98s, `format:check` 1.87s, `stories` 0.26s, **`env:check` 0.26s**, `test` 0.47s, with the cold build splitting `tsc -b` 1.53s / `vite build` 0.45s / `storybook build` 1.33s. The chain got faster while gaining a step, which is run-to-run variance rather than an improvement; the figure that means something is the new step's 0.26s. **Task 1.7.7 re-ran it with the whole of Story 1.7 in the tree and it is 8.77s warm** — build 3.69s, lint 3.17s, `format:check` 2.20s, `stories` 0.27s, `env:check` 0.27s, `test` 0.48s, with the cold build splitting `tsc -b` 1.57s / `vite build` 0.46s / `storybook build` 1.37s. Three consecutive stories have now measured this total going up and down while the tree only grew, so **stop reading the total as a trend and read the per-step split**. From a genuinely cold clone with an empty store it is a different number again: install 3.2s and `verify` **13.2s**, and the artefact it produces is byte-identical (md5 `cba2825c…`) to the working tree's. Storybook is the slower bundler and the workshop's own output is **299 modules and 9.3 MB on disk across 59 files** since Task 1.7.6's three stories files (it was 289 / 52 / 9.2 MB through Story 1.6, and 291 / 52 / 9.2 MB after Task 1.7.3 put two tree-shaken modules in the graph — see the artefact table above) — Task 1.5.6 recorded 227 modules, 50 files and 7.4 MB, and all three were wrong: a worktree built at that commit gives the same 289 / 52 / 9.2 MB, and 7.4 was the **apparent** size (9.2 MB on disk is 7.3 MB apparent). Story 1.6 changed none of it. Recorded rather than silently overwritten, because copied figures are how it happened. The reasoning behind each decision is collected in `docs/adr/0001-repository-structure-and-typescript-toolchain.md` and `docs/adr/0003-frontend-build-tooling-and-browser-baseline.md`; this file is the operational summary and those are the record of _why_. Two things a fresh checkout deliberately does **not** prove: the stale-`dist` trap above (a clean clone has no stale `dist` — the evidence is in Tasks 1.1.4 and 1.1.7), and the nested-worktree problem (a clean clone has no worktrees). **Task 1.8.6 is the seventh clean-clone run and the first that started the pair**, so the third thing a fresh checkout used not to prove — that a clean clone reaches a **running application** by following `README.md` alone — is now proved rather than outstanding. Its figures, all re-taken rather than cited: **327 packages downloaded cold in 3.1s** (Task 1.3.5's 200 is stale by 127 across five stories — quote 327), `pnpm verify` exit 0 at 14.64s cold-after-install / 9.66s warm / 12.78s cold again after a `pnpm clean`, warm steps build 2.12s, lint 3.38s, `format:check` 2.58s, `stories` 0.25s, `env:check` 0.26s, `test` 0.49s, cold build splitting `tsc -b` 1.83s / `vite build` 0.51s / `storybook build` 1.50s, and the same 271 modules / 343,658 B / 10,926 B / three files / md5 `cba2825c…` as the working tree. `pnpm dev` is **13 lines and 1,159 ms** to the listening line; `pnpm ready` is 0.33s warm and 0.86s run in the same breath as `pnpm dev`. One mechanical note for anyone re-running it: **pnpm 11 ignores `npm_config_store_dir` and `NPM_CONFIG_STORE_DIR`**, so an install meant to be cold is not unless `--store-dir` is passed — measured, after an install that reported `reused 327`. **Task 1.8.7 is the eighth run and it is a comparison rather than a first reading**, taken on the shipping tree one task later: **327 packages cold in 3.52s**, `pnpm verify` **13.56s** exit 0 from the clone, and the same 271 modules / 343,658 B / 10,926 B / three files / md5 `cba2825c…` — byte-identical again, and unchanged since Task 1.7.7, because Story 1.8 added nothing to the frontend. Warm on the working tree it is **9.25s** — build 3.04s, lint 3.56s, `format:check` 2.77s, `stories` 0.27s, `env:check` 0.27s, `test` 0.50s — with the cold build splitting `tsc -b` 1.78s / `vite build` 0.53s / `storybook build` 1.55s. Four consecutive stories have now measured the total going up and down while the tree only grew (9.3–9.8s, 10.1s, 8.77s, 9.25s), which is the point of the paragraph above. **Task 1.9.7 is the ninth run, and it is the first where the install figure genuinely moved: 398 packages cold in 3.13s** against Task 1.8.7's 327 — Story 1.9's five dependencies (`vitest`, `@vitest/coverage-v8`, `jsdom`, `@testing-library/react`, `@testing-library/dom`) plus their transitives are the whole difference, and `allowBuilds` did not fire. `pnpm verify` from the clone is **16.41s** exit 0 cold-after-install; warm inside the clone the steps are build 2.09s, lint 3.36s, `format:check` 2.82s, `stories` 0.24s, `env:check` 0.24s, `test` **2.26s**, with the cold build splitting `tsc -b` 1.48s / `vite build` 0.41s / `storybook build` 1.31s. All 103 tests pass from the clone, after the build the chain orders — which matters here in a way it never did before, because `pnpm test` on its own does **not** pass without one. The artefact is **byte-identical** again: 271 modules / 343,658 B / 10,926 B / three files / md5 `cba2825c…`, unchanged since Task 1.7.7 and now for the fifth story running, because Story 1.9 added nothing reachable from `index.html`. Warm on the working tree the chain is **11.78s** — build 2.44s, lint 3.66s, `format:check` 2.79s, `stories` 0.25s, `env:check` 0.25s, `test` **2.36s** — so five consecutive stories have now measured the total moving in both directions (9.3–9.8s, 10.1s, 8.77s, 9.25s, 11.78s) and exactly one step has genuinely moved. **Task 1.13.6 is the tenth run and the first that could point a command at something outside the clone.** Its figures: **402 packages cold in 31.2 s** — the download is the whole of that, and it is the first reading here taken over a real network rather than a warm laptop store — **404 store entries, 280,732 KB, 4,641 lockfile lines**, `pnpm verify` **exit 0 in 29.28 s** from the clone against **24.33 s** warm on the working tree (build 2.63 / lint 4.48 / `format:check` 4.41 / `stories` 0.24 / `env:check` 0.25 / `test` 3.21 / `test:process` 7.78). 189 tests plus 10 process tests, **10 components and 10 stories files**, and the artefact reproducing Task 1.13.4's figures to the byte: 348,135 B `b98aeaa5…`, 12,128 B `134d5dd8…`, `index.html` 1,101 B `07983678…`, 300 B, **361,664 B over four files**, with Storybook at **63 files / 9.3 MB**. Two things this run proved that no previous clean clone could. **The install-script sweep was run against the clone's own store and returns `esbuild@0.28.2` and nothing else**, so Playwright genuinely ships none and `allowBuilds` still has one entry — read off the installed tree rather than inferred from a green install. And **`pnpm e2e:deployed` was run from the clone**, which is the only place its built-output guard actually fires: a fresh checkout has no `dist/` and no `.tsbuildinfo`, the guard stopped with the message naming `pnpm --filter @marketpulse/e2e typecheck`, that command built exactly what the specs import in **0.87 s**, and the check then drove the live environment to **10 passed in 10.8 s**. Note what a clean clone still does not download: **the browser**. `pnpm install` fetches none, and `pnpm exec playwright install chromium` is a separate explicit command — 554.3 MB over three artefacts on this machine, once per machine.

## What MarketPulse is

An AI-assisted situational-awareness tool for US equities. It detects statistically unusual market behaviour and lets a human or an AI agent investigate it against primary-source evidence. `planning/PRODUCT_SPEC.md` is the authoritative product definition — read the relevant section before implementing a feature. Each `planning/epic-NN-*/EPIC.md` states that epic's goal, scope, exit criteria and the spec sections behind it; read the current epic's file before starting work on it.

It is explicitly **not** a trading system. It never predicts prices, recommends trades, or produces target prices.

## Non-negotiable architectural invariants

These are the load-bearing decisions. They are cheap to honour up front and very expensive to retrofit — treat a change to any of them as a design discussion, not an implementation detail.

**1. The LLM never calculates.** Every number a user sees comes from deterministic code (an analytical tool or service). The model chooses _what_ to investigate and explains results; it must not produce figures from its own reasoning. If a feature needs a number the tool layer can't produce, add the tool — don't let the model estimate it.

**2. The AI manipulates typed application state, never markup or code.** Agent-driven UI changes are schema-validated `WorkspaceCommand` objects (`focusSymbols`, `openPriceChart`, `compareSymbols`, `setTimeWindow`, `pinEvidence`, …) executed against trusted, pre-existing components. No LLM-generated HTML, JSX, or executable frontend code, ever. Validate → permission-check → execute → record in investigation history.

**3. The product works with the AI switched off.** Every analytical capability must be reachable through direct user interaction. AI accelerates investigation; it is never the only path to a feature.

**4. Temporal isolation is enforced in the data/tool layer, not the prompt.** In replay mode, no component may read data timestamped after the replay clock. This constraint belongs in the data-access and tool implementations so future-information leakage is structurally impossible — never rely on instructing the model to behave. Design data access with this in mind from the first query, even before replay exists.

**5. Confidence and provenance are part of the domain model, not prose.** Findings carry an explicit confidence level — `CONFIRMED` / `SUPPORTED` / `POSSIBLE` / `UNKNOWN`. Evidence records source, event timestamp, retrieval timestamp, calculation method, and a raw-data reference. "Not enough evidence to explain this move" is a correct, first-class outcome, not a failure.

**6. Market-data provenance is displayed, never implied.** Alpaca's free tier is IEX, not consolidated SIP. The UI must label the feed (e.g. `Market feed: IEX`) and must not suggest full US-market coverage.

**7. Provider abstractions at the edges.** Market data sits behind a provider interface; the LLM sits behind an `AgentProvider` interface (`runInvestigation` / `streamEvents` / `cancel`). No vendor SDK types leak into the domain model.

## Domain model

The core objects, in dependency order — `Investigation` → `Step` → `Finding` → `Evidence`. An **Investigation is a persisted, first-class, long-running object**, not a chat session: it outlives any single AI response and has explicit status (`running` / `awaiting user` / `completed` / `failed` / `cancelled`). Steps have observable status so the UI shows real progress rather than a generic spinner.

The frontend renders investigation state from an ordered stream of typed backend events (`STEP_STARTED`, `TOOL_CALL_COMPLETED`, `FINDING_CREATED`, `WORKSPACE_COMMAND`, `INVESTIGATION_COMPLETED`, …) — never by parsing unstructured model text. This is what makes streaming, cancellation, retries, replay and testing tractable.

Anomaly detection is deterministic and deliberately interpretable: price percentile, volume ratio, relative move vs. sector and market, and breadth, normalised into a 0–100 score. **Every score must carry its explanation.** The score measures "how unusual is this?" — never risk or opportunity.

## Intended stack

React + TypeScript, Redux for domain state, RxJS for streaming pipelines and cancellation. Node + TypeScript backend (Fastify or NestJS). PostgreSQL, optionally TimescaleDB. Sigma.js/WebGL for the market topology, with the graph model kept separate from the renderer.

WebSocket for continuous market data; SSE/streaming HTTP for agent investigation events. These two streams have different semantics and stay separate.

Resist adding libraries before complexity demonstrates the need, and don't introduce a second database in V1 without a measurement justifying it.

## Frontend structure

Feature modules under `app/`: `market`, `topology`, `charts`, `anomalies`, `investigations`, `replay`, `filings`, `shared`. Modules expose domain-level APIs; they do not reach into each other's stores. Create a module when the iteration needs it, not before.

## Delivery order

Fifteen epics, delivered in sequence — see `planning/EPICS.md`. Condensed: foundation → historical data → live data → overview → anomaly detection → topology → **deterministic investigations** → evidence workspace → SEC evidence → AI investigations → generative workspace → persistence → replay → performance → portfolio release.

Checkpoints: by end of Epic 8 MarketPulse is a credible non-AI product; Epic 10 makes it agentic; Epic 11 delivers the AI/frontend interaction the portfolio is built around; Epic 13 delivers its signature capability.

**Do not start with the AI.** The investigation engine, its analytical tools, and its event stream must work end-to-end without an LLM first. Only once the Investigation model feels correct should a model be allowed to drive it. This ordering exists specifically to prevent the architecture collapsing into `chat box → LLM → miscellaneous API calls`.

First milestone: display ~100 securities, receive live price updates, calculate an explainable anomaly score, click through to underlying price/volume evidence.

## Failure handling

Agentic failures are normal product states, not exceptions. Degrade incrementally and locally — a failed SEC lookup, analytical tool, or dropped market socket must leave the rest of the workspace and any already-gathered evidence intact and clearly labelled (e.g. "Live feed disconnected — displaying data through 10:42:17"). Never collapse to a global error screen.

## Performance targets

Measured, and published in the repo. Event → application state <250 ms p95 (excluding provider latency); 60 FPS at 500 nodes / 5k edges; >45 FPS in synthetic mode at 5k nodes / 25k edges; no routine main-thread task >50 ms; visible investigation feedback <500 ms after user action, streaming incrementally.

## Out of scope for V1

Brokerage integration, trade execution, portfolios, options, crypto, price predictions, buy/sell recommendations, social sentiment, news aggregation, real authentication, mobile UX, tick-level replay. Don't build toward these.
